Honest Inference for Stochastic Optimization
Kenta Takatsu, Arun Kumar Kuchibhotla

Abstract
This supplement contains the proofs of all the main results in the paper and some supporting lemmas.



Keywords—
Honest inference, Adaptive inference, Irregular M-estimation, Non-standard asymptotics, Extremum estimators.

Contents

1
Introduction

The present study examines inference for parameters defined as solutions to stochastic minimization (or maximization) problems, which arise in broad statistical applications. Let
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N})
be random variables defined on a common probability space, and let
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
denote the law of the corresponding random variables. Define

P^{N}:=\mathcal{L}(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N})\quad\textrm{and}\quad P_{i}:=\mathcal{L}(Z_{i})\quad\textrm{for}\quad 1\leq i\leq N.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{N}
be a collection of joint distributions
P^{N}
. Given a metric space
(\Theta,\|\cdot\|)
and a criterion function
\M:\Theta\times\mathcal{P}^{N}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, the parameter of interest is

\theta(P^{N}):=\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}\,\M(\theta,P^{N}).

(1)

We assume that the population minimizer is common across all marginals in the sense that for any
P^{N}\in\mathcal{P}^{N}
, we have
\theta(P^{N})=\theta(P_{i})
for all
1\leq i\leq N
. This formulation allows observations
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N}
to be neither independent nor identically distributed. The main statistical challenge is that the criterion
\M(\theta,P^{N})
must be estimated from data.

The primary objective of this manuscript is the construction of an
honest
confidence set
(
Li, 1989
;
Pötscher, 2002
)
for the
P^{N}
-dependent minimizer such that

\liminf_{N\to\infty}\,\inf_{P^{N}\in\mathcal{P}^{N}}\,\inf_{\theta^{*}\in\theta(P^{N})}\,\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}(\theta^{*}\in\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha})\geq 1-\alpha

(2)

where
\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}(\cdot)
denotes probability under
P^{N}
. It is known that a finite-sample guarantee for fixed
N
is generally impossible without imposing strong restrictions on the class of distributions
\mathcal{P}^{N}

(
Bahadur and Savage, 1956
)
.
Although the asymptotic validity guarantee in (
2
) may appear less informative in the settings with high-dimensional parameters, the methods studied in this manuscript satisfies (
2
) and retains validity regardless of the dimension/complexity of the space
\Theta
, including the cases where the dimension is comparable to the sample size. This property has recently been termed
dimension-agnostic

(
Kim and Ramdas, 2024
)
, though closely related ideas also appear in
Robins and van der Vaart (2006)
.

Finally, the guarantee (
2
) does not require uniqueness of the minimizer. To simplify notation, the following convention will be used:

\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}(\theta(P^{N})\in\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha})~:=~\inf_{\theta^{*}\in\theta(P^{N})}\,\mathbb{P}_{P}(\theta^{*}\in\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}).

(3)

Motivation: Irregular and High-dimensional Inference

Common approaches for constructing confidence sets include (i) Wald methods based on the limiting distribution of an estimator, and (ii) resampling methods. Both require an estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{N}
such that, for a diverging sequence
r_{N}
, the scaled quantity
r_{N}(\widehat{\theta}_{N}-\theta(P^{N}))
converges in distribution. Wald methods posit a parametric form for this limit and construct confidence sets from its quantiles, whereas resampling methods estimate the limiting distribution nonparametrically. In many settings, the honest validity guarantee (
2
) can fail if the weak convergence of
r_{N}(\widehat{\theta}_{N}-\theta(P^{N}))
is not continuous in
P^{N}

(
Andrews, 2000
;
Andrews and Guggenberger, 2010
;
Cattaneo et al., 2020
;
Cattaneo et al., 2024
)
. A classical example of such “continuity” is the regularity of the estimator
(
van der Vaart, 2000
, Sec. 8.5)
. We refer to the settings where such condition fails (or equivalently, the estimator is ill-behaved) as irregular problems.

Inference for stochastic optimization under non-standard or irregular conditions has been extensively studied in statistics, econometrics, operations research, and other related fields
(
Geyer, 1994
;
Ketz, 2018
;
Horowitz and Lee, 2019
;
Hsieh et al., 2022
;
Li, 2024a
)
. Existing inference methods are typically tailored to specific regularity conditions that are required for validity. For instance, the general approach by
Vogel (2008b)
is valid under regularity conditions on the objective function and parameter space. The framework by
Li (2024b)
requires knowledge of the estimator’s convergence rate. Procedures based on sample-splitting by
Dey et al. (2025)
and
Park et al. (2025)
are proved under stronger distributional assumptions to ensure valid coverage.

This manuscript proposes a simple, general-purpose approach to inference for stochastic optimization problems, building on robust procedures based on sample-splitting
(
Robins and van der Vaart, 2006
;
Chakravarti et al., 2019
;
Wasserman et al., 2020
;
Park et al., 2025
;
Kim and Ramdas, 2024
;
Dey et al., 2025
)
. The resulting confidence set is valid under weak distributional assumptions, applicable to irregular or high-dimensional settings, and accommodates constraints or regularization. The corresponding inference tasks have been difficult without knowledge of the estimator’s convergence rate, the existence of a limiting distribution, or specific structure in the parameter space. In this regard, we provide a significant improvement, as the convergence rate in irregular problems can be impossible to estimate uniformly, and the corresponding limiting distributions can be highly complex
(
Wang, 1996
)
.

The following statistical problems are a few examples in which inference remains difficult to date and the proposed confidence set provides a simple solution.

1.

High-dimensional Linear Regression
: Inference for ordinary least squares (OLS) remains challenging when the dimension
d
increases with the sample size
N
, due to bias of
d/N^{1/2}

(
Mammen, 1993
;
Cattaneo et al., 2018
)
. Existing bias-corrected methods regain validity in some regimes
d\gg N^{1/2}

(
Cattaneo et al., 2019
;
Chang et al., 2023
)
, but typically require growth restrictions on
d
. The proposed method is valid regardless of the dimension (Sections
8.1
and
8.2
).

2.

Cube-root Estimators
:
Kim and Pollard (1990)
identify
a class of problems exhibiting
cube-root asymptotics
, where the minimizer converges at
N^{-1/3}
. Examples include Manski’s maximum score estimator
(
Manski, 1975
;
Manski, 1985
;
Horowitz, 1992
;
Delgado et al., 2001
)
, the Grenander estimator
(
Grenander, 1956
;
Sen et al., 2010
;
Westling and Carone, 2020
;
Cattaneo et al., 2024
)
, and classification in machine learning
(
Mohammadi and van de Geer, 2005
)
. Empirical bootstrap is known to be inconsistent
(
Sen et al., 2010
;
Patra et al., 2018
)
, and modified resampling procedures have been proposed
(
Cattaneo et al., 2020
;
Cattaneo et al., 2024
)
. We provide new inferential results for a prototypical example (Section
8.3
).

3.

Non-smooth Objective
: Many criterion functions can be written as
\M(\theta,P^{N})\equiv\E_{P^{N}}[m_{\theta}(Z)]
, where
m_{\theta}(Z)
is a “loss” function. When
\theta\mapsto m_{\theta}
is non-smooth, the limiting distribution of an estimator can be non-standard
(
Smirnov, 1952
;
Knight, 1998
)
. A canonical example is quantile estimation whose limiting distribution depends on the smoothness of the cumulative distribution function (CDF). While distribution-free finite sample valid confidence intervals exist
(
Lanke, 1974
)
, we study the behavior of the proposed confidence set in this setting (Section
8.4
).

4.

Constrained Optimization
: The parameter space
\Theta
can incorporate structural constraints, such as sparsity or shape
(
Wang, 1996
;
Candes and Tao, 2007
;
Li et al., 2015
;
Royset and Wets, 2020
)
. Confidence sets under such constraints have been studied
(
Geyer, 1994
;
Vogel, 2008a
;
Vogel, 2008b
;
Vogel and Seeger, 2017
;
Vogel, 2019
)
. The proposed confidence set remains valid under such structural constraints.

Summary of Methods and Contributions

A key conceptual idea is that, instead of relying on the behavior of an estimator, we can exploit the defining property of the functional (
1
). In particular, the following holds from the definition (
1
):

\M(\theta(P^{N}),P^{N})\leq\M(\theta,P^{N})\quad\text{for any non-random}\quad\theta\in\Theta.

(4)

At an intuitive level, the proposed confidence set can be motivated in two steps: (1) Since
\theta(P^{N})
minimizes
\theta\mapsto\mathbb{M}(\theta,P^{N})
, it must belong to the set
\{\theta:\,\mathbb{M}(\theta,P^{N})\leq\mathbb{M}(\theta^{\prime},P^{N})\}
for any
\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
; (2) if
\theta\mapsto\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{N}(\theta)
is an estimator of
\mathbb{M}(\theta,P^{N})
, then it is natural to expect that
\theta(P^{N})
will also belong to
\{\theta:\,\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{N}(\theta)\leq\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{N}(\theta^{\prime})+\gamma_{N}\}
for an appropriate tolerance level
\gamma_{N}
. In particular, the reference point
\theta^{\prime}
can be replaced by a data-dependent estimator, provided that
\widehat{\M}_{N}(\cdot)
and
\theta^{\prime}
are (approximately) independent; one way to achieve this independence is through sample-splitting. This idea is not new and can be found as early as
Stein (1981)
, and more recently in
Robins and van der Vaart (2006)
and
Vogel (2008b)
; see Section
S.1
for further historical discussion.

The main contributions of this manuscript are as follows: (1) we provide a systematic way to obtain a dimension- and complexity-agnostic validity guarantee; (2) we analyze the width (or diameter) of the proposed confidence set under mild conditions; and (3) we use the general result to establish rate adaptivity in several examples. The conditions used in the width analysis are comparable to those commonly employed in studying the convergence rates of M-estimators. These results contribute to the development of honest and adaptive inference procedures for stochastic optimization problems.

This flexibility and generality come with a price. First, although we provide conditions under which the proposed confidence set shrinks to a singleton at the optimal rate adaptively, it can be larger than traditional confidence sets due to the use of sample splitting. In practice, data efficiency can be partially recovered by swapping the roles of the splits and aggregating the resulting sets via a majority-vote procedure
(
Gasparin and Ramdas, 2024
)
, though such efficiency improvements are not the focus of this work. Importantly, the role of sample splitting here is fundamentally different from that in double machine learning (DML)
(
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
;
Foster and Syrgkanis, 2023
)
: at its core, DML relies on influence-function expansions and regularity conditions to establish asymptotic normality, whereas the proposed framework is precisely designed to avoid this type of assumptions. Second, unlike traditional methods that control the shape of the confidence set by considering an appropriate statistic, the proposed confidence set can be non-convex or even disconnected depending on the (estimated) objective function. It might be worth pointing out that, in regular cases, the proposed confidence set will approximately be an ellipsoid in similarity to the likelihood ratio confidence set. Furthermore, we note that the universal inference procedure of
Wasserman et al. (2020)
also shares the same drawbacks.

Organization.

The remainder of this manuscript is organized as follows.
Section

2
provides the most general construction for stochastic optimization and
Section

3
discusses more refined results for M-estimation;
Table

1
at the end of
Section

3
summarizes the validity results from both sections.
Section

4
develops methods for any prescribed significance level with corresponding validity guarantees.
Section

5
establishes non-asymptotic diameter bounds for the proposed confidence sets.
Sections

6
and
7
address practical considerations, covering power improvements and computational aspects respectively.
Section

8
provides an analysis of the confidence set proposed in statistical applications whose inference has been considered challenging.
Section

9
presents numerical results, with additional experiments available in
Section

S.9
.
Section

10
concludes with remarks on open problems and future directions.

Notation.

We adopt the following convention. For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we write
\|x\|_{2}=\sqrt{x^{\top}x}
. In particular, we define the unit sphere with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{2}
such that
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,\|u\|_{2}=1\}
. Given a square matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, its trace, the smallest and the largest eigenvalues are denoted by
\mathrm{tr}(A)
,
\lambda_{\min}(A)
and
\lambda_{\max}(A)
respectively. A standard indicator function is denoted by
\mathbf{1}\{\cdot\}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{1}\{x\in A\}=1
if
x\in A
and
0
if
x\notin A
. For any deterministic sequences
\{x_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
and
\{r_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
, we denote
x_{n}=O(r_{n})
if there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that
|x_{n}|\leq C|r_{n}|
for all
n
larger than some
N
. Similarly, we denote
x_{n}=O_{P}(r_{n})
if, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a constant
C_{\varepsilon}>0
such that
\mathbb{P}(|x_{n}|\leq C_{\varepsilon}|r_{n}|)\leq\varepsilon
for all
n
larger than some
N_{\varepsilon}
.
We denote
x_{n}=o(r_{n})
if
x_{n}/r_{n}\to 0
and
x_{n}=o_{p}(r_{n})
if
x_{n}/r_{n}\overset{p}{\to}0
where
\overset{p}{\to}
denotes convergence in probability.

2
Construction for General Stochastic Optimization

We begin with the construction for general stochastic optimization problems. Let
N\geq 1
denote the total sample size, and partition the index set as:

I_{1}=\{1,\ldots,n_{1}\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad I_{2}=\{n_{1}+r+1,\ldots,N\},

(5)

where
n_{1},r\geq 0
and
n_{2}:=|I_{2}|\geq 1
such that
N=n_{1}+n_{2}+r
. Write
D_{\ell}=\{Z_{i}:i\in I_{\ell}\}
for
\ell=1,2
, with induced marginal laws
P^{\ell}:=\mathcal{L}(D_{\ell})
. From
D_{1}
, we construct an estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{1}:=\widehat{\theta}_{1}(D_{1})\in\Theta
of
\theta(P^{N})
, without imposing any restrictions on its choice. The population objective evaluated at
P^{2}
and its estimator based on
D_{2}
as

\M_{2}(\theta):=\M(\theta,P^{2})\quad\textrm{and}\quad\widehat{\M}_{2}(\theta):=\widehat{\M}_{2}(\theta;D_{2}).

(6)

As discussed in
Section

1
, an ideal (albeit unactionable) confidence set is

\widetilde{\mathrm{CI}}:=\left\{\theta\in\Theta:\,\mathbb{M}_{2}(\theta)-\mathbb{M}_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1})\leq 0\right\}.

(7)

A natural approximation is

\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\dagger}_{N}:=\left\{\theta\in\Theta:\,\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\theta)-\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1})\leq 0\right\}.

(8)

We now state the first validity result for
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\dagger}_{N}
. To this end, we introduce several key objects, beginning with the
\beta
-mixing coefficient following
Bradley (2005)
.

Definition 1

(
\beta
-mixing coefficient)
.

Given the data splits
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
with induced marginals
P^{1},P^{2}
and joint law
P^{1,2}:=\mathcal{L}(D_{1},D_{2})
, the
\beta
-mixing coefficient is

\beta(n_{1},r)=d_{\mathrm{TV}}(P^{1,2},P^{1}\otimes P^{2})

(9)

where
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes total variation distance and
r
corresponds to the gap size in (
5
). We omit the dependence on
n_{1}
and write
\beta(r):=\beta(n_{1},r)
.

The coefficient
\beta(r)
quantifies the dependence between
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
. Under independence,
\beta(r)=0
for all
r\geq 0
. Under
m
-dependence
(
Hoeffding and Robbins, 1948
)
,
\beta(r)=0
for all
r\geq m
. Many practically relevant processes satisfy
\beta(r)\to 0
as
r\to\infty
, including certain Markov chains
(
Blum et al., 1963
)
, linear AR models with absolutely continuous innovations
(
Chanda, 1974
)
(Bernoulli innovations notably fail to be strong mixing
(
Andrews, 1984
)
), near epoch sequences
(
Ibragimov, 1962
)
, and weakly physically dependent processes
(
Wu, 2005
;
Heinrichs, 2026
)
. See
Bradley (2005)
;
Kiessler (2009)
and reference therein for further examples interacting interacting particle systems.

Next, for
\theta\in\Theta
, we define

\begin{split}\mathbb{V}_{2}(\theta)&:=\mathbb{E}_{P^{2}}[|(\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}-\mathbb{M}_{2})(\theta)-(\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}-\mathbb{M}_{2})(\theta(P^{N}))|^{2}]\quad\textrm{and}\\
\mathbb{C}_{2}(\theta)&:=\mathbb{M}_{2}(\theta)-\mathbb{M}_{2}(\theta(P^{N})).\end{split}

(10)

The quantity
\mathbb{V}_{2}(\theta)
is the mean squared error of the estimated optimization objective at two points
\theta,\theta(P^{N})
. The estimator
\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
is permitted to be biased. The quantity
\mathbb{C}_{2}(\theta)
is known as the curvature, which quantifies the “difficulty” of estimating
\theta(P^{N})
. Both are defined for non-random
\theta\in\Theta
; when evaluated at the random point
\widehat{\theta}_{1}\in\Theta
, they become random variables. For brevity, write

\begin{split}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}=\mathbb{V}_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}),\quad\widehat{\mathbb{C}}_{2}=\mathbb{C}_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1})\quad\textrm{and}\quad\widehat{\Delta}_{2}=\widehat{\mathbb{C}}_{2}/\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}^{1/2}.\end{split}

(11)

Remark 1
.

The evaluation of
\M(\theta,P^{2})
at
P^{2}\not\in\mathcal{P}^{N}
is an abuse of notation. This is justified under the assumption that
\theta(P^{N})=\theta(P_{i})
for all
i
, so
\mathbb{C}_{2}(\theta)
continues to reflect the curvature relative to the population target
\theta(P^{N})
.

The first validity result is as follows:

Theorem 1
.

The confidence set
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}
in (
8
) satisfies

\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\!\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}\right)\;\leq\;\mathbb{E}_{P^{1}}\!\left[\min\left\{\frac{1}{\widehat{\Delta}_{2}^{2}},1\right\}\right]+\beta(r).

Moreover, if
\ratio{}^{2}\overset{p}{\to}\infty
and
\beta(r)=o(1)
uniformly over all
P^{N}\in\mathcal{P}^{N}
, then
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}
is asymptotically uniformly valid at confidence level
1
.

Theorem 2
.

Suppose
\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1})-\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\theta(P^{N}))
is an unbiased estimator of
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}_{2}
, in the sense that
\E_{P^{2}}[\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\theta)]=\mathbb{M}_{2}(\theta)
for all
\theta\in\Theta
. Then, the confidence set
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}
in (
8
) satisfies

\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\!\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}\right)\;\leq\;\mathbb{E}_{P^{1}}\left[\frac{1}{1+\widehat{\Delta}^{2}_{2}}\right]+\beta(r).

The proofs of both results appear in
Section

S.2
. Both results are stated under following generality: uniqueness of
\theta(P^{N})
is not assumed, the observations need not be independent, and no specific structure is imposed on the optimization objective.

The miscoverage bound depends on the ratio
\ratio{}^{2}=\widehat{\mathbb{C}}_{2}^{2}/\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}
, which increases with curvature
\mathbb{C}_{2}^{2}
and decreases with estimation error
\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}
. Because
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}
is constructed without reference to a nominal level, it provides an agnostic bound. When
\ratio{}^{2}\to\infty
in probability, the set is valid at confidence level 1. Two illustrative examples follow.

Example 1

(U-statistics)
.

Let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N}
be IID observations from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
with unknown
\sigma^{2}
. Consider inference for
\mu^{2}
, which admits the representation

\mu^{2}=\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}}\,\E_{P^{2}}[(Z_{1}Z_{2}-\theta)^{2}].

Set
D_{1}=\{Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n_{1}}\}
and
D_{2}=\{Z_{n_{1}+1},\ldots,Z_{N}\}
with
|D_{2}|=n_{2}
. The optimization objective can be estimated unbiasedly from
D_{2}
via the U-statistic

\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\theta)=\binom{n_{2}}{2}^{-1}\sum_{n_{1}+1\leq i<j\leq N}(Z_{i}Z_{j}-\theta)^{2}.

Direct calculation yields

\ratio{}^{2}=(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\mu^{2})^{2}\left(\frac{8\sigma^{4}}{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}+\frac{16\mu^{2}\sigma^{2}}{n_{2}}\right)^{-1},

which reflects different behaviors depending on whether
\mu=0
or
\mu\neq 0
. In particular,
\ratio\overset{p}{\to}\infty
whenever

\min\left\{\frac{n_{2}|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\mu^{2}|}{\sigma^{2}},\frac{n_{2}^{1/2}|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\mu^{2}|}{|\mu|\sigma}\right\}\overset{p}{\to}\infty.

This is satisfied, for instance,
(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\mu^{2})^{2}\geq c>0
for some constant
c
, and
n_{2}\to\infty
with
\mu
and
\sigma
fixed.
Section

S.8.1
provides the explicit distribution of for the constant and the U-statistic estimators, from which the upper bound of
Theorem

2
can be evaluated analytically.

Example 2

(Super-efficient initial estimator)
.

Let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N}
be IID observations from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
with unknown
\sigma^{2}
. Consider inference for
\mu
, which corresponds to

\mu=\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}}\,\E_{P^{2}}[(Z_{1}-\theta)^{2}].

Using
D_{2}=\{Z_{n_{1}+1},\ldots,Z_{N}\}
, the objective is estimated unbiasedly by

\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\theta)=\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{n_{1}+1\leq i\leq N}(Z_{i}-\theta)^{2}.

Direct calculation gives
\ratio{}^{2}=n_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\mu)^{2}/(4\sigma^{2})
. As the initial estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
based on
D_{1}
, take Hodges’ estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{1}:=\bar{Z}_{n_{1}}\mathbf{1}\{|\bar{Z}_{n_{1}}|\geq{n_{1}}^{-1/4}\}
where
\bar{Z}_{n_{1}}={n_{1}}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}Z_{i}
.
When
\mu=0
, Hodges’ estimator satisfies
{n_{1}}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\mu)^{2}\overset{p}{\to}0
, exhibiting super-efficiency. In this case,
\ratio{}^{2}\overset{p}{\to}0
. When
\mu={n_{1}}^{-1/4}/2
, one can verify that
{n_{1}}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\mu)^{2}\overset{p}{\to}\infty
, and consequently
\ratio{}^{2}\overset{p}{\to}\infty
. Hence,
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}
is asymptotically valid at confidence level 1. The same conclusion holds when
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
is inconsistent. Then we also have
\ratio{}^{2}\overset{p}{\to}\infty
regardless of
\mu
. Again,
Section

S.8.3
provides the explicit distribution of for the constant estimator, sample mean and Hodges’ estimator, from which the upper bound of
Theorem

2
can be evaluated analytically.

The two examples illustrate when
Theorem

2
yields an informative bound. The confidence set achieves zero miscoverage whenever
\ratio\to\infty
in probability. This happens, for instance, when the initial estimator is inconsistent (the constant estimators in
Example

1
and
Example

2
), or when it converges sufficiently slowly (Hodges’ estimator in a certain neighborhood of
\mu=0
in
Example

2
). The bounds become uninformative when
\ratio\to 0
in probability, as with the Constant or Hodges’ estimator at
\mu=0
. Crucially, however, an uninformative upper bound does not imply that the confidence set itself is uninformative.

Figure

1(a)
and
Figure

1(b)
display the empirical miscoverage of
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}
as well as the analytical upper bound of
Theorem

2
for both examples. The details of the numerical experiments can be found in
Section

S.8.2
and
Section

S.8.5
. A visible gap between the bound and the empirical performance confirms that the bound is conservative. This is because
Theorem

1
and
Theorem

2
are proved for general stochastic optimization. Once additional structure is imposed on the objective, the same confidence set (
8
) remains non-trivial for any choice of
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
. The bound from the forthcoming
Theorem

3
, visible in
Figure

1(b)
, tracks the empirical miscoverage far more closely.

(a)

Estimated miscoverage of
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}
in (
8
) for the U-statistics problem of
Example

1
. The
X
-axis displays the true mean
\mu
and the
Y
-axis displays the empirical miscoverage over
1000
replications. The performances of two estimators, the constant estimator at zero and the U-statistics estimator, are shown in red. The analytical upper bound of
Theorem

2
is shown as a dashed line.

(b)

Estimated miscoverage probability of the confidence set
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}
in (
8
) for the normal mean problem of
Example

2
and
Example

3
. The
X
-axis displays the true mean
\mu
and the
Y
-axis displays the empirical miscoverage over
1000
replications. The performances of three estimators, the constant estimator at zero, Hodges’ estimator and the sample mean, are shown in red. The analytical upper bound of
Theorem

2
is shown as a dashed line; the upper bound of
Theorem

3
is shown as a solid line.

Figure 1
:
The empirical and theoretical miscoverage of the confidence set.

3
Confidence Sets for M-estimation

More refined results become available when the objective takes the form of an expected loss function, a setting commonly referred to as
M-estimation
or
empirical risk minimization
. Let
m_{\theta}:\mathcal{Z}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function, indexed by
\theta\in\Theta
, and define

\M_{2}(\theta):=\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[m_{\theta}(Z_{i})]\quad\textrm{and}\quad\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\theta):=\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}m_{\theta}(Z_{i}).

(12)

For instance, taking
Z=(X^{\top},Y)
and
m_{\theta}(Y,X):=(Y-\theta^{\top}X)^{2}
with
\Theta=\mathbb{R}^{d}
corresponds to linear regression (without assuming linearity), while
m_{\theta}(Z):=-\log p(Z;\theta)
for a (possibly misspecified) parametrized family of likelihood
p(Z;\theta)
yields maximum likelihood estimation. This framework also includes more general nonparametric or constrained problems.

3.1
Validity under Independence

Assume that
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N}
are independent. For
i\in I_{2}
, define the centered differences

\begin{split}\widehat{\xi}_{i}=m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}}(Z_{i})-m_{\theta(P^{N})}(Z_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}}(Z)-m_{\theta(P^{N})}(Z)\mid D_{1}].\end{split}

(13)

Theorem 3
.

The confidence set
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}
in (
8
) satisfies

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\!\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}\right)\;\leq\;\mathbb{E}_{P^{1}}[1-\Phi(\ratio)]\\
&\quad+\E_{P^{1}}\left[\min\left\{1,C\,\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|^{2}}{n_{2}^{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}(1+\ratio)^{2}}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|}{n_{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}^{1/2}(1+\ratio)}\right\}\bigg|D_{1}\right]\right\}\right],\end{split}

(14)

where
C>0
is a universal constant. Whenever the second term vanishes uniformly over
P^{N}\in\mathcal{P}^{N}
, the set
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}
is asymptotically uniformly valid at level
1/2
since
\ratio\geq 0
almost surely.

The proof appears in
Section

S.3
and uses a non-uniform Berry-Esseen bound (e.g., Theorem 2.1 of
Chen and Shao (2001)
). The ratio quantifies the degree of conservativeness. When
\ratio\to 0
in probability, the miscoverage approaches 1/2 exactly; when
\ratio\to\infty
in probability, the set becomes increasingly conservative and the miscoverage tends to zero. The conditions under which the remainder term vanishes also depend on . The least favorable case is when
\ratio\to 0
in probability. In such case, the assumption on
\widehat{\xi}_{i}
reduces to the classical Lindeberg-Feller conditions (conditional on
D_{1}
). When
\ratio\to\infty
in probability, finite
\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}
suffices and the remainder decays faster than in standard Berry-Esseen bounds.

Example 3

(Super-efficient initial estimator, Revisited)
.

Consider the same setting as
Example

2
.
Theorem

3
implies that

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\!\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}\right)\;\leq\;\E_{P^{1}}\left[1-\Phi(\ratio)+\frac{C\sigma^{3/2}}{n_{2}^{1/2}(1+\ratio)^{3}}\right].\end{split}

(15)

Taking
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
to be Hodges’ estimator and combining with the analysis of
Example

2
, the miscoverage of
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}
exhibits three regimes: it approaches
1/2
when
\mu\approx 0
, where super-efficiency causes

\ratio\overset{p}{\to}0
; it tends to zero when
\mu\approx n_{1}^{-1/4}
where
\ratio\overset{p}{\to}\infty
; for for large
\mu
, where Hodges’ estimator becomes comparable with the sample mean, the miscoverage becomes similar to that based on the sample mean as
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
.

Figure

1(b)
compares the analytic upper bound of
Theorem

3
with the empirical miscoverage. The bound exactly tracks the empirical performance with visibly negligible approximation error. This confirms that
1-\Phi(\ratio)
characterizes the miscoverage with high precision. Taken together,
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}
is asymptotically valid at level
1/2
for any
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
and any
P^{N}
under which the remainder term vanishes. The miscoverage approaches
1/2
when the estimator converges “too fast”, and tends to zero when the estimator converges slowly or is inconsistent. For estimators satisfying
\ratio=O_{P}(1)
, the confidence set is valid at level
1/2
but may in practice be conservative. The bound of
Theorem

2
does not capture this conservativeness precisely, whereas
Theorem

3
aligns with the observed miscoverage almost exactly.

Remark 2

(Extensions beyond M-estimation)
.

The result of this section extends to optimization objectives of a more complex form than (
12
). Notable examples include criteria defined as U-statistics or higher-order U-statistics
(
Bose and Chatterjee, 2018
;
DiCiccio and Romano, 2022
)
and U-quantile functionals
(
Choudhury and Serfling, 1988
)
. A special case was already studied in
Example

1
. More refined validity results in these settings follow from the same proof strategy, with the Berry-Esseen bound replaced by the appropriate analogue for the statistic of interest; see, for instance,
Zhao and Chen (1983)
;
Bentkus et al. (1997)
;
Wang (2002)
and
Chen and Shao (2007)
. As evident in
Figure

1(a)
, the miscoverage can exceed
1/2
for U-statistics. This occurs because the normal approximation is valid only under non-degeneracy; the degenerate case requires separate treatment.

3.2
Validity under Dependence

The proof of
Theorem

3
relies crucially on a non-uniform Berry–Esseen bound for independent observations. An extension to dependent data is possible through a martingale approximation argument
(
Wu and Woodroofe, 2004
, Section 2)
, without imposing any specific dependence structure on the observations.

Suppose the dependent observations
\widehat{\xi}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\xi}_{N}
are split according to

I_{1}:=\{1,\ldots,n_{1}\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad I_{2}:=\{n_{1}+1,\ldots,N\}\quad\textrm{with}\quad|I_{2}|=n_{2}.

(16)

Let
\mathcal{H}_{0}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\{\widehat{\xi}_{i}\}_{i\in I_{1}}
, and for each
k\in I_{2}
, let
\mathcal{H}_{k}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathcal{H}_{0}
and
\{\widehat{\xi}_{i}\}_{i=n_{1}+1}^{k}
, so that
\mathcal{H}_{0}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{N}
is a filtration supporting the full sequence. The estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
is
\mathcal{H}_{0}
-measurable by construction.

Theorem 4
.

Recall
\widehat{\xi}_{i}
defined in (
13
). Define the martingale approximation

\widetilde{\xi}_{i}=\sum_{r\in I_{2}}(\E[\widehat{\xi}_{r}|\mathcal{H}_{i}]-\E[\widehat{\xi}_{r}|\mathcal{H}_{i-1}])\quad\textrm{for}\quad i\in I_{2}.

(17)

For
\delta\in(0,\infty)
, define

\begin{split}L_{2\delta}&:=\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\E_{P_{i}}\left[\left|\frac{\widetilde{\xi}_{i}}{n_{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}^{1/2}}\right|^{2+2\delta}\,|\,D_{1}\right]\quad\textrm{and}\\
M_{2\delta}&:=\E_{P^{1}}\left(\left|\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\E_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{\widetilde{\xi}_{i}^{2}}{n_{2}^{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}}\mid\mathcal{H}_{i-1}\right]-1\right|^{1+\delta}\mid D_{1}\right)\quad.\end{split}

(18)

The confidence set
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}
in (
8
) satisfies

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\!\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}\right)\\
&\quad\leq\mathbb{E}_{P^{1}}[1-\Phi(\ratio)]+\mathbb{E}_{P^{1}}\left[\min\left\{1,C_{\delta}\frac{(L_{2\delta}+M_{2\delta})^{1/(3+2\delta)}}{1+|\ratio|^{2+2\delta}}\right\}\right],\end{split}

(19)

where
C_{\delta}>0
is a constant depending only on
\delta
.

The proof of
Theorem

4
appears in
Section

S.3.2
. The original sequence
\{\widehat{\xi}_{i}\}_{i\in I_{2}}
is replaced by the martingale difference sequence
\{\widetilde{\xi}_{i}\}_{i\in I_{2}}
defined in (
17
), to which the non-uniform Berry-Esseen bound of
Haeusler and Joos (1988)
is applied. No specific dependence structure is imposed on the original sequence. The term
L_{2\delta}
controls the
(2+2\delta)
-th moment of the normalized martingale increments;
M_{2\delta}
quantifies the
L^{1+\delta}
deviation of the quadratic variation from the marginal variance.

Compared with
Theorem

3
, which is its counterpart under independence,
Theorem

4
also shows that the miscoverage probability remains governed by
\ratio\geq 0
once the remainder terms are negligible under suitable moment conditions. In particular, the confidence set is asymptotically valid at level
1/2
whenever the remainder terms vanish, extending the conclusions of
Section

3.1
to general dependent observations.

Remark 3

(Martingale approximation)
.

For general dependent observations,

\widetilde{\xi}_{i}=\widehat{\xi}_{i}-\E[\widehat{\xi}_{i}|\mathcal{H}_{i-1}]+\sum_{r=i+1}^{N}(\E[\widehat{\xi}_{r}|\mathcal{H}_{i}]-\E[\widehat{\xi}_{r}|\mathcal{H}_{i-1}])\quad\textrm{for}\quad i\in I_{2}.

(20)

The second term vanishes, for instance, when
\{\widehat{\xi}_{i}\}_{i\in I_{2}}
is itself a martingale difference sequence. This follows since for
r>i
,

\E[\widehat{\xi}_{r}|\mathcal{H}_{i}]=\E[\E[\widehat{\xi}_{r}|\mathcal{H}_{r-1}]\mid\mathcal{H}_{i}]=0\quad\textrm{whenever}\quad\E[\widehat{\xi}_{r}|\mathcal{H}_{r-1}]=0.

Remark 4

(Sharpness and faster rates)
.

The bound (
19
) is sharp in its dependence on
L_{2\delta}
and
M_{2\delta}
; see Section 3 of
Haeusler (1988)
for a matching lower bound based on an example. Recovering a
\sqrt{n_{2}}
-rate comparable to the independent case in (
14
) requires additional assumptions, under which sharper non-uniform Berry-Esseen bounds are available; see, for instance,
Fan et al. (2017)

Remark 5

(Other dependence structures)
.

The approach underlying
Theorem

4
extends to other dependence structures by substituting the martingale Berry–Esseen bound with an analogous result tailored to the structure of interest; see, for instance,
Chen and Shao (2004)
;
Hörmann (2009)
;
Hafouta (2022)
;
Liu and Austern (2023)
.

3.3

The validity results for
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}
established thus far are summarized in
Table

.

Table 1
:
Validity results for the confidence set
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}

defined in (
8
). “Conservative”
indicates that the miscoverage tends to zero as

\ratio\to\infty
in probability; “
1/2
” indicates
asymptotic validity at level
1/2
whenever the remainder terms vanish.
The Lindeberg–Feller condition in the third row is sufficient but not
necessary; see the discussion following
Theorem

3
.

For general stochastic optimization under
\beta
-mixing, the set
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}
is asymptotically conservative as long as
\ratio\to\infty
in probability. This is achieved when
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
is inconsistent or converges at a sub-optimal rate (
Example

1
and
Example

2
). For M-estimation problems of the form (
12
) under non-identical distributions, the same set
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}
achieves level
1/2
for any estimator, including super-efficient ones (
Example

3
), provided the remainder term vanishes. The dependent extension in
Theorem

4
accommodates arbitrary dependence structures with no further restriction. Notably,
Theorems

1
,
2
,
3
and
4
are all dimension-free and proved without any reference to the dimension or complexity of
\Theta
.

4
Finer Control over the Coverage Level

The results of
Section

3
establish that the set
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}
defined in (
8
) satisfies the honest validity guarantee (
2
) under both independent and dependent observations. In particular, miscoverage is bounded by
1/2
under M-estimation, and tends to zero when
\ratio\to\infty
in probability. While the set is thus valid across a range of levels, it does not allow the practitioner to prescribe a desired significance level
\alpha
. This section describes how to construct confidence sets at any prespecified level.

4.1
Data-splitting Approach

Suppose a miscoverage bound of the following form:

\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N}^{\dagger}\right)\leq p+\mathfrak{R}_{N,P^{N}},

(21)

is available for some
p\in(0,1)
and
\mathfrak{R}_{N,P^{N}}\geq 0
. Under M-estimation,
Theorems

3
and
4
establish (
21
) with
p=1/2
for both independent and dependent observations.

Towards achieving a target level
\alpha<p
, consider the following construction. For integers
B\geq 1
and
r\geq 0
, let
S_{1},G_{1},S_{2}\ldots,G_{B-1},S_{B}
be a partition of
\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
with
|G_{\ell}|=r
for
1\leq\ell\leq B-1
, where each
G_{\ell}
serves as a gap of
r
indices separating consecutive bins
S_{\ell}
and
S_{\ell+1}
. Assume that
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N}
satisfies

\beta(n,r)\leq\beta^{\dagger}(r)\quad\textrm{for all}\quad n\geq 1,

that is, the
\beta
-mixing coefficient as defined in (
9
) only depends on the gap size
r
.

Theorem 5
.

Suppose each set
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\dagger}_{\ell}
, constructed from
\{Z_{i}\,:\,i\in S_{\ell}\}
as in (
8
), satisfies (
21
) with same
p\in(0,1)
and remainder
\mathfrak{R}_{N_{0},P^{N}}
where
N_{0}=\min_{\ell}|S_{\ell}|
. Then for any
\alpha\in(0,p)
, setting
B\geq\lceil\log_{p}(\alpha)\rceil
, the union
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{DS}}_{N,\alpha}=\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{B}\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\dagger}_{\ell}
satisfies

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{DS}}_{N,\alpha}\right)\leq\alpha(1+p^{-1}\mathfrak{R}_{N_{0},P^{N}})^{B}+(B-1)\beta^{\dagger}(r).\end{split}

(22)

Whenever
\mathfrak{R}_{N_{0},P^{N}}\to 0
and
\beta^{\dagger}(r)\to 0
uniformly over
P^{N}\in\mathcal{P}^{N}
, the set
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\texttt{DS}}_{N,\alpha}
is asymptotically uniformly valid at level
\alpha
.

The proof appears in
Section

S.4
. For
p=1/2
and common significance levels
\alpha\in\{0.1,0.05,0.01\}
, this procedure requires
B\in\{4,5,7\}
bins respectively. The admissible range of
\alpha
is implicitly restricted by the sample size, since we need
N\geq B\geq\lceil\log_{1/2}(\alpha)\rceil
. A closely related procedure was proposed by
Kuchibhotla et al. (2024)
.

Theorem

5
is not limited to M-estimation: the result holds for general stochastic optimization whenever (
21
) is available with an appropriate
p
. As
Example

1
illustrates, the constant estimator in the U-statistics problem can yield miscoverage exceeding
1/2
due to degeneracy, so a larger
p
would be required there.

4.2
Lower Confidence Bounds Approach

Returning to the general stochastic optimization problem (
1
), suppose one can construct a data-dependent function
\widehat{t}_{\alpha}:\Theta\times\Theta\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, measurable with respect to
D_{2}
, such that

\mathbb{P}_{P^{2}}\left(\M_{2}(\theta(P^{N}))-\M_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1})\geq\widehat{\M}_{2}(\theta(P^{N}))-\widehat{\M}_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1})-\widehat{t}_{\alpha}(\theta(P^{N}),\widehat{\theta}_{1})|D_{1}\right)\geq 1-\alpha_{N},

(23)

where in many practical situations
\alpha_{N}=\alpha+o(1)
. This yields the lower confidence bound (LCB)-based confidence set:

\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{LCB}}_{N,\alpha}:=\left\{\theta\in\Theta:\,\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\theta)-\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1})-\widehat{t}_{\alpha}(\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1})\leq 0\right\}.

(24)

Theorem 6
.

Suppose
\widehat{t}_{\alpha}
satisfies (
23
). Then

\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\left(\theta(P^{N})\not\in\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{LCB}}_{N,\alpha}\right)\leq\alpha_{N}+\beta(r).

The proof appears in
Section

S.4
. This result holds at the same level of generality as
Theorem

1
: no structure is imposed on the optimization problem and the observations need not be independent. The only requirements are the basic inequality (
4
) and the lower bound condition (
23
).

It may initially appear puzzling that constructing a confidence set for
\theta(P^{N})
requires building a lower confidence bound for
\M_{2}(\theta(P^{N}))-\M_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1})
, which itself depends on
\theta(P^{N})
. A stronger but more transparent restatement of (
23
) clarifies what is actually needed:

\begin{split}\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\,\inf_{\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta}\,\mathbb{P}_{P^{2}}\left(\M_{2}(\theta)-\M_{2}(\theta^{\prime})\geq\widehat{\M}_{2}(\theta)-\widehat{\M}_{2}(\theta^{\prime})-\widehat{t}_{\alpha}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})|D_{1}\right)\geq 1-\alpha_{N}.\end{split}

(25)

That is, one needs a lower confidence bound for
\mathbb{M}_{2}(\theta)-\mathbb{M}_{2}(\theta^{\prime})
for every (non-stochastic) pair
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
. Under M-estimation with
\mathbb{M}_{2}(\theta)=n_{2}^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[m_{\theta}(Z_{i})]
, the difference reduces to
\mathbb{M}_{2}(\theta)-\mathbb{M}_{2}(\theta^{\prime})=n_{2}^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[m_{\theta}(Z_{i})-m_{\theta^{\prime}}(Z_{i})]
, for which such bounds follow from concentration inequalities or the central limit theorem.

Since the map
(\theta,\theta^{\prime})\mapsto\M_{2}(\theta)-\M_{2}(\theta^{\prime})
is always real-valued, a lower confidence bound can in principle be constructed without reference to the complexity of
\Theta
. This contrasts sharply with “classical” approaches based on the weak convergence of
r_{N}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}))
to a limit process, which often depends heavily on the complexity of
\Theta
.

4.3
Studentized Confidence Sets under M-estimation

This subsection develops a concrete choice of
\widehat{t}_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot)
satisfying (
23
) under M-estimation (
12
) with independent but not necessarily identically distributed observations. The requirement reduces to a lower confidence bound for the expected difference of loss functions based on a sample mean, for which a natural construction uses the central limit theorem for t-statistics
(
Bentkus and Götze, 1996
;
Bentkus et al., 1996
)
. For
\theta\in\Theta
, define the sample variance of
m_{\theta}(Z_{i})-m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}}(Z_{i})=(m_{\theta}-m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}})(Z_{i})
as

\widehat{\sigma}_{\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1}}^{2}:=\frac{1}{n_{2}-1}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\left\{(m_{\theta}-m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}})(Z_{i})-\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{j\in I_{2}}(m_{\theta}-m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}})(Z_{j})\right\}^{2}.

(26)

The studentized confidence set is

\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}:=\left\{\theta\in\Theta:\,\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\theta)-\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1})\leq n_{2}^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma}_{\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1}}\right\},

(27)

where
z_{\alpha}
denotes the
(1-\alpha)
-th quantile of the standard normal. This is the special case of
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{LCB}}_{N,\alpha}
defined in (
24
) with
\widehat{t}_{\alpha}(\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1})=n_{2}^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma}_{\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1}}
. Several validity results follow; all proofs appear in
Section

S.4.2
.

Theorem 7
.

Recall
\widehat{\xi}_{i}
defined in (
13
). For any
V>0
, define the truncated random variables,

\overline{\xi}_{i}=V^{-1}\widehat{\xi}_{i}\mathbf{1}\{|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|\leq V\},\quad\textrm{and}\quad M^{2}=\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathrm{Var}_{P_{i}}[\overline{\xi}_{i}|D_{1}].

(28)

There exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
,

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\left(\theta(P^{N})\not\in\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\right)\leq\alpha\\
&\quad+\mathbb{E}_{P^{1}}\left[\min\left\{1,C\left(\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{P}_{P_{i}}(\widehat{\xi}^{2}_{i}>V^{2}|D_{1})+\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\frac{|\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[\overline{\xi}_{i}|D_{1}]|}{M}+\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[|\overline{\xi}_{i}|^{3}|D_{1}]}{M^{3}}\right)\right\}\right].\end{split}

(29)

Theorem 8
.

Under (
12
), one has
n_{2}^{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}=\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathrm{Var}_{P_{i}}[\widehat{\xi}_{i}|D_{1}]
by definition (
11
). Setting
V^{2}=n_{2}^{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}
in
Theorem

7
gives

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\left(\theta(P^{N})\not\in\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\right)\leq\alpha\\
&\quad+\E_{P^{1}}\left[\min\left\{1,C\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|^{2}}{n_{2}^{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|}{n_{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}^{1/2}}\right\}\bigg|D_{1}\right]\right\}\right],\end{split}

(30)

for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
where
C>0
is an absolute constant.

Theorem 9
.

Assume
\{Z_{i}\,:\,i\in I_{2}\}
is IID. Set
V_{*}
as the largest solution that satisfies:

V_{*}^{2}=\E_{P^{2}}[\widehat{\xi}_{1}^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{\xi}_{1}^{2}\leq V_{*}^{2}n_{2}\}|D_{1}],

(31)

and define

R_{*}=n_{2}\mathbb{P}_{P^{2}}(\widehat{\xi}^{2}_{1}>n_{2}V_{*}^{2}|D_{1})+n_{2}|\mathbb{E}_{P^{2}}[\overline{\xi}_{1}|D_{1}]|+n_{2}\mathbb{E}_{P^{2}}[|\overline{\xi}_{1}|^{3}|D_{1}]

(32)

where
\overline{\xi}_{1}=(V_{*}^{2}n_{2})^{-1/2}\widehat{\xi}_{1}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{\xi}_{1}^{2}\leq V_{*}^{2}n_{2}\}
.
Then, (
29
) becomes

\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\left(\theta(P^{N})\not\in\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\right)\leq\alpha+\mathbb{E}_{P^{1}}[\min\{1,CR_{*}\}],

(33)

for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

The remainder in (
29
) vanishes under the Lindeberg-Feller condition, though this is sufficient but not necessary. See a counterexample in Example 1.1 of
Bentkus et al. (1996)
.
Theorem

8
and
Theorem

9
are direct consequences of
Theorem

7
.
Theorem

8
restates the result under more familiar moment conditions. The remainder in (
30
) should be compared with that in
Theorem

3
: beyond vanishing under the Lindeberg–Feller condition, it yields an explicit convergence rate of
n_{2}^{-\delta/2}
whenever
\E_{P_{i}}[|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|^{2+\delta}|D_{1}]
is finite for some
\delta\in(0,1]
. When
\{Z_{i}\,:\,i\in I_{2}\}
is IID, the condition weakens significantly. In particular, the second term of (
33
) tends to zero when
\widehat{\xi}_{1}
belongs to the domain of attraction of the normal law (DAN). Also under IID observations, the folllowing multiplicative error bound becomes available.

Theorem 10
.

Assume that
\{Z_{i}\,:\,i\in I_{2}\}
is IID. Define

z_{\alpha,n_{2}}=z_{\alpha}\sqrt{\frac{n_{2}}{n_{2}-1+z_{\alpha}^{2}}},

and let
V_{*}
be the largest solution satisfying

V_{*}^{2}(1+z_{\alpha,n_{2}}^{2})=\E_{P^{2}}[\widehat{\xi}_{1}^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{\xi}_{1}^{2}\leq V_{*}^{2}n_{2}\}|D_{1}].

(34)

Then, for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there exist absolute constants
C,C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}>0
such that

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\left(\theta(P^{N})\not\in\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\right)\leq\min\{1,C\min\left\{R_{1},R_{2}\right\}\},\end{split}

(35)

where

\begin{split}R_{1}&=\left(1-\Phi(z_{\alpha,n_{2}})\right)\mathbb{E}_{P^{1}}[\exp(R_{*})\mathbf{1}\{R_{*}\leq C^{\prime}(1+|z_{\alpha,n_{2}}|)^{2}\}]\quad\text{and}\\
R_{2}&=\exp(-z_{\alpha,n_{2}}^{2})\mathbb{P}_{P^{1}}(R_{*}\leq C^{\prime\prime}),\end{split}

with
R_{*}
defined as (
32
).

The proof follows from Theorem 2 of
Robinson and Wang (2005)
and sub-Gaussianity result of t-statistics in
Giné et al. (1997)
, yielding a tighter bound whenever
R_{*}
is small. This result also only requires
\widehat{\xi}_{1}
to belong to the domain of attraction of the normal law

Theorems

7
,
8
,
9
and
10
fail to capture the refined conservativeness governed by the ratio as in
Theorem

3
or
Theorem

4
. Existing non-uniform Berry-Esseen bounds for t-statistics
(
Shao, 1999
;
Jing et al., 2003
;
Wang, 2005
)
are not directly applicable since we require results for non-central t-statistics whose limiting distributions are non-standard
(
Bentkus et al., 2007
)
. Under additional assumptions, the following sharper result is available.

Theorem 11
.

Assume that
\{Z_{i}\,:\,i\in I_{2}\}
is IID and let
\sigma^{2}=\mathrm{Var}[\widehat{\xi}_{1}|D_{1}]
. Define

\displaystyle\Xi_{n_{2},t}=\E_{P^{2}}\left[\frac{(1+t)^{3}|\widehat{\xi}_{1}|^{3}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}\sigma^{3}}\min\left\{1,\frac{(1+t)|\widehat{\xi}_{1}|}{\sqrt{n_{2}}\sigma}\right\}|D_{1}\right],\quad\rho_{N}=n_{2}^{-1/2}\E_{P^{2}}[|\widehat{\xi}_{1}|^{3}/\sigma^{3}|D_{1}],\quad\textrm{and}

\displaystyle\Psi_{n_{2},t}=\exp\left(\frac{(t+\ratio)^{2}t}{4}\left(\frac{2(t+\ratio)}{3t}-2\right)\frac{\E_{P^{2}}[\widehat{\xi}_{1}^{3}|D_{1}]}{\sqrt{n_{2}}\sigma^{3}}\right).

Then, there exist finite absolute constants
C,C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}
such that

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\left(\theta(P^{N})\not\in\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\right)\\
&\quad=\E_{P^{1}}\left[\left(1-\Phi(z_{\alpha}+\ratio)\right)\Psi_{n_{2},z_{\alpha}}\exp(C^{\prime}\Xi_{n_{2},z_{\alpha}})\{1+C^{\prime\prime}(1+z_{\alpha})\rho_{n_{2}}\}\right],\end{split}

(36)

for all
\ratio\leq z_{\alpha}/5
and
0\leq z_{\alpha}\leq\rho_{n_{2}}^{-1}C
.

The proof follows from Theorem 1 of
Wang and Hall (2009)
; see also Theorem 2.1 of
Shao and Zhou (2016)
for a relevant result. Crucially, (
36
) is an equality, not an inequality, and recovers the precise dependence on in the tail. When
\E_{P^{2}}[|\widehat{\xi}^{3}||D_{1}]<\infty
and
z_{\alpha}=o(N^{1/6})
(
36
) implies

\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\left(\theta(P^{N})\not\in\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\right)/\E_{P^{1}}[1-\Phi(z_{\alpha}+\ratio)]\to 1.

Two natural extensions remain open: relaxing the finite third moment assumption to the domain of attraction of a stable law, and extending the range of validity beyond
\ratio\leq z_{\alpha}/5
. Both directions are non-trivial problems in their own right and merit dedicated treatment; they are left for future work.

4.4
Summary of Prescribed-level Methods

The constructions proposed in this section extend the based confidence set
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\dagger}_{N}
as in (
8
) to a prescribed significance level
\alpha
. the associated conditions are summarized in
Table

2
.

-mixing

)

)

)
