Table 2
:
Validity results for the prescribed-level confidence sets proposed in
this section. All constructions achieve the nominal level
\alpha
either in finite samples
(empirical Bernstein) or asymptotically (CLT-based and data-splitting); the data-splitting method restricts the admissible range of
\alpha
, depending on the sample size.
“Indep.” allows for non-identical distributions. The Lindeberg-Feller condition is sufficient but not necessary. DAN denotes the domain of attraction of the normal law.

Most results in this section are stated for M-estimation, with the exception of
Theorem

6
; the concrete construction satisfying (
23
) is nevertheless studied for M-estimation. The data-splitting approach is based on
Theorem

3
–
Theorem

4
and therefore extends to the observations with general dependence. Among the distributional assumptions presented, the weakest is the DAN condition of
Theorems

9
,
10
and
11
; Two directions are deferred to future work: extending the CLT-based confidence set (
27
) to self-normalized martingales, for which
Fan and Shao (2018)
provides a relevant Berry–Esseen bound; and obtaining a precise characterization of miscoverage analogous to
Theorem

11
for non-central t-statistics, which requires moderate and large deviation results not yet available in the literature.

Remark 6

(Comparison with existing methods)
.

Vogel (2008b)
study a confidence set essentially equivalent to (
8
) without sample-splitting. Their condition CI2, which enters directly into their validity guarantee, typically requires control of the covering numbers or metric entropy of
\Theta
, whereas the proposed methods require no such complexity conditions.

Dey et al. (2025)
study a confidence set for the same target but impose a “
strong central condition
”, requiring a finite moment-generating function for
\widehat{\xi}_{i}
, or some sub-exponential tails. By contrast, the present results require only moment conditions. In the IID case,
Theorem

9
requires no finite moments as long as
\widehat{\xi}_{i}\in\mathrm{DAN}
. The confidence set (
27
) was previously studied by
Chakravarti et al. (2019)
and
Park et al. (2025)
.
Chakravarti et al. (2019)
test against a null that is a set of multivariate Gaussians.
Park et al. (2025)
introduce synthetic noise to prevent the variance from vanishing, thereby inflating the confidence set; this inflation is unnecessary under the Lindeberg–Feller condition. Finally, while this manuscript focuses on inference for
\theta(P^{N})
itself, one may instead be interested in low-dimensional summary such as a univariate projection
\ell^{\top}\theta(P^{N})
for (non-random)
\ell\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Honest inference for such projections was studied for Z-estimation framework by
Chang and Kuchibhotla (2024)
, whose validity results impose smoothness conditions on
\M(\theta,P^{N})
and growth restrictions of the form
\mathrm{polylog}(d)=o(n)
.

Remark 7

(Lower Confidence Bounds based on Concentration Inequalities)
.

An alterative route to constructing
\widehat{t}_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot)
satisfying (
23
) is through concentration inequalities.
There is a wide range of tools for this purpose; see
Boucheron et al. (2013)
for classical results and
Hao et al. (2019)
;
Ramdas and Manole (2023)
;
Waudby-Smith and Ramdas (2024)
;
Bates et al. (2021)
for more recent developments. This approach may yield finite-sample valid confidence sets under stronger tail assumptions. Results based on the one-sided empirical Bernstein inequality are developed in
Section

S.10
.

5
Convergence Rates of the Confidence Sets

5.1
Diameter Bounds under General Optimization

This section establishes non-asymptotic bounds on the diameter of the proposed confidence sets, illustrating adaptive rates of convergence that depend on the unknown curvature of the objective. Throughout,
\theta(P^{N})
is assumed to be the unique minimizer of (
1
), and the following conditions are imposed.

(A1)

There exist constants
c_{0},\gamma\geq 0
such that

\M_{2}(\theta)-\M_{2}(\theta(P^{N}))\geq c_{0}\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|^{1+\gamma}

for all
\theta\in\Theta
.

(A2)

There exists a function
\phi_{n_{2}}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\E^{*}_{P^{2}}\left[\sup_{\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|<\delta}|(\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}-\mathbb{M}_{2})(\theta)-(\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}-\mathbb{M}_{2})(\theta(P^{N}))|\right]\leq\phi_{n_{2}}(\delta)

(37)

for every
n_{2}\geq 1
and
\delta>0
, where
\E^{*}_{P^{2}}[\cdot]
denotes outer expectation. Furthermore,
\phi_{n_{2}}(x)/x^{q}
is assumed non-increasing for some
q<1+\gamma
.

(A3)

For every
n_{1},n_{2}\geq 1
and
\varepsilon_{\mathtt{init}}>0
, the initial estimator

\widehat{\theta}_{1}
based on
D_{1}
satisfies

\mathbb{P}_{P^{1}}\!\left(\mathbb{E}_{P^{2}}[|\widehat{\M}_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1})-\widehat{\M}_{2}(\theta(P^{N}))|\,|\,D_{1}]\geq C_{\mathtt{init}}\cdot s_{n_{1},n_{2}}\right)\leq\varepsilon_{\mathtt{init}},

(38)

where
s_{n_{1},n_{2}},C_{\mathtt{init}}
are non-negative constants.

The parameter
\gamma
in
(A1)
links the optimization problem at hand to the curvature within the parameter space. This condition implies that
\theta(P^{N})
is a strong global minimizer of
\theta\mapsto\mathbb{M}_{2}(\theta)

(
Drusvyatskiy and Lewis, 2013
)
. The inequality
(A2)
is known as a maximal inequality
(
van der Vaart and Wellner, 1996
, Section 2.3.1)
with the modulus
\phi_{n_{2}}(\cdot)
reflecting the local complexity of
\Theta
. To handle possible measurability issues, the outer expectation
\E^{*}_{P^{2}}[\cdot]
is adopted. See Section 1.2 of
van der Vaart and Wellner (1996)
. Finally,
(A3)
pertains to the convergence rate of the initial estimator. As shown below, diameter of the proposed confidence set depends on all three quantities.
(A1)
and
(A2)
are standard in the analysis of M-estimators, as discussed in Theorem 3.2.5 of
van der Vaart and Wellner (1996)
. They also appear in
Kim and Pollard (1990)
to explain the differences between regular and irregular M-estimators in the estimation context.

For a set
A
equipped with a metric
\|\cdot\|
, define
\mathrm{Diam}_{\|\cdot\|}(A):=\sup\{\|a-b\|\,:\,a,b\in A\}
.

Theorem 12
.

Assume
(A1)
–
(A3)
. Define
r_{n_{2}}
as any value satisfying

r_{n_{2}}^{-2}\phi_{n_{2}}(c_{0}^{-1/(1+\gamma)}r_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)})\leq 1,

(39)

and set

\mathrm{R}^{\dagger}_{N}:=c_{0}^{-1/(1+\gamma)}(r_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)}+s_{n_{1},n_{2}}^{1/(1+\gamma)}).

Then, for any
n_{1},n_{2}\geq 1
and
\varepsilon>0
, writing
\mathbb{P}^{*}_{P^{N}}(\cdot)
for outer probability over
P^{N}
,

\mathbb{P}^{*}_{P^{N}}\left(\mathrm{Diam}_{\|\cdot\|}\big(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\dagger}_{N}\big)\leq C\varepsilon^{-1/(1+\gamma-q)}\mathrm{R}^{\dagger}_{N}\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon-\varepsilon_{\mathtt{init}}-\beta(r),

and
C
is a constant depending on
\gamma,q
and
C_{\mathtt{init}}
.

The proof appears in
Section

S.5.1
. The proof requires no independence structure on the observations and no particular form of the optimization problem, so the result extends beyond the M-estimation framework. The diameter shows dependence on the curvature parameter
\gamma
in
(A1)
despite the confidence set being constructed without prior knowledge of
\gamma
, reflecting adaptation to the local geometry of the problem. Diameter bounds for the data-splitting construction of
Section

4.1
follow as an immediate corollary. A companion bound for the LCB-based set (
24
) is developed in
Section

S.5.5
.

5.2
Diameter Bounds under M-estimation

Section

4.3
developed a confidence set for M-estimation at any prescribed significance level
\alpha
using
\widehat{t}_{\alpha}(\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1})=n_{2}^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma}_{\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1}}
. This subsection establishes a non-asymptotic diameter bound for this set. The conditions below are stated in the M-estimation notation of (
12
) under independent but not necessarily identically distributed observations.

(A4)

There exist functions
\omega_{n_{2},\mathtt{emp}},\omega_{\mathtt{pop}}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\E_{P^{2}}^{*}\left[\sup_{\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|<\delta}\left|\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})^{2}]\right|\right]\leq\omega^{2}_{n_{2},\mathtt{emp}}(\delta)

and

\sup_{\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|<\delta}\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})^{2}(Z_{i})]\leq\omega^{2}_{\mathtt{pop}}(\delta)

for every
n_{2}\geq 2
and
\delta>0
. The combined modulus is
\omega^{2}_{n_{2}}(\delta)=\omega^{2}_{n_{2},\mathtt{emp}}(\delta)+\omega^{2}_{\mathtt{pop}}(\delta)
, and
\omega_{n_{2}}(x)/x^{q}
is assumed non-increasing for some
q<1+\gamma
.

(A5)

For every
n_{1}\geq 1
and
n_{2}\geq 2
, and
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\mathtt{init}}>0
, the initial estimator based on
D_{1}
satisfies

\mathbb{P}_{P^{1}}\left(\frac{1}{n_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P^{2}|P^{1}}\left[\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\widehat{\xi}_{i}^{2}\right]+\widehat{\mathbb{C}}_{2}^{2}>\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{init}}\widetilde{s}^{2}_{n_{1},n_{2}}\right)\leq\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\mathtt{init}}.

Condition
(A4)
resembles
(A2)
, but controls the growth rate of the localized
squared
empirical process rather than the process itself. Condition
(A5)
plays the role of
(A3)
; the proof shows that
{s}^{2}_{n_{1},n_{2}}\leq\widetilde{s}^{2}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
under independence. The following result is proved in
Section

S.5.2
.

Theorem 13
.

Assume
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N}
are independent and
(A1)
,
(A2)
,
(A4)
and
(A5)
. Define
r_{n_{2}}
as in (
39
),
u_{n_{2}}
as any value satisfying

u_{n_{2}}^{-2}\omega_{n_{2}}(c_{0}^{-1/(1+\gamma)}u_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)})\leq n_{2}^{1/2}.

(40)

Set

\mathrm{R}_{N}^{\mathtt{CLT}}=c_{0}^{-1/(1+\gamma)}(r_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)}+u_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)}+\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}^{1/(1+\gamma)}).

Then, for any
n_{1}\geq 1
,
n_{2}\geq 2
and
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathbb{P}^{*}_{P^{N}}\left(\mathrm{Diam}_{\|\cdot\|}\big(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\big)\leq C\left(\frac{1+|z_{\alpha}|}{\varepsilon}\right)^{1/(1+\gamma-q)}\mathrm{R}_{N}^{\mathtt{CLT}}\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon-\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\mathtt{init}},

where
C
is a constant depending only on
\gamma,q
, and
\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{init}}
.

Remark 8

(Controlling
(A4)
)
.

Light-tail assumptions on

m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})}
, including sub-Gaussian and sub-exponential conditions, are commonly imposed to obtain
\omega_{n_{2}}
for unbounded processes. This assumption can be relaxed substantially.
Lemma

33
in
Section

S.7.6
provides general construction of
\omega_{n_{2}}
via a truncation argument under two settings: finite moment conditions, and sub-Weibull tails
(
Kuchibhotla and Chakrabortty, 2022
)
, the latter yielding sharper rates. See also Proposition 3.1 of
Giné et al. (2000)
and Proposition B.1 of
Kuchibhotla and Patra (2022)
for related results.

5.3
Localizing the Diameter Analysis

In many statistical applications,
(A1)
,
(A2)
, and
(A4)
hold only locally in a neighborhood of
\theta(P^{N})
. For convergence rate analysis of M-estimators, this is addressed by assuming consistency of the estimator first and then restricting attention to a neighborhood of
\theta(P^{N})

(
van der Vaart and Wellner, 1996
, Theorem 3.2.5)
. This approach does not extend directly to confidence set analysis, where the defining inequality must be evaluated at every
\theta\in\Theta
. The following two conditions provide the additional global structure needed to localize the diameter analysis.

(A1-
global
)

For
\rho>0
, there exist a function
C_{\rho}:[\rho,\infty)\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
with generalized inverse
C^{-1}_{\rho}(s)=\inf\{r\geq\rho\,:\,C_{\rho}(r)\geq s\}
and a function
g:[1,\infty)\mapsto[1,\infty)
, such that

\M_{2}(\theta)-\M_{2}(\theta(P^{N}))\geq C_{\rho}(\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|)\quad\text{for all}\quad\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|\geq\rho

and

C^{-1}_{\rho}(\lambda r)\leq g(\lambda)C^{-1}_{\rho}(r)\quad\text{for all}\quad r\geq\rho,\,\lambda\geq 1.

(A2-
ratio
)

For
\rho>0
, there exists
R(n_{2},\rho)
with
\lim_{t\to\infty}R(t,\rho)=0
, such that

\mathbb{P}_{P^{2}}^{*}\left(\sup_{\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|>\rho}\left|\frac{(\widehat{\M}_{2}-\M_{2})(\theta)-(\widehat{\M}_{2}-\M_{2})(\theta(P^{N}))}{\M_{2}(\theta)-\M_{2}(\theta(P^{N}))}\right|\geq C_{\mathtt{ratio}}R(n_{2},\rho)\right)\leq\varepsilon_{\mathtt{ratio}},

where
C_{\mathtt{ratio}}>0
is a constant depending on
\varepsilon_{\mathtt{ratio}}
.

Condition
(A1-global)
requires the population objective to grow globally away from
\theta(P^{N})
, but imposes no curvature condition on the growth. It is satisfied whenever
C_{\rho}(r)=cr^{p}
for some
c,p>0
, corresponding to polynomial growth of any order with
g(\lambda)=\lambda^{1/p}
. Condition
(A1-global)
allows for more exotic choices of growth functions. Condition
(A2-ratio)
is a ratio-type empirical process condition as in
Giné and Koltchinskii (2006)
, which can be shown to concentrate without local curvature.

Theorem 14
.

Assume
(A1)
and
(A2)
hold for all
\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|\leq\rho
, and that
(A3)
,
(A1-global)
,
(A2-ratio)
and
\beta(r)=0
hold. Then for
n_{2}
sufficiently large,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Diam}_{\|\cdot\|}\big(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\dagger}_{N})=O_{\mathbb{P}^{*}}\left(\max\{\mathrm{R}^{\dagger}_{N},\mathrm{Q}^{\dagger}_{N}\mathbf{1}\{\mathrm{Q}^{\dagger}_{N}\geq\rho\}\}\right)

where
\mathrm{Q}^{\dagger}_{N}:=C_{\rho}^{-1}\left(s_{n_{1},n_{2}}\right)
and
\mathrm{R}^{\dagger}_{N}
is as defined in
Theorem

12
.

The full non-asymptotic statement without assumption
\beta(r)=0
is provided in
Section

S.5.3
. The diameter bound comprises two terms. The first term
\mathrm{R}^{\dagger}_{N}
is the local rate from
Theorem

12
depending on the curvature and the modulus. The second term
\mathrm{Q}^{\dagger}_{N}
is a conservative global radius that eventually vanishes when
s_{n_{1},n_{2}}
is sufficiently small. Once
\mathrm{Q}^{\dagger}_{N}
falls below
\rho
, the bound reduces to the local rate
\mathrm{R}^{\dagger}_{N}
alone.

An analogous localization applies to the CLT-based set
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}
as in (
27
). We place one additional condition controlling the ratio-limit process of the squared empirical process.

(A4-
ratio
)

For
\rho>0
, there exist functions
S_{\mathtt{emp}}(n_{2},\rho,\alpha)
and
S_{\mathtt{pop}}(n_{2},\rho,\alpha)
with

\lim_{t\to\infty}S_{\mathtt{emp}}(t,\rho,\alpha)=0\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{t\to\infty}S_{\mathtt{pop}}(t,\rho,\alpha)=0,

such that

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{P^{2}}^{*}\left(\sup_{\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|>\rho}\frac{z_{\alpha}^{2}}{n_{2}^{2}}\left|\frac{\sum_{i\in I_{2}}(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})^{2}]}{\{\M_{2}(\theta)-\M_{2}(\theta(P^{N}))\}^{2}}\right|\geq\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{emp}}S_{\mathtt{emp}}\right)\\
&\qquad\leq\varepsilon_{\mathtt{\mathtt{emp}}}\end{split}

and

\sup_{\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|>\rho}\frac{z_{\alpha}^{2}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})^{2}(Z_{i})]}{n_{2}^{2}\{\M_{2}(\theta)-\M_{2}(\theta(P^{N}))\}^{2}}\leq S_{\mathtt{pop}},

where
\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{emp}}>0
is a constant depending on
\varepsilon_{\mathtt{\mathtt{emp}}}
, and
S_{\mathtt{emp}}=S_{\mathtt{emp}}(n_{2},\rho,\alpha)
,
S_{\mathtt{pop}}=S_{\mathtt{pop}}(n_{2},\rho,\alpha)
for brevity.

Theorem 15
.

Assume
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N}
are independent,
(A1)
,
(A2)
,
(A4)
hold for all
\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|\leq\rho
and that
(A5)
,
(A1-global)
,
(A2-ratio)
, and
(A4-ratio)
hold. Then for
n_{2}
sufficiently large,

\mathrm{Diam}_{\|\cdot\|}\big(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\big)=O_{\mathbb{P}^{*}}\bigg(\max\{(1+|z_{\alpha}|)^{1/(1+\gamma-q)}\mathrm{R}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N},\mathrm{Q}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\mathbf{1}\{\mathrm{Q}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\geq\rho\}\}\bigg),

where
\mathrm{Q}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}:=C_{\rho}^{-1}\left((1+|z_{\alpha}|)\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}\right)
and
\mathrm{R}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N}
is as defined in
Theorem

13
.

The full non-asymptotic statement is provided in
Section

S.5.4
.

6
Some Improvements for Less Conservative Sets

The confidence sets,
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\dagger}_{N}
and
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}
tends to be conservative. The miscoverage probability often lies strictly below the prescribed level, depending on the behavior of in
Theorem

3
. This section describes two potential remedies.

6.1
Contraction of the Initial Estimator

The proposed construction is agnostic to the choice of
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
, provided it is obtained approximately independently of
\widehat{\M}_{2}(\cdot)
. This flexibility allows to contract towards zero. One possible device is the convex combination

\widehat{\theta}^{\prime}_{1}(\theta,\lambda)=(1-\lambda)\theta+\lambda\widehat{\theta}_{1},\quad\lambda\in[0,1],

(41)

When
\theta=\theta(P^{N})
and
\lambda
is close to zero, this shrinks the initial estimator toward the population minimizer.

Example 4
.

Consider the normal mean setting of
Example

2
. The derivation there gives

\frac{\mathbb{C}_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\theta}^{\prime}_{1}(\theta,\lambda))}{\mathbb{V}_{2}(\widehat{\theta}^{\prime}_{1}(\theta,\lambda))}=\frac{n_{2}((1-\lambda)\mu+\lambda\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\mu)^{2}}{4\sigma^{2}}=\lambda^{2}\frac{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}_{2}^{2}}{\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}}=\lambda^{2}\ratio{}^{2}.

Any
\lambda\in[0,1)
strictly reduces this ratio.

In view of
Theorem

3
, reducing brings the miscoverage closer to
1/2
, preventing the confidence set from becoming overly conservative. Taking
\lambda=0
leads to a non-informative set as it flattens the curvature and corresponding confidence set (
8
) will simply be an entire parameter space
\Theta
. Thus
\lambda
should tend to zero slowly, for instance, at a logarithmic rate. A systematic methodological and theoretical treatment is deferred to future work.

6.2
Curvature Estimation

The basic inequality (
4
) underlying the construction uses the trivial upper bound of zero for
\M_{2}(\theta)-\M_{2}(\theta(P^{N}))
. This may be too loose for parameters far from the minimizer. A sharper confidence set can be obtained by replacing this bound with a upper confidence bound for the curvature. Specifically, suppose one has access to
\widehat{u}(\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1})
, satisfying

\mathbb{M}_{2}(\theta(P^{N}))-\mathbb{M}_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1})\leq\min\{\widehat{u}(\theta(P^{N}),\widehat{\theta}_{1}),0\}

(42)

with high probability. The corresponding confidence set is

\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{UCB}}_{N}:=\left\{\theta\in\Theta:\,\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\theta)-\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1})\leq\min\{\widehat{u}(\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1}),0\}\right\},

(43)

which satisfies
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{UCB}}_{N}\subseteq\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\dagger}_{N}
by construction. This approach can also be combined with the lower confidence bound methods of
Section

4.2
; for instance, (
27
) becomes

\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}-\mathtt{UCB}}_{N,\alpha}:=\left\{\theta\in\Theta:\,\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\theta)-\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1})-n_{2}^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma}_{\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1}}\leq\min\{\widehat{u}(\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1}),0\}\right\}.

(44)

This technique is employed by
Takatsu (2025)
to correct the bias of universal inference
(
Wasserman et al., 2020
)
under misspecification.
Takatsu (2025)
also establish that the resulting confidence set achieves asymptotically exact coverage under a product rate condition on the estimation errors of
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
and
\widehat{u}(\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1})
, reminiscent of double robustness in the semiparametric literature
(
Bickel, 1982
;
Pfanzagl and Wefelmeyer, 1985
;
Klaassen, 1987
)
.

Example 5
.

In the normal mean setting of Example
2
, direct calculation gives

\mathbb{M}_{2}(\mu)-\mathbb{M}_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1})=-(\mu-\widehat{\theta}_{1})^{2}.

Taking
\widehat{u}(\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1})=-(\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{1})^{2}
satisfies (
42
) almost surely.

Example 6

(Misspecified linear regression;
Takatsu (2025)
)
.

Let
(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{N},Y_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
and consider the best linear projection:

\theta(P^{N})=\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\E_{P^{2}}[(Y-\theta^{\top}X)^{2}].

Direct calculation gives

\mathbb{M}_{2}(\theta(P^{N}))-\mathbb{M}_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1})=-(\theta(P^{N})-\widehat{\theta}_{1})^{\top}\E_{P^{2}}[XX^{\top}](\theta(P^{N})-\widehat{\theta}_{1}).

Takatsu (2025)
considers a simple estimator

\widehat{u}(\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1})=-(\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{1})^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\right)(\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{1}),

which yields a substantially smaller confidence set in practice.

For many irregular problems, constructing a valid
\widehat{u}(\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1})
is itself a difficult or impossible task, without introducing structural assumptions that the proposed framework is designed to avoid. Two examples above are instances of the quadratic curvature regime, i.e.,
item

(A1)
with
\gamma=1
, which permits estimation of the curvature. See
Figure

A.3
for an illustration.

7
On Computation

Since the proposed confidence sets are constructed via test inversion, explicit computation may appear challenging in general. Moreover, the sets involving sample variance estimation, namely (
27
), can be non-convex due to the dependence of
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1}}
on
\theta
in both the left and right-hand sides of the defining inequality. This non-convexity is visible in
Figure

A.3
.

Despite these challenges, the geometry is tractable in some high-dimensional settings. First, the set (
8
) is convex if and only if the mapping
\theta\mapsto\widehat{\M}_{2}(\theta)
is quasi-convex, that is,

\widehat{\M}_{2}(t_{1})\leq 0\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\widehat{\M}_{2}(t_{2})\leq 0\implies\widehat{\M}_{2}(\lambda t_{1}+(1-\lambda)t_{2})\leq 0\quad\textrm{for all}\quad\lambda\in[0,1].

(45)

The complex dependence in the variance term can be removed by evaluating it at a fixed reference point. Introduce a third data split
D_{3}
, independent of
D_{2}
up to the
\beta
-mixing, and replace
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1}}
with

\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{\theta}_{3},\widehat{\theta}_{1}}^{2}:=\frac{1}{n_{2}-1}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\left\{(m_{\widehat{\theta}_{3}}-m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}})(Z_{i})-\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{j\in I_{2}}(m_{\widehat{\theta}_{3}}-m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}})(Z_{j})\right\}^{2},

(46)

where
\widehat{\theta}_{3}=\widehat{\theta}(D_{3})
.
With this substitution, the resulting confidence sets are convex under (
45
). Two concrete high-dimensional examples where the geometry can be characterized exactly are presented below.

7.1
High-dimensional Mean Inference

Consider normal mean setting of Example
2
with observations partitioned into three parts via
I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup I_{3}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,N\}
. The population parameter is

\theta(P^{N})=\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\E_{P_{i}}\|Z_{i}-\theta\|_{2}^{2}.

Let
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{3}
be estimators, obtained from
\{Z_{i}\,:\,i\in I_{1}\}
and
\{Z_{i}\,:\,i\in I_{3}\}
respectively. Consider the confidence sets

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT},1}

\displaystyle=\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\|Z_{i}-\theta\|_{2}^{2}-\|Z_{i}-\widehat{\theta}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\leq n_{2}^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{\theta}_{3},\widehat{\theta}_{1}}\right\},\quad\mathrm{and}

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT},2}

\displaystyle=\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\|Z_{i}-\theta\|_{2}^{2}-\|Z_{i}-\widehat{\theta}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\leq n_{2}^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{\theta}_{3},\widehat{\theta}_{1}}-\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}\right\}.

where
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT},1}
is the set (
27
) with variance estimated at
\widehat{\theta}_{3}
, and
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT},2}
further combines the upper confidence bound of
Example

5
as in (
44
). At
\alpha=1/2
where
z_{\alpha}=0
, both sets become equivalent to (
8
) and (
43
) respectively.

Theorem 16
.

Both confidence sets are Euclidean balls. Denoting

\overline{Z}_{2}=n_{2}^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}Z_{i}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\widehat{H}=\overline{Z}_{2}-\widehat{\theta}_{1},

the centers and squared radii are as follows:

(1)

\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT},1}
: center
\overline{Z}_{2}
, squared radius
\|\widehat{H}\|^{2}_{2}+n_{2}^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{\theta}_{3},\widehat{\theta}_{1}}
, and

(2)

\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT},2}
: center
(\overline{Z}_{2}+\widehat{\theta}_{1})/2
, squared radius
\|\widehat{H}\|^{2}_{2}/4+n_{2}^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{\theta}_{3},\widehat{\theta}_{1}}/2
.

7.2
High-dimensional Misspecified Linear Regression

Consider
(X_{1}^{\top},Y_{1})^{\top},\ldots,(X_{N}^{\top},Y_{N})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
with three-way partition
I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup I_{3}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,N\}
. The population parameter is the best linear projection:

\theta(P^{N})=\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\E_{P_{i}}[(Y_{i}-\theta^{\top}X_{i})^{2}].

Let
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{3}
be arbitrary estimators in from
\{Z_{i}\,:\,i\in I_{1}\}
and
\{Z_{i}\,:\,i\in I_{3}\}
respectively, and write
\widehat{\Gamma}={n_{2}}^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}
. Consider the confidence sets

\begin{split}\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT},1}&=\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}(Y_{i}-\theta^{\top}X_{i})^{2}-(Y_{i}-\widehat{\theta}_{1}^{\top}X_{i})^{2}\leq{n_{2}}^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{\theta}_{3},\widehat{\theta}_{1}}\right\},\quad\mathrm{and}\\
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT},2}&=\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}(Y_{i}-\theta^{\top}X_{i})^{2}-(Y_{i}-\widehat{\theta}_{1}^{\top}X_{i})^{2}\leq{n_{2}}^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{\theta}_{3},\widehat{\theta}_{1}}-\|\theta-\widehat{\theta}_{1}\|_{\widehat{\Gamma}}^{2}\right\}.\end{split}

(47)

Theorem 17
.

Both confidence sets are ellipsoids with respect to the
\widehat{\Gamma}
matrix norm. Denoting

\theta_{\mathrm{OLS}}=\left(\sum_{i\in I_{2}}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\right)^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}X_{i}Y_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{H}=\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta_{\mathrm{OLS}},

both confidence sets take the form
\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,\|\theta-\mathrm{center}\|_{\widehat{\Gamma}}^{2}\leq\mathrm{radius}^{2}\}
with

(1)

\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT},1}
: center
\theta_{\mathrm{OLS}}
, squared radius
\|\widehat{H}\|^{2}_{\widehat{\Gamma}}+{n_{2}}^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{\theta}_{3},\widehat{\theta}_{1}}
, and

(2)

\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT},2}
: center
(\theta_{\mathrm{OLS}}+\widehat{\theta}_{1})/2
, squared radius
\|\widehat{H}\|^{2}_{\widehat{\Gamma}}/4+{n_{2}}^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma}_{\widehat{\theta}_{3},\widehat{\theta}_{1}}/2
.

7.3
Further Remarks on Computation

Other high-dimensional problems may exhibit additional parameter space structure that can be exploited computationally. For instance, Manski’s maximum score estimator studied in the forthcoming
Section

8
, has
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
This constraint allows diameter approximations, outlined in
Section

S.9.3
and employed in the numerical study of
Section

9
. More generally, the computational aspects are problem-specific and require case-by-case analysis. An alternative that applies broadly is to estimate critical values via nonparametric regression on repeated parameter draws, as proposed by
Park et al. (2025, Section 7)
.

When the confidence set is made convex through the variance estimator (
46
) or when
\alpha=1/2
, its volume can be approximated efficiently. Evaluating set membership reduces to a single function evaluation and
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
always belongs to the confidence set. These facts together make the extensive methods for volume approximation of convex bodies directly applicable. See
Cousins and Vempala (2018)
and references therein.

Finally, even when computing the full set (
27
) is difficult, it remains useful for testing: evaluating the defining inequality at a single null value reduces the problem to one function evaluation, and the diameter convergence rate provides an explicit uniform critical radius. More broadly, the existence of the set (
27
) itself has theoretical value as a concrete procedure that is dimension-agnostic, adaptive to the unknown curvature of the objective, whose validity requires no assumptions beyond the domain of attraction of the normal law.

8
Statistical Applications

This section establishes theoretical guarantees for the proposed confidence sets in several concrete statistical problems. For each application, the confidence sets
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}
are constructed directly from the general formulae (
27
). At
\alpha=1/2
, the set reduces to
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\dagger}_{N}
as in (
8
) since
z_{\alpha}=0
. Two sets of theoretical guarantees are provided for each application: sufficient conditions for validity and convergence rates for the diameter. To this end, we can observe

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha_{1}}\subseteq\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\dagger}_{N}\subseteq\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha_{2}}\quad\text{when}\quad\alpha_{2}\leq 1/2\leq\alpha_{1}.

Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\!\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha_{2}}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\!\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\dagger}_{N}\right)\quad\text{for any}\quad\alpha_{2}\leq 1/2,

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Diam}_{\|\cdot\|}(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha_{1}})\leq\mathrm{Diam}_{\|\cdot\|}(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\dagger}_{N})\quad\text{for any}\quad 1/2\leq\alpha_{1}.

Thus the validity result for
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\dagger}_{N}
also implies the validity for
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}
whenever
\alpha\leq 1/2
. Similarly, the diameter bound for
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\dagger}_{N}
also implies the same diameter bound for
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}
with
1/2\leq\alpha
. All results follow from the general theory of
Section

3
and
Section

5
applied to the specific scenarios.

Throughout,
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N}
are assumed independent but not necessarily identically distributed. The observations are split with
|I_{1}|=n_{1}
,
|I_{2}|=n_{2}
and
r=0
so that
N=n_{1}+n_{2}
.

8.1
High-dimensional Mean Inference

Consider independent observations
X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with a common mean. The inference of interest is the expectation of
X
under
P^{2}
:

\theta(P^{N}):=\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\E_{P_{i}}\|X_{i}-\theta\|_{2}^{2}.

The covariance matrix of
X_{i}
is allowed to vary across
i
, denoted
\Sigma_{i}:=\E_{P_{i}}[(X_{i}-\theta(P^{N}))(X_{i}-\theta(P^{N}))^{\top}]
. Although mean estimation may appear elementary, inference for the mean in growing dimensions under weak distributional assumptions remains an active area of research
(
Lugosi and Mendelson, 2019
)
. Denote the average covariance matrices within each split by

\bar{\Sigma}_{k}=n_{k}^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{k}}\Sigma_{i}\quad\textrm{for}\quad k\in\{1,2\}.

Validity.

Theorem 18
.

Let
X_{i}^{\circ}=\bar{\Sigma}_{2}^{-1/2}(X_{i}-\theta(P^{N}))
and define

R_{n_{2}}=\sup_{u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{\langle X_{i}^{\circ},u\rangle^{2}}{n_{2}}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|\langle X_{i}^{\circ},u\rangle|}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\right\}\right].

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that the following hold.

1.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
n_{2}\geq 2
,

\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\!\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\right)\;\leq\;\alpha+\min\{1,CR_{n_{2}}\}.

2.

For
\alpha\in(0,1/2]
and
n_{2}\geq 1
, setting
\ratio=\sqrt{n_{2}}\|\bar{\Sigma}_{2}^{-1/2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}))\|/2
,

\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\right)\leq\min\left\{\E_{P^{1}}\left[1-\Phi(\ratio)+\frac{C}{(1+\ratio)^{2}}\right],\;\alpha+\min\{1,CR_{n_{2}}\}\right\}.

The remainder term
R_{n_{2}}
satisfies

R_{n_{2}}\leq Cn_{2}^{-(1+\delta/2)}\sup_{u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[|\langle X_{i}^{\circ},u\rangle|^{2+\delta}].

The condition
\sup_{u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[|\langle X_{i}^{\circ},u\rangle|^{2+\delta}]\leq K
for some constant
K\geq 1
is the so-called
L_{2+\delta}
-
L_{2}
norm equivalence, under which
R_{n_{2}}\leq CKn_{2}^{-\delta/2}
without any dependence on
d
. This condition, with particular emphasis on
\delta=1
, is widely employed in high-dimensional covariance matrix estimation
(
Minsker, 2018
;
Mendelson and Zhivotovskiy, 2020
)
and high-dimensional least squares
(
Oliveira, 2016
;
Catoni, 2016
;
Mourtada et al., 2022
)
.
This assumption is considerably less restrictive than imposing the sub-Gaussianity of
X
since any such
X
satisfies the
L_{2+\delta}
-
L_{2}
norm equivalence with
\delta\geq 2
. See Remarks 2.19, 2.20 and Figure S.7 of
Patil et al. (2022)
for useful visual comparison. Note that
Theorem

18
establishes the validity under weaker conditions.

When
\alpha\leq 1/2
, validity is further controlled by the first argument of the outer minimum, which decays whenever is large. This occurs when the initial estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
converges to
\theta(P^{N})
slower than
n_{2}^{-1/2}
. To illustrate this in high-dimensional problems, if
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
is the sample mean on
D_{1}
, then

\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|\asymp\sqrt{d/n_{1}}\implies\ratio\asymp\sqrt{dn_{2}/n_{1}}/\lambda^{1/2}_{\max}(\bar{\Sigma}_{2}).

Remarkably, when
n_{1}\asymp n_{2}
and
\lambda_{\max}(\bar{\Sigma}_{2})=O(1)
, the validity condition reduces to
d\to\infty
alone, with no dependence on the proportion
d/n_{1}
or
d/n_{2}
. This is substantially weaker than any moment equivalent condition.

Width Analysis.

Theorem 19
.

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that the following hold.

1.

For
\alpha\in[1/2,1)
,
n_{1},n_{2}\geq 1
, and any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
,

\mathrm{Diam}_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}\bigr)\leq C\varepsilon^{-1/2}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{tr}(\bar{\Sigma}_{2})}{n_{2}}}+\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|_{2}\right\}.

2.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
n_{1}\geq 1
, and any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, let

\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
be as in
(A5)
. With probability at least
1-\varepsilon
, provided
\max\{2,z_{\alpha}^{2}C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\}\leq n_{2}

\mathrm{Diam}_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}\bigr)\leq C_{\varepsilon}\left(1+|z_{\alpha}|\right)\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{tr}(\bar{\Sigma}_{2})}{n_{2}}}+\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}^{1/2}\right\},

where
C_{\varepsilon}
and
C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
depend on
\varepsilon
, but not on
d
or
\alpha
.

Corollary 19.1
.

Suppose the initial estimator satisfies, for all
n_{1}\geq N_{1}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|_{2}^{2}=O_{P^{1}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{tr}(\bar{\Sigma}_{1})}{n_{1}}\right).

(48)

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, and
n_{1}\geq N_{1}
, with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Diam}_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}\bigr)\leq C_{\varepsilon}\left(1+|z_{\alpha}|\right)\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{tr}(\bar{\Sigma}_{2})}{n_{2}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{tr}(\bar{\Sigma}_{1})}{n_{1}}}\right\},

provided
n_{2}\geq 1
when
\alpha\geq 1/2
, and
n_{2}\geq\max\{2,z_{\alpha}^{2}C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\}
when
\alpha<1/2
,
where
C_{\varepsilon}
and
C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
depend on
\varepsilon
, but not on
d
or
\alpha
.

The minimum sample size requirements reflect a genuine distinction the two cases. For
\alpha\geq 1/2
, the rate holds under
n_{2}\geq 1
by directly analyzing the analytical expression for the diameter given in
Theorem

16
, bypassing sample variance estimation entirely. For
\alpha<1/2
, the sample size must be large enough depending on
\varepsilon
, as the result relies on
Theorem

15
which requires concentration of the sample variance.

Condition (
48
) is satisfied when
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
is the sample mean based on
D_{1}
. When
\mathrm{tr}(\bar{\Sigma}_{1})=\mathrm{tr}(\bar{\Sigma}_{2})
,
Corollary

19.1
implies that the balanced split
n_{1}=n_{2}=N/2
minimizes the diameter, yielding the rate
\sqrt{2\mathrm{tr}(\bar{\Sigma}_{1})/N}
, which is minimax optimal: it matches the exact risk of mean estimation under a multivariate Gaussian, as established by the formal lower bound argument of
Lee and Valiant (2022, Section 5)
. Both
Theorem

18
and
Theorem

19
impose no restriction on
d
, hence both the validity guarantee and the convergence rate are dimension-agnostic.

The results in this subsection extend to inference for the Fréchet mean on a general metric space
\Theta
, where
(A1)
is referred to as a growth condition, or variance inequality. The quadruple condition studied in
Schötz (2019)
can be used to establish
(A2)
. A detailed treatment is deferred to future work.

8.2
High-dimensional Misspecified Linear Regression

Consider independent observations
(X_{1}^{\top},Y_{1})^{\top},\ldots,(X_{N}^{\top},Y_{N})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
generated from

\displaystyle Y_{i}=\theta(P^{N})^{\top}X_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\quad\text{where}\quad\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[\varepsilon_{i}X_{i}]=0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[\varepsilon_{i}^{2}|X_{i}]=\sigma_{i}^{2}.

The inference of interest is the best linear projection under
P^{2}
:

\theta(P^{N}):=\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\E_{P_{i}}[(Y-\theta^{\top}X)^{2}].

Denote the average Gram matrices within each split by

\displaystyle\bar{\Gamma}_{k}=n_{k}^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{k}}\E_{P_{i}}[X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}]\quad\textrm{for}\quad k\in\{1,2\}.

We assume that
\bar{\Gamma}_{2}
is invertible such that
\theta(P^{N})
exists even when the regression function
\E_{P^{2}}[Y_{i}|X_{i}]
is not linear.
We introduce the following assumptions:

(B1)

There exists a constant
\bar{\sigma}>0
such that
\sigma_{i}\leq\bar{\sigma}
for all
i\in I_{2}
.

(B2)

There exist constants
q_{x}\geq 2
and
L\geq 1
such that

\displaystyle\E_{P_{i}}[(u^{\top}\bar{\Gamma}_{2}^{-1/2}X_{i})^{q_{x}}]\leq L^{q_{x}},

for all
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
i\in I_{2}
.

(B3)

There exists constants
q_{y}\geq 2
and
K>0
such that

\displaystyle\E_{P_{i}}[|Y_{i}-\theta(P^{N})^{\top}X_{i}|^{q_{y}}\mid X_{i}]\leq K^{q_{y}}

for all
i\in I_{2}
.

Condition
(B1)
bounds the conditional variance of the population residuals. Condition
(B2)
imposes an
L_{q_{x}}
-
L_{2}
norm equivalence on covariates, allowing for heavy-tailed distributions and forging sub-Gaussian or sub-exponential assumptions. Condition
(B3)
requires finite conditional moments of the population residuals.

Validity.

Theorem 20
.

Assume
(B1)
and
(B2)
with
q_{x}=4
. Let
\Sigma_{i}=\mathrm{Cov}_{P_{i}}(X_{i})
, and suppose there exits constants
\underline{\sigma},\underline{\lambda}>0
such that
\underline{\sigma}\leq\sigma_{i}
, and
\underline{\lambda}\leq\lambda_{\min}(\bar{\Gamma}_{2}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{i}\bar{\Gamma}_{2}^{-1/2})
for all
i\in I_{2}
. Define the sandwich covariance matrix and normalized score

\bar{H}_{2}=\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathrm{Cov}_{P_{i}}(X_{i}\epsilon_{i})\quad\textrm{and}\quad W_{i}^{\circ}=\bar{H}_{2}^{-1/2}(X_{i}\epsilon_{i}-\E_{P_{i}}[X_{i}\epsilon_{i}]),

and define

\begin{split}R_{n_{2}}&=\inf_{\delta>0}\left\{\frac{2L^{2}\delta}{\underline{\sigma}\sqrt{\underline{\lambda}}}+\mathbb{P}_{P^{1}}(\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|_{\bar{\Gamma}_{2}}>\delta)\right\}\\
&\quad+\sup_{u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{\langle W_{i}^{\circ},u\rangle^{2}}{n_{2}}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|\langle W_{i}^{\circ},u\rangle|}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\right\}\right].\end{split}

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that the following hold.

1.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
n_{2}\geq 2
,

\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\!\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\right)\;\leq\;\alpha+\min\{1,CR_{n_{2}}\}.

2.

For
\alpha\in(0,1/2]
and
n_{2}\geq 1
, setting

\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}^{2}=\frac{n_{2}\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|_{\bar{\Gamma}_{2}}^{2}}{4\bar{\sigma}^{2}+2L^{4}\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|_{\bar{\Gamma}_{2}}^{2}},

it holds

\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\right)\leq\min\left\{\E_{P^{1}}\left[1-\Phi(\widetilde{\Delta}_{2})+\frac{C}{(1+\widetilde{\Delta}_{2})^{2}}\right],\;\alpha+\min\{1,CR_{n_{2}}\}\right\}.

As in
Theorem

18
, the remainder
R_{n_{2}}
becomes negligible under a finite
(2+\delta)
-th moment condition
\sup_{u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[|\langle W_{i}^{\circ},u\rangle|^{2+\delta}]\leq K
. The first term in
R_{n_{2}}
also requires consistency of
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
in the
\bar{\Gamma}_{2}
-norm, which implicitly places a dimension requirement, such that
d=o(n_{1})
.

When
\alpha\leq 1/2
, validity holds under weaker conditions when
\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}
is large. In particular,

\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}\to\infty\quad\text{when}\quad\min\left\{\frac{n_{2}\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|_{\bar{\Gamma}_{2}}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}},\frac{n_{2}}{L^{4}}\right\}\to\infty.

This does not require consistency of
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
. If the initial estimator is inconsistent or converges slowly, for instance, a penalized estimator with a large regularization parameter, then
\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}
diverges as
n_{2}\to\infty
. In this regime,
n_{1}
may be much smaller than
d
. As an illustration, consider the case when
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
is the OLS estimator on
D_{1}
. Then

n_{2}\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|^{2}_{\bar{\Gamma}_{2}}\gtrsim\frac{dn_{2}}{n_{1}}\cdot\lambda_{\min}(\bar{\Gamma}_{1}^{-1/2}\bar{\Gamma}_{2}\bar{\Gamma}_{1}^{-1/2}).

Therefore when
n_{1}\asymp n_{2}
and
\lambda_{\max}(\bar{\Gamma}_{1}^{-1/2}\bar{\Gamma}_{2}\bar{\Gamma}_{1}^{-1/2})
bounded away from zero, validity at any level
\alpha\leq 1/2
holds as long as
d,n_{2}\to\infty
and does not require any proportional behavior of
n_{2}
and
d
.

Width Analysis.

Theorem 21
.

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that the following hold.

1.

Assume
(B1)
and
(B2)
with
q_{x}>2
. For
\alpha\in[1/2,1)
,
n_{1}\geq 1
, and any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\varepsilon-\exp(-Cn_{2})
,

\mathrm{Diam}_{\|\cdot\|_{\bar{\Gamma}_{2}}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}\bigr)\leq C\varepsilon^{-1/2}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\sigma}^{2}d}{n_{2}}}+\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|_{\bar{\Gamma}_{2}}\right\}

provided that
n_{2}
satisfies
n_{2}\geq\mathfrak{C}d
where
\mathfrak{C}
depends only on
q_{x}
.

2.

Assume
(B1)
,
(B2)
with
q_{x}\geq 4
,
(B3)
, and let

\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
be as in
(A5)
. For
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
n_{1}\geq 1
, and any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
,

\mathrm{Diam}_{\|\cdot\|_{\bar{\Gamma}_{2}}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}\bigr)\leq C_{\varepsilon}(1+|z_{\alpha}|)\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\overline{\sigma}^{2}d}{n_{2}}}+\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}^{1/2}\right\}

provided that
n_{2}
satisfies

C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon^{\circ}}\max\bigg\{((1+|z_{\alpha}|)d\log(2d)L^{4})^{q_{x}/(q_{x}-2)},((1+|z_{\alpha}|)(1+K)dL^{2})^{p/(p-1)}\bigg\}\leq n_{2},

(49)

where
p=\min\{q_{y},q_{x}/2\}
, and
C_{\varepsilon},C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
depend on
\varepsilon
, but not on
d
or
\alpha
.

Corollary 21.1
.

Suppose the initial estimator satisfies, for all
n_{1}\geq N_{1}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|^{2}_{\bar{\Gamma}_{1}}=O_{P^{1}}\left(\frac{d\bar{\sigma}^{2}}{n_{1}}\right).

(50)

Assume
(B1)
and
(B2)
with
q_{x}>2
when
\alpha\geq 1/2
and
q_{x}\geq 4
when
\alpha<1/2
. Additionally assume
(B3)
when
\alpha<1/2
. For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
n_{1}\geq N_{1}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Diam}_{\|\cdot\|_{\bar{\Gamma}_{2}}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}\bigr)\leq C_{\varepsilon}(1+|z_{\alpha}|)\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\sigma}^{2}d}{n_{2}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\sigma}^{2}d}{n_{1}}}\lambda^{1/2}_{\max}(\bar{\Gamma}_{1}^{-1/2}\bar{\Gamma}_{2}\bar{\Gamma}_{1}^{-1/2})\right\}

with probability at least
1-\varepsilon-\exp(-Cn_{2})
,
provided
n_{2}\geq\mathfrak{C}d
when
\alpha\geq 1/2
, and with probability
1-\varepsilon
provided
n_{2}
satisfies (
49
) when
\alpha<1/2
, where
C
is a universal constant,
p=\min\{q_{y},q_{x}/2\}
,
\mathfrak{C}
depends on
q_{x}>2
,
C_{\varepsilon}
and
C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
depend on
\varepsilon
, but not on
d
or
\alpha
.

Theorem

21
reveals a salient distinction between
\alpha\geq 1/2
and
\alpha<1/2
. For
\alpha\geq 1/2
, the width guarantee requires the weak moment assumptions
(B1)
and
(B2)
with
q_{x}>2
, and a sample size
n_{2}\geq\mathfrak{C}d
for
\mathfrak{C}
depending only on
q_{x}
. This is obtained by directly analyzing the analytical expression for the diameter in
Theorem

17
, bypassing sample variance estimation, as in the mean inference case. For
\alpha<1/2
, the width guarantee is obtained as an application of
Theorem

15
under stronger assumptions to control concentration of the sample variance, namely
(B2)
with
q_{x}\geq 4
and
(B3)
. Under the weakest moment conditions implied by these assumptions, the sample size requirement becomes
d=o(\sqrt{n_{2}})
, reflecting a well-known quadratic barrier. Throughout, the assumptions permit heavy-tailed covariates without any sub-Gaussian condition.

Condition (
50
) is satisfied when
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
is the OLS estimator based on
D_{1}
. When
\bar{\Gamma}_{2}=\bar{\Gamma}_{1}
,
Corollary

21.1
implies that the balances split
n_{1}=n_{2}=N/2
minimizes the diameter, yielding the rate
\sqrt{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}d/N}
, which is minimax optimal as established by Theorem 1 of
Mourtada (2022)
. The combined guarantees of
Theorem

20
and
Theorem

21
at
\alpha=1/2
yield dimension-agnostic validity with diameter rate
\sqrt{d\bar{\sigma}^{2}/n_{2}}
under
\mathfrak{C}d\leq n
, which appears to be new. The same guarantee extends to any fixed
\alpha\leq 1/2
by employing the data-splitting method of
Section

4.1
.

Recently,
Chang and Kuchibhotla (2024)
proposed confidence sets for high-dimensional OLS based on
Z
-estimation. Theorem 8 of
Chang and Kuchibhotla (2024)
establishes a similar diameter bound but requires
d=o(n^{1-2/q_{x}})
, matching the first term of (
E.14
) in
Theorem

21
with
\alpha<1/2
.
Chang et al. (2023)
provides a one-step bias-corrected method, improving the requirement to
d=o(n^{2/3})
.

Remark 9
.

Since
\mathbb{C}_{P^{2}}(\theta)=\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|^{2}_{\bar{\Gamma}_{2}}\geq\lambda_{\min}(\bar{\Gamma}_{2})\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|^{2}_{2}
,

(A1)
holds with
c_{0}=\lambda_{\min}(\bar{\Gamma}_{2})
and
\gamma=1
. Therefore,
Theorem

21
can be restated in terms of the
\|\cdot\|_{2}
-norm with an additional assumption that
\lambda_{\min}(\bar{\Gamma}_{2})>
forsomeconstant
\lambda
¿ 0
.\end{remark}\par\begin{remark}The proposed framework can be easily extended to penalized least squares, where the minimizer is defined as\begin{equation}\theta(P^{N}):=\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\E_{P_{i}}[(Y-\theta^{\top}X)^{2}]+\lambda(\theta),\end{equation}and $\lambda:\Theta\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{+\infty\}$ is a convex function of $\theta$, which may depend on $n$ (but not on data). We obtain the following validity result.
\begin{theorem}Assume $\lambda(\cdot)$ is a convex function. Then, the same conclusion as \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{thm:LR-valid} holds for the confidence set $\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}$ for the penalized objective \eqref{eq:pen-objective}.
\end{theorem}\end{remark}

8.3

Manski’s Discrete Choice Model

Consider independent observations
(X_{1}^{\top},Y_{1})^{\top},\ldots(X_{N}^{\top},Y_{N})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\}
generated from the binary response model:

Y_{i}:=\mathrm{sgn}(\theta(P^{N})^{\top}X_{i}+\varepsilon_{i})\quad\text{where}\quad\mathrm{sgn}(t)=2\mathbf{1}\{t\geq 0\}-1,

(52)

where the error
\varepsilon_{i}
has zero conditional median given
X_{i}
, i.e.,
\mathrm{med}(\varepsilon_{i}|X_{i})=0
, but is otherwise allowed to depend on
X_{i}
. The inference of interest is

\displaystyle\theta(P^{N}):=\argmax_{\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\,\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\E_{P_{i}}[Y_{i}\,\mathrm{sgn}(\theta^{\top}X_{i})].

A natural estimator of
\theta(P^{N})
is maximum score estimator
(
Manski, 1975
)
, defined as

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta}_{1}:=\argmax_{\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\,\sum_{i\in I_{1}}Y_{i}\,\mathrm{sgn}(\theta^{\top}X_{i}).

(53)

The asymptotic behavior of
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
is non-standard
(
Manski, 1985
;
Kim and Pollard, 1990
)
. The inference for this problem is challenging, and
Cattaneo et al. (2020)
proposes a bootstrap-based approach. The following assumptions on the joint distribution of
(X_{i},\varepsilon_{i})
are standard in the literature
(
Mukherjee et al., 2019
;
Mukherjee et al., 2021
)

(B4)

Let
\eta_{P_{i}}(x):=\mathbb{P}_{P_{i}}(Y=1\mid X=x)
. There exist constants
C_{0}>0
,
0<t^{*}<1/2
, and
\gamma>0
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P_{i}}\left(\left|\eta_{P_{i}}(X)-\frac{1}{2}\right|<t\right)\leq C_{0}t^{1/\gamma},

for all
0<t<t^{*}
and
i\in I_{2}
.

(B5)

There exists a constant
c_{1}>0
, not depending on
n_{2}
or
d
, such that

\displaystyle c_{1}\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|_{2}\leq\mathbb{P}_{P_{i}}\left(\mathrm{sgn}(\theta^{\top}X_{i})\neq\mathrm{sgn}(\theta(P^{N})^{\top}X_{i})\right)

for all
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
i\in I_{2}
.

Assumption
(B4)
is the low noise (margin) assumption in the classification literature
(
Mammen and Tsybakov, 1999
;
Tsybakov, 2004
)
. It quantifies the deviation of the conditional class probability from
1/2
near the decision boundary; as
\gamma\to 0
, the decision boundary becomes well-separated, representing the most favorable situation for estimation. Assumption
(B5)
relates the distribution of covariates
X_{i}
to the geometry in the parameter space
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. See
Audibert and Tsybakov (2007)
;
Mukherjee et al. (2021)
for further discussion.

Validity.

Theorem 23
.

Assume
(B5)
and define
R_{n_{2}}=(n_{2}c_{1}\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|)^{-1/2}
. There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that the following hold.

1.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
n_{2}\geq 2
,

\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\!\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\right)\;\leq\;\alpha+\E_{P^{1}}[\min\{1,CR_{n_{2}}\}].

2.

Additionally assume
(B4)
. For
\alpha\in(0,1/2]
and
n_{2}\geq 1
, setting

\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}^{2}=\mathfrak{C}n_{2}\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|\min\{\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|^{2\gamma},(t^{*})^{2}\},

with
\mathfrak{C}
depending on
C_{0}
and
c_{1}
,
it holds

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,1/2}\right)\\
&\quad\leq\min\left\{\E_{P^{1}}\left[1-\Phi(\widetilde{\Delta}_{2})+\frac{C}{(1+\widetilde{\Delta}_{2})^{2}}\right],\;\alpha+\E_{P^{1}}[\min\{1,CR_{n_{2}}\}]\right\}.\end{split}

The validity requires
n_{2}\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|\to\infty
in probability. Theorem 3.2 of
Mukherjee et al. (2019)
establishes that for Manski’s estimator,

\displaystyle n_{2}\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|=O_{P^{1}}\left(n_{2}\cdot\left(\frac{d\log(n_{1}/d)}{n_{1}}\right)^{1/(1+2\gamma)}\right).

With an even split
n_{1}\asymp n_{2}
, this diverges for all
\gamma
bounded away from zero. For small
d/n_{1}
and
\gamma\to 0
(the well-separated, low dimensional case),
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
converges nearly at rate
n_{1}^{-1}
and
n_{2}\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|
remains bounded, so validity may fail. The condition is satisfied for growing
d
or any
\gamma
. Similarly, when
n_{1}\gg n_{2}
, the initial estimator converges too fast and validity may fail. For
\alpha=1/2
, the bound decays at the faster rate.

Validity holds trivially for both levels when
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
is inconsistent for
\theta(P^{N})
since
\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|
is bounded away from zero. Natural examples include penalized logistic regression
(
Cessie and Houwelingen, 1992
)
, support vector machines, and the smoothed maximum score estimator of
Horowitz (1992)
when the bandwidth is set to a fixed constant rather than optimally tuned.

Width Analysis.

In the following analysis, we assume that
X_{i}
for
i\in I_{2}
are identically distributed while still allowing for
\varepsilon_{i}
to be heterogeneous conditioning on
X_{i}
. This simplifies the analysis as
\mathbb{P}_{P_{i}}\left(\mathrm{sgn}(\theta^{\top}X_{i})\neq\mathrm{sgn}(\theta(P^{N})^{\top}X_{i})\right)
no longer depends on
i\in I_{2}
.

Theorem 24
.

Assume
(B4)
,
(B5)
and let
\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
be as in
(A5)
. For
n_{1}\geq 1
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
and any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
,

\begin{split}&\mathrm{Diam}_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}\big(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\big)\\
&\quad\leq C_{\varepsilon}\max\bigg\{\left(1+|z_{\alpha}|\right)^{2/(1+2\gamma)}\left(\left(\frac{d\log(n_{1}/d)}{n_{1}}\right)^{1/(1+2\gamma)}+\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}^{1/(1+\gamma)}\right),\\
&\quad\quad\mathrm{Q}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\mathbf{1}\{\mathrm{Q}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\geq(t^{*})^{1/\gamma}\}\bigg\},\end{split}

provided
\max\{2,C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(1+|z_{\alpha}|)^{2}d\}\leq n_{2}
, where
\mathrm{Q}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}=(1+|z_{\alpha}|)\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}/t^{*}
,
C_{\varepsilon}
depends on
\varepsilon
,
C_{0}
,
c_{1}
and
\gamma
, while
C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
depends on
\varepsilon
,
t^{*}
and
\gamma
.

For the specific case where
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
is Manski’s maximum score estimator, we introduce the following additional assumption.

(B6)

There exists constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
, not depending on
n_{2}
or
d
, such that for all
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
i\in I_{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P_{i}}\left(\mathrm{sgn}(\theta^{\top}X_{i})\neq\mathrm{sgn}(\theta(P^{N})^{\top}X_{i})\right)\leq C_{1}\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|_{2},

and when
\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|_{2}\leq\delta
for some
\delta\in(0,(t^{*})^{1+1/\gamma})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P_{i}}\left(\mathrm{sgn}(\theta^{\top}X_{i})\neq\mathrm{sgn}(\theta(P^{N})^{\top}X_{i})\right)\leq C_{2}\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|_{2}^{1+\gamma}.

Together, Assumptions
(B4)
and
(B5)
establish a lower bound on the curvature
\mathbb{C}_{2}(\theta)\gtrsim\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|^{1+\gamma}
locally, while
(B6)
provides the matching local upper bound
\mathbb{C}_{2}(\theta)\lesssim\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|^{1+\gamma}
. the first part of
(B6)
has appeared in the literature, for instance, Assumption 2.8 of
Mukherjee et al. (2021)
. The second part is considerably stronger, and the margin condition
(B4)
must be sharp. This is not implied by any of the standard assumptions in the maximum score literature. The data-generating distribution in
Section

9
satisfies this condition. Note that
Theorem

23
and
Theorem

24
require only the lower bound.

Corollary 24.1
.

Assume
(B4)
,
(B5)
,
(B6)
and suppose the initial estimator satisfies for all
n_{1}\geq N_{1}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|_{2}=O_{P^{1}}\left(\frac{d\log(n_{1}/d)}{n_{1}}\right)^{1/(1+2\gamma)}.

(54)

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
n_{1}\geq N_{1}
, with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
,

\begin{split}&\mathrm{Diam}_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}\big(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\big)\\
&\quad\leq C_{\varepsilon}\max\bigg\{\left(1+|z_{\alpha}|\right)^{2/(1+2\gamma)}\left(\left(\frac{d\log(n_{1}/d)}{n_{1}}\right)^{1/(1+2\gamma)}+\left(\frac{d\log(n_{2}/d)}{n_{2}}\right)^{1/(1+2\gamma)}\right),\\
&\quad\quad\mathrm{Q}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\mathbf{1}\{\mathrm{Q}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\geq(t^{*})^{1/\gamma}\}\bigg\},\end{split}

provided
\max\{2,C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(1+|z_{\alpha}|)^{2}d\}\leq n_{2}
, where
\mathrm{Q}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}=(1+|z_{\alpha}|)\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|_{2}/t^{*}
and
C_{\varepsilon}
depends on
\varepsilon
,
C_{0}
,
c_{1}

\gamma
,
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
, while
C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
depend on
\varepsilon
,
t^{*}
and
\gamma
.

Under
(B4)
and
(B5)
, Assumption
(A1)
only holds locally, and the width guarantees for both
\alpha=1/2
and
\alpha\neq 1/2
are obtained through
Theorem

15
, with no distinction between the two cases. When the initial estimator is consistent and
\mathrm{Q}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}
is eventually bounded by
(t^{*})^{1/\gamma}
, with high probability, the diameter is determined by the first term, scaling as
\left(d\log(n_{2}/d)/n_{2}\right)^{1/(1+2\gamma)}
, and exhibiting adaptive behavior to the unknown curvature
\gamma
.

Theorem 3.2 of
Mukherjee et al. (2019)
establishes that (
54
) is satisfied by the standard maximum score estimator, and the rate of convergence matches that of the minimax lower bound up to a logarithmic factor (See Theorem 3.4 of
Mukherjee et al. (2019)
). Whenever
n_{1}
is large enough such that
\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|_{2}\leq(t^{*})^{1+1/\gamma}/(1+|z_{\alpha}|)
, with high probability, the diameter is dominated by the first term of
Corollary

24.1
. The balanced split
n_{1}=n_{2}=N/2
minimizes the diameter, yielding the rate
\left(d\log(N/d)/N\right)^{1/(1+2\gamma)}
.

8.4

Quantile without Positive Densities

Consider an IID observation
X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
where the inference of interest is the
\eta
-quantile defined as

\theta(P^{N}):=\inf\left\{t\,:F_{P^{N}}(t)\geq\eta\right\}\quad\text{for}\quad\eta\in(0,1)

and
F_{P^{N}}(t):=\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}(X\leq t)
. It is well-known that
\theta(P^{N})
minimizes the following “quantile” loss:

\theta(P^{N}):=\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}}\,\E_{P^{N}}[\eta(X-\theta)_{+}+(1-\eta)(\theta-X)_{+}].

We work under IID observations as the setting and the forthcoming assumption become unnatural under non-identical observations. The method and the theoretical results do not require IID observations. The sample quantile centered at
\theta(P^{N})
converges to a Gaussian distribution when scaled by
N^{1/2}
if the distribution of
X
has a strictly positive density at
\theta(P^{N})
. If the density at
\theta(P^{N})
is zero or non-existent, however, the sample quantile converges at a rate depending on the Hölder smoothness of the
F_{P^{N}}(t)
in the neighborhood of
\theta(P^{N})
. In this case, the limiting distribution is no longer Gaussian and also depends on the Hölder smoothness of the
F_{P^{N}}(t)
in the neighborhood of
\theta(P^{N})

(
Smirnov, 1952
)
. Although finite-sample valid, distribution-free confidence intervals for quantiles already exist
(
Scheffé and Tukey, 1945
)
, we present this result to illustrate the behavior of the proposed method in irregular settings. We quantity the smoothness of the
F_{P^{N}}(t)
near
\theta(P^{N})
as follows:

(B7)

There exist
\delta_{0}>0
,
M_{0},M_{1}\in(0,\infty)
and
M_{0}>M_{1}
such that

|F_{P^{N}}(\theta)-F_{P^{N}}(\theta(P^{N}))-M_{0}|\theta-\theta(P^{N})|^{\gamma}\mathrm{sgn}(\theta-\theta(P^{N}))|\leq M_{1}|\theta-\theta(P^{N})|^{\gamma}

for all
\theta
such that
|\theta-\theta(P^{N})|\leq\delta_{0}
.

The Hölder smoothness as described in
(B7)
should be compared to
Knight (1998, Equation (6))
. When
\gamma=1
, this assumption becomes equivalent to requiring that the density at the true
\eta
-quantile is bounded away from zero.

Validity.

Theorem 25
.

Assume
(B7)
and define

\begin{split}R_{n_{2}}&=\inf_{\delta_{0}\geq\rho>0}\left\{2\sqrt{\frac{2M_{0}\rho^{\gamma}}{\eta(1-\eta)}}+\mathbb{P}_{P^{1}}(|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|>\rho)\right\}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}\eta(1-\eta)}}.\end{split}

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that the following hold.

1.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
n_{2}\geq 2
,

\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\!\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\right)\;\leq\;\alpha+\min\{1,CR_{n_{2}}\}.

2.

For
\alpha\in(0,1/2]
and
n_{2}\geq 1
, setting
\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}^{2}=\mathfrak{C}n_{2}\min\{|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|^{2\gamma},\delta_{0}^{2\gamma}\}
with
\mathfrak{C}
depending on
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
,

\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,1/2}\right)\leq\min\left\{\E_{P^{1}}\left[1-\Phi(\widetilde{\Delta}_{2})+\frac{C}{(1+\widetilde{\Delta}_{2})^{2}}\right],\;\alpha+\min\{1,CR_{n_{2}}\}\right\}.

Theorem

25
reveals a phase transition in the validity conditions, depending on the data split. Suppose
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
is the sample quantile based on
D_{1}
, satisfying

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P^{1}}(|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|>n_{1}^{-1/(2\gamma)})\leq\varepsilon.

As an illustration, we set
\rho=n_{1}^{-1/(2\gamma)}
in the infimum, assuming that
n_{1}
is large enough so that
\rho\leq\delta_{0}
. Then, the remainder
R_{n_{2}}
is negligible whenever

n_{1}^{1/2}\eta(1-\eta)\to\infty\quad\textrm{and}\quad n_{2}\eta(1-\eta)\to\infty.

The validity requirement is similar to that of the CLT, which precludes the extreme quantile levels
(1-\eta),\eta\to 0
at rate faster than
n_{2}
and
n_{1}^{1/2}
.

For
\alpha\leq 1/2
, validity holds under weaker condition of
\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}\to\infty
, which reduces to

\frac{n_{2}}{n_{1}}\cdot(n_{1}|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|^{2\gamma})\to\infty.

This condition is free of
\eta
. Hence, when
n_{2}\gg n_{1}
, the asymptotic validity holds for any value of
\eta
, including extreme quantile levels. For fixed
\eta
, validity conditions are agnostic to the Hölder smoothness
\gamma
.

Width Analysis.

Theorem 26
.

Assume
(B7)
and let
\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
be as in
(A5)
. For
n_{1}\geq 1
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
and any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
,

\mathrm{Diam}_{|\cdot|}\big(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\big)\leq C_{\varepsilon}\max\bigg\{\left(1+|z_{\alpha}|\right)^{1/\gamma}(n_{2}^{-1/(2\gamma)}+\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}^{1/(1+\gamma)}),\mathrm{Q}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\mathbf{1}\{\mathrm{Q}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\geq\delta_{0}\}\bigg\},

provided
\max\{2,C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(1+|z_{\alpha}|)^{2}\delta_{0}^{2\gamma}\}\leq n_{2}
, where
\mathrm{Q}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}=\delta_{0}^{-\gamma}(1+|z_{\alpha}|)\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
and
C_{\varepsilon}
depends on
M_{0},M_{1}
and
\gamma
, while
C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
only depend on
\varepsilon
.

Corollary 26.1
.

Assume
(B7)
and suppose the initial estimator satisfies for all
n_{1}\geq N_{1}
,

\displaystyle|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|\ =O_{P^{1}}(n_{1}^{-1/(2\gamma)}).

(55)

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
n_{1}\geq N_{1}
, with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
,

\mathrm{Diam}_{|\cdot|}\big(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\big)\leq C_{\varepsilon}\max\bigg\{\left(1+|z_{\alpha}|\right)^{1/\gamma}(n_{2}^{-1/(2\gamma)}+n_{1}^{-1/(2\gamma)}),\mathrm{Q}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\mathbf{1}\{\mathrm{Q}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\geq\delta_{0}\}\bigg\},

provided
\max\{2,C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(1+|z_{\alpha}|)^{2}\delta_{0}^{2\gamma}\}\leq n_{2}
, where
\mathrm{Q}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}=\delta_{0}^{-\gamma}(1+|z_{\alpha}|)|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|
and
C_{\varepsilon}
depends on
M_{0},M_{1}
and
\gamma
, while
C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
only depends on
\varepsilon
.

Under
(B7)
, Assumption
(A1)
only holds locally. The width guarantees are obtained through
Theorem

15
, and there is no distinction between the two cases:
\alpha\geq 1/2
and
\alpha<1/2
. When the initial estimator is consistent and
\mathrm{Q}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}
is eventually bounded by
\delta_{0}
, with high probability, the diameter is determined by the first term, scaling as
n_{2}^{-1/(2\gamma)}
, and exhibiting adaptive behavior to the unknown curvature
\gamma
.

Condition (
55
) is satisfied when
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
is the sample quantile based on
D_{1}
. Whenever
n_{1}
is large enough such that
|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|\leq\delta_{0}^{1+\gamma}/(1+|z_{\alpha}|)
, with high probability, the diameter is dominated by the first term of
Corollary

26.1
. The balanced split
n_{1}=n_{2}=N/2
minimizes the diameter, yielding the rate
(N/2)^{-1/(2\gamma)}
, which matches the estimation rate of the sample quantile under
(B7)
as given by Example 1 of
Knight (1998)
. In the special case
\gamma=1
, i.e., where the density is bounded away from zero at the
\eta
-quantile, the confidence shrinks at the parametric rate
N^{-1/2}
.

8.5

Discrete Argmin Inference

This application is motivated by
Zhang et al. (2024)
, who study a prototypical problem in general model selection. The framework extends naturally to constructing confidence sets that contain the best predictor minimizing the population risk among
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}\}
where
f_{i}
may correspond to a machine learning method trained on the same data; consequently
f_{i}
and
f_{j}
for
i\neq j
can be highly correlated.

Consider independent observations
X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Observations share a common mean vector
\mu=\E_{P_{i}}[X_{i}]
but have heterogeneous covariance matrices
\Sigma_{i}=\mathrm{Cov}_{P_{i}}(X_{i})
and we write
\bar{\Sigma}_{2}=n_{2}^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\Sigma_{i}
. In this problem, the inference of interest is the index set corresponding to the minimum marginal mean:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{*}:=\theta(P^{N})=\argmin_{j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}}\,\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\E_{P_{i}}[e_{j}^{\top}X].

Its complement
\mathcal{S}^{c}=\{1,\ldots,d\}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{*}
denotes the non-argmin index set. Following
Zhang et al. (2024)
, we do not assume that
\mathcal{S}^{*}
is a singleton set, allowing the proposed confidence set to accommodate ties.

Validity.

Theorem 27
.

Denote
\delta_{j,k}=(e_{j}-e_{k})^{\top}\mu
and
D_{i}^{j,k}=(e_{j}-e_{k})^{\top}(X_{i}-\mu)
, and

\sigma^{2}_{j,k}=(e_{j}-e_{k})^{\top}\bar{\Sigma}_{2}(e_{j}-e_{k})=[\bar{\Sigma}_{2}]_{jj}-2[\bar{\Sigma}_{2}]_{jk}+[\bar{\Sigma}_{2}]_{kk},

where
[A]_{jk}
denotes the
(j,k)
th entry of the matrix
A
. Define

R_{n_{2}}=\max_{(j,k)\in\mathcal{S}^{c}\times\mathcal{S}^{*}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{|D_{i}^{j,k}|^{2}}{n_{2}\sigma_{j,k}^{2}}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|D_{i}^{j,k}|}{n_{2}^{1/2}\sigma_{j,k}}\right\}\right].

(56)

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that the following hold.

1.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
n_{2}\geq 2
,

\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\!\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\right)\;\leq\;\alpha+\min\{1,CR_{n_{2}}\}.

2.

For
\alpha\in(0,1/2]
and
n_{2}\geq 1
, setting
\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}^{2}=n_{2}\min_{(j,k)\in\mathcal{S}^{c}\times\mathcal{S}^{*}}\delta_{j,k}^{2}/\sigma_{j,k}^{2}
, it holds

\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\left(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,1/2}\right)\leq\min\left\{\E_{P^{1}}\left[1-\Phi(\widetilde{\Delta}_{2})+\frac{C}{(1+\widetilde{\Delta}_{2})^{2}}\right],\;\alpha+\min\{1,CR_{n_{2}}\}\right\}.

Zhang et al. (2024)
establish validity of their method under the assumption that the smallest eigenvalue of the covariance matrix of
X
is bounded away from zero (See Theorem 3.1 of
Zhang et al. (2024)
). As pointed out in their work, this assumption may be violated in practice when the components of
X
are highly correlated, such as in model selection for LASSO (see Section 6.2 of
Zhang et al. (2024)
).

For any
\alpha\in(0,1)
, validity holds whenever the
L_{n+\delta}
-
L_{2}
moment equivalence holds; see also Theorem 2.1 of
Kim and Ramdas (2025)
, which became available after the initial version of this manuscript. For
\alpha\leq 1/2
, validity holds additionally whenever
\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}\to\infty
, requiring no moment assumptions beyond finite variance. When
\sigma_{j,k}
is bounded away from zero,
\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}\to\infty
holds as long as
\delta_{j,k}\gg n_{2}^{-1/2}
, allowing for shrinking mean gaps. On the other hand,
\sigma_{j,k}\to 0
is favorable for establishing
\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}\to\infty
. This happens, for instance, when
[\bar{\Sigma}_{2}]_{jj}\approx[\bar{\Sigma}_{2}]_{kk}
and the
j
th and
k
th components are strongly positively correlated. In this case the, validity can hold even when
\delta_{j,k}
vanishes faster than
n_{2}^{-1/2}
.

The convergence rate of the diameter does not translate directly to the discrete parameter space. We refer to
Kim and Ramdas (2025)
, which studies the proposed method in the context of testing and provides minimax power analysis.

9

Numerical Illustration

This section provides an empirical illustration of the proposed method for inference in high-dimensional and irregular settings. Manski’s maximum score estimator is used as the primary example, as it captures both dimension-agnostic validity and curvature-adaptivity in a single framework. Additional results for high-dimensional mean estimation, misspecified linear regression, and median estimation without positive density are presented in Section
S.9
.

9.1

Data-generating Distributions and Experimental Setup

For given sample size
N
and dimension
d
, we generate

X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)\quad\textrm{where}\quad\Sigma_{i,j}=0.1^{|i-j|}/d.

(57)

Set
\beta_{0}=(1/\sqrt{d},\ldots,1/\sqrt{d})^{\top}
so
\beta_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Conditional on
X_{i}
, the binary response is generated as

\mathbb{P}(Y_{i}=1\mid X_{i})=\Phi(\mathrm{sgn}(\beta_{0}^{\top}X_{i})\cdot|\beta_{0}^{\top}X_{i}|^{\gamma-1}).

(58)

This data-generating distribution approximately satisfies
(B4)
with parameter
\gamma
. Numerical studies are conducted for
\gamma\in\{1/2,1,2\}
, under which the maximum score estimator is expected to converge at rates,
(d/N)^{1/2},(d/N)^{1/3}
and
(d/N)^{1/5}
respectively, up to logarithmic factors. The proposed CLT-based confidence set is compared against subsampling with estimated rate of convergence
(
Bertail et al., 1999
)
and the nonparametric bootstrap. Although the bootstrap is known to be inconsistent for this problem
(
Sen et al., 2010
)
, it is included as a reference.

Two experimental settings are considered.

(1)

Experiment 1 (low dimension).
Set
d=2
and
N\in\{100,200,\ldots,1000\}
. Two base estimators are compared: Manski’s maximum score estimator (
Manski
) and logistic regression (
Logistic
). The latter is inconsistent due to model misspecification and serves to illustrate the robustness of the proposed method to the choice of initial estimator. Data are split evenly between
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
.

(2)

Experiment 2 (high dimension).
Set
N=200
, and
d\in\{10,\ldots,50\}
. The maximum score estimator is computationally infeasible in this regime; since the proposed method remains valid for any initial estimator, two alternatives are considered: the smoothed maximum score estimator (
SmoothManski
)
(
Horowitz, 1992
)
and penalized logistic regression (
PenLogistic
)
(
Cessie and Houwelingen, 1992
)
. Neither estimator has known limiting distributions under misspecification, to the best of our knowledge. Data are split evenly between
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
.

See
Section

S.9
for omitted implementation details.

9.2

Validity for Irregular and High-dimensional Settings

Experiment 1:

Figure

2
displays the estimated coverage of all confidence sets constructed at the
90\%
nominal level, based on
500
replications for the proposed method and
300
replications for the resampling methods. The
X
-axis shows sample size
N
and the
Y
-axis shows estimated coverage. From left to right, the panels correspond to data-generating distributions with
\gamma=1/2,1
, and
2
.

The proposed CLT-based confidence set achieves coverage near or above
90\%
across all values of
\gamma
and for both initial estimators. In particular, when
\gamma=2
, coverage is closest to the nominal level, consistent with the regime where is numerically close to zero. See
Theorem

11
. On the other hands, the performance of the resampling methods vary. Subsampling with estimated rate achieves near-nominal coverage for
\gamma=1/2
, but undercovers significantly as
\gamma
increases. The bootstrap undercovers for all values of
\gamma
, particularly when a consistent estimator is used. Its reported coverage falls well below
60\%
lies outside the plotted range.

Experiment 2:

Figure

3
displays the estimated coverage of all confidence sets constructed at the
90\%
nominal level. The
X
-axis shows sample dimension
d
, with
N=200
fixed. The proposed CLT-based confidence sets remain valid for
90\%
nominal level, yet it becomes more conservative as dimension of the sample grows. For a fixed sample size and dimension, the coverage becomes closer as
\gamma
increases (from left panel to the right). These are expected behaviors characterized by the behavior of . As for the comparative methods, subsampling with estimated rate of convergence produces extremly conservative sets with
100\%
coverage and bootstrap fails for large dimension. We emphasize that both base estimators,
SmoothManski
or
PenLogistic
, are inconsistent estimators as the maximum score estimator is computational infeasible. Only the proposed methods establish validity completely agnostic to the choice of the estimator.

9.3

Diameter for Irregular and High-dimensional Settings

displays the average diameter of each confidence set over

for resampling methods) with the

-1/2,-1/3
and

for

respectively. The slope for the proposed method is estimated via linear regression and overlaid on each panel. The diameter of the proposed confidence set converges at a rate closely matching the theoretical value in each case, demonstrating curvature-adaptive convergence without prior knowledge of
\gamma
.

and

for

respectively. The slope for the proposed method is estimated via linear regression and overlaid on each panel. We observe that the observed rate of the diameter scales similarly to the theoretical rates, but not as closely matching as
Figure

. This is expected as the size of the confidence sets depend on the convergence rate of the initial estimator, in this case,
SmoothManski
or
PenLogistic
. Neither of them is expected to achieve the same rate as
Manski
is computationally infeasible.

10

Concluding Remarks

This manuscript introduces a general framework for constructing confidence sets for solutions of stochastic optimization problems, rendering empirical risk minimization as special cases. The proposed method employs sample splitting, which facilitates validity across both regular and irregular settings. In particular, the method offers a dimension-agnostic solution, which becomes applicable in high-dimensional problems where standard asymptotic theory breaks down. The manuscript provides a unified treatment of validity, conservativeness, and the diameter of the resulting confidence sets.

The theoretical properties are illustrated through several challenging statistical applications. For high-dimensional and misspecified linear regression, the proposed confidence set achieves dimension-agnostic validity and recovers the
\sqrt{d/N}
rate under weak moment conditions, with a dimensional requirement that appears to be new. For Manski’s maximum score estimator, the confidence set adapts to the unknown margin condition, yielding rates that depend on the Tsybakov noise parameter; a confidence set with this adaptive behavior in high-dimension also appears to be new.

The following are problems where honest inference under weak distributional assumptions is limited or largely absent, and which are closely related to the themes of this manuscript.

•

Generalized linear models.
Extending the framework to high-dimensional generalized linear models, including logistic and Poisson regression.

•

Constrained optimization.
Quantifying convergence rates under general constraints, which likely requires handling the geometry of the feasible set near the solution using tools from variational analysis.

•

Irregular parameter spaces.
Analyzing the problem where the parameter space poses structural challenges, including the space of probability distributions equipped with optimal transport metrics, discrete parameter spaces, and Hadamard spaces for Fréchet mean inference.

•

Nuisance parameters.
Extending the framework to problems where the target is a functional of a higher-dimensional object estimated in a first stage, and where it is of interest to understand whether the elbow effect between parametric and functional rates of convergence can be recovered.

•

Dependence beyond mixing.
Studying dependence structures beyond
\beta
-mixing and martingale differences, such as graphical dependence arising in Ising models or other Markov random fields.

•

Removing sample splitting.
Investigating whether sample splitting can be avoided using tools from algorithmic stability or differential privacy, which provide alternative mechanisms for decoupling estimation and inference.

•

Probabilistic tools.
Developing new probabilistic tools including non-uniform Berry-Esseen bounds, moderate and large deviation inequalities for degenerate U-statistics, and non-central
t-statistics under weak moment conditions.

Each of these directions is independently motivated by the limitations and extensions identified in the present work. Any one of them in isolation, or any combination thereof, represents a research program of both theoretical and practical interest.

Supplement to “Honest Inference for Stochastic Optimization”

S.1

Review of History

This section summarizes the historical developments of the key idea behind the content of this manuscript.

•

Inverting the asymptotic risk of (irregular) estimators to construct confidence sets has a long history. The idea dates back at least to
Stein (1981, Equation 8.11)
, who foreshadowed the possibility of the inference for a shrinkage estimator of the multivariate Gaussian mean in large dimensions.

•

Later,
Li (1989)
applied a similar risk inversion framework to nonparametric regression with Gaussian errors and introduced the concept of “honest” confidence sets, which subsequently sparked developments in adaptive nonparametric inference.
Beran (1996, Theorem 3.1)
and
Beran and Dümbgen (1998, Theorem 3.1)
extended these ideas, explicitly crediting
Stein (1981)
, and proposed the inversion of the asymptotic normality based on the central limit theorem (CLT), which they call modulation of estimators. Here, sample-splitting was not considered.

•

Parallel developments in stochastic programming analyzed the risk of constrained optimization problems. Early references include
Shapiro (1989, Theorem 3.2)
and
Geyer (1994, Theorem 4.4)
, both of whom leveraged the CLT to establish asymptotic distributions. Confidence set construction in this setting was explicitely mentioned by
Geyer (1996)
. The asymptotic behavior of the risk under general loss functions and constraints was studied in great generality by
Pflug (1991)
;
Pflug (1995)
;
Pflug (2003)
. Here, as well, sample splitting was not considered.

•

Robins and van der Vaart (2006)
were among the first to combine sample-splitting with with risk inversion based on the CLT.
