\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{M_{1}|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|^{\gamma}+M_{0}|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|^{\gamma}}{\eta(1-\eta)}\leq\frac{2M_{0}|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|^{\gamma}}{\eta(1-\eta)}

where we used
(B7)
and the fact that
M_{0}>M_{1}
. We can repeat the identical argument for
\widehat{\theta}_{1}<\theta(P^{N})
. Thus the requirement of
Lemma

32
holds with

\displaystyle\varphi(|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|)=\frac{2M_{0}|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|^{\gamma}}{\eta(1-\eta)}.

By
Lemma

32
, we conclude

\displaystyle R_{n_{2}}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\delta_{0}\geq\delta>0}\left\{2\sqrt{\frac{2M_{0}\delta^{\gamma}}{\eta(1-\eta)}}+\mathbb{P}_{P^{1}}(|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|>\delta)\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{|\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}\leq\theta(P^{N})\}-\eta|^{2}}{n_{2}\eta(1-\eta)}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}\leq\theta(P^{N})\}-\eta|}{\sqrt{n_{2}\eta(1-\eta)}}\right\}\right].

Furthermore, observe that

\displaystyle\E_{P_{i}}|\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}\leq\theta(P^{N})\}-\eta|^{3}=(1-\eta)^{3}\eta+\eta^{3}(1-\eta),

and hence we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{|\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}\leq\theta(P^{N})\}-\eta|^{2}}{n_{2}\eta(1-\eta)}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}\leq\theta(P^{N})\}-\eta|}{\sqrt{n_{2}\eta(1-\eta)}}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{(1-\eta)^{3}\eta+\eta^{3}(1-\eta)}{\eta(1-\eta)\sqrt{n_{2}\eta(1-\eta)}}=\frac{(1-\eta)^{2}+\eta^{2}}{\sqrt{n_{2}\eta(1-\eta)}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}\eta(1-\eta)}}.

This concludes the result by invoking
Lemma

32
.

Next, we establish the result for
\alpha=1/2
as an application of
Theorem

3
. The proof is split into two cases: (1)
|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|\leq\delta_{0}
and (2)
|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|>\delta_{0}
where
\delta_{0}>0
is defined in
(B7)
. First we consider the case (1). We have shown that

\displaystyle\E_{P_{i}}[m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}}-m_{\theta(P^{N})}|D_{1}]

\displaystyle=\E_{P_{i}}[(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-X)\mathbf{1}\{\theta(P^{N})<X\leq\widehat{\theta}_{1}\}|D_{1}]

\displaystyle\quad+\E_{P_{i}}[(X-\widehat{\theta}_{1})\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{\theta}_{1}<X\leq\theta(P^{N})\}|D_{1}].

We observe that
(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-X)\mathbf{1}\{\theta(P^{N})<X\leq\widehat{\theta}_{1}\}
is a non-negative random variable, taking values from
0
to
\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})
. Then

\displaystyle(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-X)\mathbf{1}\{\theta(P^{N})<X\leq\widehat{\theta}_{1}\}

\displaystyle\quad\geq\frac{1}{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}))\mathbf{1}\left\{\theta(P^{N})<X\leq\theta(P^{N})+\frac{1}{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}))\right\}

and thus taking expectations both sides,

\displaystyle\E_{P_{i}}[(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-X)\mathbf{1}\{\theta(P^{N})<X\leq\widehat{\theta}_{1}\}|D_{1}]

\displaystyle\quad\geq\frac{1}{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}))\left\{F\left(\theta(P^{N})+\frac{1}{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}))\right)-F(\theta(P^{N}))\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\geq\frac{1}{2^{1+\gamma}}M_{0}|\theta-\theta(P^{N})|^{1+\gamma}-M_{1}\frac{1}{2^{1+\gamma}}|\theta-\theta(P^{N})|^{1+\gamma}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{M_{0}-M_{1}}{2^{1+\gamma}}|\theta-\theta(P^{N})|^{1+\gamma}

By the assumption that
M_{0}>M_{1}
, we can conclude that

\displaystyle\E_{P_{i}}[m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}}-m_{\theta(P^{N})}|D_{1}]\geq\mathfrak{C}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}))^{1+\gamma}

where
\mathfrak{C}
depends on
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
.
The case with
\theta(P^{N})>\widehat{\theta}_{1}
is analogous and omitted.

Next, we consider the case where
|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|>\delta_{0}
. For any
\widehat{\theta}_{1}=\theta(P^{N})+\ell u
such that
u\in\{-1,1\}
and
\ell>\delta_{0}
, we define
\bar{\theta}=\theta(P^{N})+\ell u
. Since
\bar{\theta}=(1-\delta/\ell)\theta(P^{N})+\delta/\ell\widehat{\theta}_{1}
, it follows by the convexity of
\theta\mapsto\E_{P_{i}}[m_{\theta}]
,

\displaystyle(1-\delta/\ell)\E_{P_{i}}[m_{\theta(P^{N})}]-\delta/\ell\E_{P_{i}}[m_{\theta}]\geq\E_{P_{i}}[m_{\bar{\theta}}]

\displaystyle\quad\Longleftrightarrow\E_{P_{i}}[m_{\theta}]-\E_{P_{i}}[m_{\theta(P^{N})}]\geq(\ell/\delta)(\E_{P_{i}}[m_{\bar{\theta}}]-\E_{P_{i}}[m_{\theta(P^{N})}])\geq\mathfrak{C}|\theta-\theta(P^{N})|\delta^{\gamma}.

Putting together, we have established that

\displaystyle\E_{P_{i}}[m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}}-m_{\theta(P^{N})}|D_{1}]\geq\mathfrak{C}|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|\min\{|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|^{\gamma},\delta^{\gamma}\}.

We have also shown that

\displaystyle m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})}

\displaystyle=(\theta(P^{N})-\theta)(\eta-\mathbf{1}\{X\leq\theta(P^{N})\})+(X-\theta)\mathbf{1}\{\theta<X\leq\theta(P^{N})\}

\displaystyle\quad+(\theta-\theta(P^{N}))(\mathbf{1}\{X\leq\theta(P^{N})\}-\eta)+(\theta-X)\mathbf{1}\{\theta(P^{N})<X\leq\theta\},

and this function is Lipschitz in the sense that

\displaystyle|m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}}-m_{\theta(P^{N})}|\leq\{\max\{\eta,1-\eta\}+1\}|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{P_{i}}[\widehat{\xi}_{i}|D_{1}]\leq\E_{P_{i}}[|m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}}-m_{\theta(P^{N})}|^{2}|D_{1}]\leq\{\max\{\eta,1-\eta\}+1\}^{2}|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|^{2}.

Putting together, we obtain

\ratio{}^{2}=\frac{(\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\E_{P_{i}}[m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}}-m_{\theta(P^{N})}|D_{1}])^{2}}{\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathrm{Var}_{P_{i}}[\widehat{\xi}_{i}|D_{1}]}\geq\mathfrak{C}n_{2}\min\{|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|^{2\gamma},\delta_{0}^{2\gamma}\}=\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}^{2}.

Finally, the remainder term in
Theorem

3
becomes

\displaystyle\E_{P^{1}}\left[\min\left\{1,C\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|^{2}}{n_{2}^{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}(1+\ratio)^{2}}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|}{n_{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}^{1/2}(1+\ratio)}\right\}\bigg|D_{1}\right]\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\leq\E_{P^{1}}\left[\min\left\{1,\frac{C}{(1+\widetilde{\Delta}_{2})^{2}}\right\}\right].

This concludes the claim.
∎

Proof of Theorem
26
.

The proof is a direct application of
Theorem

30
and thus proceeds by verifying
(A1)
,
(A2)
, and
(A4)
to hold locally
\rho=\delta_{0}
where
\delta_{0}
is defined in
(B7)
, and
(A1-global)
,
(A2-ratio)
and
(A4-ratio)
to globally.

Verifying
(A1)

From the proof of
Theorem

25
, we have established that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\E_{P_{i}}[m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}}-m_{\theta(P^{N})}|D_{1}]\geq\mathfrak{C}|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|\min\{|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|^{\gamma},\delta_{0}^{\gamma}\},

where
\mathfrak{C}
is a constant depending on
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
. Thus
(A1)
holds with
\gamma
and
c_{0}=\mathfrak{C}
when
|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|\leq\delta_{0}
.

Verifying
(A2)

To control this term, we observe that

\displaystyle|m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})}|\leq\{\max\{\eta,1-\eta\}+1\}|\theta-\theta(P^{N})|\leq 2|\theta-\theta(P^{N})|,

that is, the function is Lipschitz in parameter. We define the following collection of “localized” functions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\delta}:=\left\{m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})}\text{ for all }\theta\text{ s.t., }|\theta-\theta(P^{N})|\leq\delta\right\}.

Then using the notation
\mathbb{G}_{n_{2}}
, defined in (
E.15
), we have

\displaystyle\E^{*}_{P^{2}}\left[\sup_{|\theta-\theta(P^{N})|<\delta}|(\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}-\mathbb{M}_{2})(\theta)-(\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}-\mathbb{M}_{2})(\theta(P^{N}))|\right]

\displaystyle\quad=n_{2}^{-1/2}\E^{*}_{P^{2}}\left[\sup_{|\theta-\theta(P^{N})|<\delta}|\mathbb{G}_{n_{2}}(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})|\right]=n_{2}^{-1/2}\E^{*}_{P^{2}}\left[\sup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}}\left|\mathbb{G}_{n_{2}}m\right|\right].

The last object can be related to the
\varepsilon
-bracketing numbers. Two functions
\ell
and
u
are defined to be an
\varepsilon
-bracket of functions
m\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}
if
l(x)\leq m(x)\leq u(x)
for all
x
and
\|\ell-u\|\leq\varepsilon
. The bracketing number
\mathcal{N}_{[\,]}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{M}_{\delta},\|\cdot\|)
is the minimum number of
\varepsilon
-brackets required for covering
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}
. Then by Theorem 2.14.2 of
van der Vaart and Wellner (1996)
, we have

\displaystyle\E^{*}_{P^{2}}\left[\sup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}}\left|\mathbb{G}_{n_{2}}m\right|\right]\lesssim(\E_{P^{2}}M^{2})^{1/2}\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{1+\log\mathcal{N}_{[\,]}(\varepsilon\|M\|_{P^{2},2},\mathcal{M}_{\delta},L_{2}(P^{2}))}\,d\varepsilon

where the envelop function can be taken as
M=2\delta
, and the Lipschtiz constant is
2
. Furthermore, Theorem 2.7.11 of
van der Vaart and Wellner (1996)
shows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{[\,]}(\varepsilon\|M\|_{P,2},\mathcal{M}_{\delta},L_{2}(P))\leq\mathcal{N}(\delta\varepsilon/4,\Theta_{\delta},|\cdot|)\lesssim 1/\varepsilon

where
\Theta_{\delta}=[-\delta,\delta]
. Evaluating the integral, we have

\displaystyle\E_{P^{2}}\left[\sup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}}\left|\mathbb{G}_{n_{2}}m\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq C\delta\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{1+\log(1/\varepsilon)}\,d\varepsilon\leq C\delta+C\delta\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log(1/\varepsilon)}\,d\varepsilon\leq C\delta,

where
C
is a universal constant that changes line by line. We thus conclude that
\phi_{n_{2}}(\delta)=C\delta/\sqrt{n_{2}}
. Hence the requirement
q<1+\gamma
is satisfied with
q=1
globally.

Verifying
(A4)

Using the Lipschitz continuity (in parameter) of
m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})}
, it follows,

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathtt{pop}}^{2}(\delta)=\sup_{|\theta-\theta(P^{N})|\leq\delta}\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\E_{P^{2}}[|m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})}|^{2}]\leq 4\delta^{2}.

To control
\omega_{n_{2},\mathtt{emp}}
, we employ
Lemma

33
.
By
Lemma

33
with
q=2
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{P^{2}}^{*}\left[\sup_{\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|<\delta}\left|\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})^{2}]\right|\right]

\displaystyle\quad\leq 64\delta^{2}+16\cdot 8^{1/2}\,n_{2}^{1/2}\delta\,\phi_{n_{2}}(\delta)\leq C\delta^{2},

for some universal constant
C
. Hence the requirement
q<1+\gamma
is satisfied with
q=1
globally.

Verifying
(A1-global)

Setting
\delta>\delta_{0}
, we have shown that

\displaystyle C_{\delta_{0}}(|\theta-\theta(P^{N})|)=\delta_{0}^{\gamma}|\theta-\theta(P^{N})|.

Verifying
(A2-ratio)

For any
\delta>\delta_{0}
,

\displaystyle\E^{*}_{P^{2}}\left[\sup_{\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|>\delta}\left|\frac{(\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}-\mathbb{M}_{2})(\theta)-(\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}-\mathbb{M}_{2})(\theta(P^{N}))}{\mathbb{M}_{2}(\theta)-\mathbb{M}_{2}(\theta(P^{N}))}\right|\right]\leq\frac{C}{\delta_{0}^{\gamma}\sqrt{n_{2}}}=R(n_{2},\delta_{0}),

where
C
is a universal constant. Hence,
(A2-ratio)
holds with
C_{\mathtt{ratio}}=1/\varepsilon_{\mathtt{ratio}}
by Markov’s inequality.

Verifying
(A4-ratio)

Similarly for any
\delta>\delta_{0}
,

\displaystyle\E_{P^{2}}^{*}\left[\sup_{\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|>\delta_{0}}\frac{z_{\alpha}^{2}}{n_{2}^{2}}\left|\frac{\sum_{i\in I_{2}}(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})^{2}]}{\{\mathbb{M}_{2}(\theta)-\mathbb{M}_{2}(\theta(P^{N}))\}^{2}}\right|\right]\leq\frac{(1+z_{\alpha}^{2})C}{\delta_{0}^{2\gamma}n_{2}},

and

\displaystyle\sup_{\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|>\delta_{0}}\frac{z_{\alpha}^{2}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})^{2}(Z_{i})]}{n_{2}^{2}\{\M_{2}(\theta)-\M_{2}(\theta(P^{N}))\}^{2}}\leq\frac{(1+z_{\alpha}^{2})C}{\delta_{0}^{2\gamma}n_{2}}.

Hence we can set

\displaystyle S_{\mathtt{emp}}(n_{2},\delta_{0},\alpha)=S_{\mathtt{pop}}(n_{2},\delta_{0},\alpha)=\frac{(1+z_{\alpha}^{2})C}{\delta_{0}^{2\gamma}n_{2}},

and
(A4-ratio)
holds with
\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{emp}}=1/\varepsilon_{\mathtt{emp}}
by Markov’s inequality.

We assume that
n_{2}
is large enough satisfies the following:

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon^{\circ}}\max\{R(n_{2},\rho),S_{\mathtt{emp}}(n_{2},\rho,\alpha),S_{\mathtt{pop}}(n_{2},\rho,\alpha)\}\leq 1/3,

(E.17)

where
C_{\varepsilon^{\circ}}
is a constant depending on
\varepsilon^{\circ}
. Then (
E.17
) is satisfied for
n_{2}
large such that
C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon^{\circ}}(1+|z_{\alpha}|)^{2}\delta_{0}^{2\gamma}\leq n_{2}
.

Evaluating the rate of convergence

We now evaluate the rate of convergence by applying
Theorem

13
and
Theorem

30
. Denote by
\mathfrak{C}
a depending on
M_{0},M_{1}
and
\gamma
that changes from line to line. For
\delta\leq\delta_{0}
,

\displaystyle r_{n_{2}}^{-2}\phi_{n_{2}}(\mathfrak{C}r_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)})\leq 1\Leftrightarrow\frac{\mathfrak{C}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\leq r_{n_{2}}^{2\gamma/(1+\gamma)}\Leftrightarrow\frac{\mathfrak{C}}{n_{2}^{1/(2\gamma)}}\leq r_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)},

\displaystyle u_{n_{2}}^{-4}\omega^{2}_{\mathtt{pop}}(\mathfrak{C}u_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)})\leq n_{2}\Leftrightarrow\mathfrak{C}n_{2}^{-1}\leq u_{n_{2}}^{\mathfrak{C}\gamma/(1+\gamma)}\Leftrightarrow\frac{4}{n_{2}^{1/(2\gamma)}}\leq u_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)},\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle u_{n_{2}}^{-4}\omega^{2}_{n_{2},\mathtt{emp}}(\mathfrak{C}u_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)})\leq n_{2}\Leftrightarrow\mathfrak{C}n_{2}^{-1}\leq u_{n_{2}}^{4\gamma/(1+\gamma)}\Leftrightarrow\frac{\mathfrak{C}}{n_{2}^{1/(2\gamma)}}\leq u_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)}.

Hence we have
\mathrm{R}_{N}^{\mathtt{CLT}}=\mathfrak{C}(n_{2}^{-1/(2\gamma)}+\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}^{1/(1+\gamma)})
where
\mathfrak{C}
depends on
M_{0},M_{1}
and
\gamma
.

Next, since
C_{\delta_{0}}(|\theta-\theta(P^{N})|)=\delta_{0}^{\gamma}|\theta-\theta(P^{N})|
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}=C^{-1}_{\delta_{0}}((1+|z_{\alpha}|)\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}})=\delta_{0}^{-\gamma}(1+|z_{\alpha}|)\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}.

This concludes the claim in view of
Theorem

30
.

∎

For
Corollary

26.1
, we instead prove the slightly rephrased version of the corollary.

Corollary 40.2
.

Suppose the initial estimator satisfies for all
n_{1}\geq N_{1}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P^{1}}\left(|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|\leq\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{init}}n_{1}^{-1/(2\gamma)}\right)\geq 1-\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\mathtt{init}}.

Assume
(B7)
. For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1-\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\mathtt{init}})
, setting
\varepsilon^{\circ}=\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\mathtt{init}}
,
n_{1}\geq N_{1}
, with probability at least
1-\varepsilon^{\circ}
,

\mathrm{Diam}_{|\cdot|}\big(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\big)\leq C_{\varepsilon^{\circ}}\max\bigg\{\left(1+|z_{\alpha}|\right)^{1/\gamma}(n_{2}^{-1/(2\gamma)}+n_{1}^{-1/(2\gamma)}),\mathrm{Q}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\mathbf{1}\{\mathrm{Q}^{\mathtt{CLT}}_{N,\alpha}\geq\delta_{0}\}\bigg\},

provided
\max\{2,C_{\varepsilon^{\circ}}^{\prime}(1+|z_{\alpha}|)^{2}\delta_{0}^{2\gamma}\}\leq n_{2}
, where
\mathrm{Q}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}=\delta_{0}^{-\gamma}(1+|z_{\alpha}|)|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|
and
C_{\varepsilon^{\circ}}
depends on
M_{0},M_{1}
and
\gamma
, while
C_{\varepsilon^{\circ}}^{\prime}
only depends on
{\varepsilon^{\circ}}
.

Proof of
Corollary

40.2
.

When
|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|\leq\delta_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P^{2}|P^{1}}\left[\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\widehat{\xi}_{i}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P^{2}|P^{1}}\left[|m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}}(X_{i})-m_{\theta(P^{N})}(X_{i})|^{2}|D_{1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n_{2}}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}))^{2}\mathbb{P}_{P^{1}}(\theta(P^{N})<X\leq\widehat{\theta}_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n_{2}}(M_{1}+M_{0})(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}))^{2+\gamma},

and

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{C}}_{2}^{2}\leq(M_{1}+M_{0})^{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}))^{2+2\gamma}.

Consider the event

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathtt{init}}:=\left\{|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|\leq\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{init}}n_{1}^{-1/(2\gamma)}\right\}.

Then on this event and
n_{1}
sufficiently large such that
\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{init}}n_{1}^{-1/\gamma}\leq\delta_{0}
,
(A5)
holds with

\displaystyle\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}^{2}=n_{2}^{-(1+\gamma)/(2\gamma)}n_{1}^{-(1+\gamma)/(2\gamma)}+n_{1}^{-(2+2\gamma)/(2\gamma)}

since

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P^{2}|P^{1}}\left[\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\widehat{\xi}_{i}^{2}\right]+\widehat{\mathbb{C}}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|}{n_{2}}(M_{1}+M_{0})|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|^{1+\gamma}+(M_{1}+M_{0})^{2}|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|^{2+2\gamma}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{init}}^{2+\gamma}}{n_{2}^{1+1/(2\gamma)}}(M_{1}+M_{0})n_{1}^{-(1+\gamma)/(2\gamma)}+\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{init}}^{2+2\gamma}(M_{1}+M_{0})^{2}n_{1}^{-(2+2\gamma)/(2\gamma)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\max\{\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{init}}^{2+\gamma}(M_{1}+M_{0}),\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{init}}^{2+2\gamma}(M_{1}+M_{0})^{2}\}\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}^{2}.

with probability greater than
1-\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\mathtt{init}}
. In particular, we used the fact that

\displaystyle n_{2}^{-1-1/(2\gamma)}=n_{2}^{-(2\gamma+1)/(2\gamma)}\leq n_{2}^{-(\gamma+1)/(2\gamma)}.

Then the result follows with

\displaystyle n_{2}^{-1/(2\gamma)}+\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}^{1/(1+\gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq n_{2}^{-1/(2\gamma)}+n_{2}^{-1/2(2\gamma)}n_{1}^{-1/2(2\gamma)}+n_{1}^{-1/(2\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim n_{2}^{-1/(2\gamma)}+n_{1}^{-2/(2\gamma)}

by AM-GM inequality. When
|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|\geq\delta_{0}
, we can instead use

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P^{2}|P^{1}}\left[\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\widehat{\xi}_{i}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P^{2}|P^{1}}\left[|m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}}(X_{i})-m_{\theta(P^{N})}(X_{i})|^{2}|D_{1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n_{2}}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}))^{2}\mathbb{P}_{P^{1}}(\theta(P^{N})<X\leq\widehat{\theta}_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{n_{2}}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}))^{2},

and

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{C}}_{2}^{2}\leq 4(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}))^{2},

where we simply used
\mathbb{P}_{P^{1}}(\theta(P^{N})<X\leq\widehat{\theta}_{1})\leq 1
. Hence we have

\displaystyle C_{\delta_{0}}^{-1}((1+|z_{\alpha}|)\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}})=\frac{4(1+|z_{\alpha}|)}{\delta_{0}^{\gamma}}|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})|=\mathrm{Q}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}.

Plugging them into the final expression of
Theorem

26
concludes the claim.
∎

S.7.5

Discrete Argmin Inference

Proof of
Theorem

27
.

The proof is an application of
Theorem

3
for
\alpha=1/2
and
Theorem

8
for
\alpha\neq 1/2
.

For both cases, we observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}})

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}\,\cap\,\{\widehat{\theta}_{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{*}\})+\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}\,\cap\,\{\widehat{\theta}_{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{c}\})

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}\,\cap\,\{\widehat{\theta}_{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{c}\}),

since
\widehat{\theta}_{1}\in\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}
almost surely and by the convention (
3
). Hence throughout, we assume that
\widehat{\theta}_{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{c}
. Observe that for any
k\in\mathcal{S}^{*}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi}_{i}=(e_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}}-e_{k})^{\top}(X_{i}-\mu)=D_{i}^{\widehat{\theta}_{1},k}

and

\displaystyle n_{2}^{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathrm{Var}_{P_{i}}[\widehat{\xi}_{i}]=\sum_{i\in I_{2}}(e_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}}-e_{k})^{\top}\Sigma_{i}(e_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}}-e_{k})=n_{2}\sigma^{2}_{\widehat{\theta}_{1},k}.

Then, the upper bound in
Theorem

8
becomes

\displaystyle\E_{P^{1}}\left[\min\left\{1,C\,\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{|D_{i}^{\widehat{\theta}_{1},k}|^{2}}{n_{2}\sigma_{\widehat{\theta}_{1},k}^{2}}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|D_{i}^{\widehat{\theta}_{1},k}|}{n_{2}^{1/2}\sigma_{\widehat{\theta}_{1},k}}\right\}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{\theta}_{1}\notin\mathcal{S}^{*}\}\bigg|D_{1}\right]\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\leq\min\left\{1,\sup_{(j,k)\in\mathcal{S}^{c}\times\mathcal{S}^{*}}C\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{|D_{i}^{j,k}|^{2}}{n_{2}\sigma_{j,k}^{2}}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|D_{i}^{j,k}|}{n_{2}^{1/2}\sigma_{j,k}}\right\}\right]\right\}.

Next, we consider the case for
\alpha=1/2
. Again, assuming that
\{\widehat{\theta}_{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{c}\}
, and for any
j,k\in\mathcal{S}^{c}\times\mathcal{S}^{*}

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{C}^{2}_{2}(j)}{\mathbb{V}_{2}(j)}=\frac{n_{2}\delta_{j,k}^{2}}{\sigma_{j,k}^{2}}\geq\min_{(j,k)\in\mathcal{S}^{c}\times\mathcal{S}^{*}}\frac{n_{2}\delta_{j,k}^{2}}{\sigma_{j,k}^{2}}=\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}^{2}\quad\text{where}\quad\delta_{j,k}=e_{j}^{\top}\mu-e_{k}^{\top}\mu.

Hence
Theorem

3
implies,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}(\theta(P^{N})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}})\leq 1-\Phi(\widetilde{\Delta}_{2})+\min\left\{1,\frac{C}{(1+\widetilde{\Delta}_{2})^{2}}\right\},

under no moment assumptions beyond finite variance.
∎

S.7.6

Auxiliary Results for Statistical Applications

S.7.6.1

Results for Validity

Proof of
Lemma

32
.

Define random variable

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}=\min\left\{1,C\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|^{2}}{n_{2}^{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|}{n_{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}^{1/2}}\right\}\bigg|D_{1}\right]\right\}.

Since
\Delta_{1}\leq 1
, we can safely introduce the following indicator function, for any
0\leq\delta\leq\delta_{0}
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}\leq\Delta_{1}\mathbf{1}\{{\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|<\delta}\}+\mathbf{1}\{{\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|\geq\delta}\}.

We also define

\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{H}=\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\E_{P_{i}}[\langle u,G_{i}\rangle^{2}].

Then, first term can be controlled as

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|^{2}}{n_{2}^{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|}{n_{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}^{1/2}}\right\}\bigg|D_{1}\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|^{2}}{n_{2}^{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|}{n_{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}^{1/2}}\right\}\bigg|D_{1}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{|\langle\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}),G_{i}\rangle|^{2}}{\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{H}}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|\langle\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}),G_{i}\rangle|}{\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{H}^{1/2}}\right\}\bigg|D_{1}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{|\langle\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}),G_{i}\rangle|^{2}}{\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{H}}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|\langle\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}),G_{i}\rangle|}{\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{H}^{1/2}}\right\}\bigg|D_{1}\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{|\langle\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}),G_{i}\rangle|^{2}}{\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{H}}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|\langle\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N}),G_{i}\rangle|}{\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{H}^{1/2}}\right\}\bigg|D_{1}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+5\varphi(\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|)+10\sqrt{\varphi(\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|)},

where the last step follows from
Lemma

41
. Since it is assumed that
\|\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta(P^{N})\|<\delta
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{|\langle u,G_{i}\rangle|^{2}}{{\mathbb{V}}_{H}}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|\langle u,G_{i}\rangle|}{{\mathbb{V}}_{H}^{1/2}}\right\}\bigg|D_{1}\right]+5\varphi(\delta)+10\sqrt{\varphi(\delta)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sup_{u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\,\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{|\langle u,G_{i}\rangle|^{2}}{{\mathbb{V}}_{H}}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|\langle u,G_{i}\rangle|}{{\mathbb{V}}_{H}^{1/2}}\right\}\right]+5\varphi(\delta)+10\sqrt{\varphi(\delta)},

where
\mathbb{V}_{G}=\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\E_{P_{i}}[\langle u,G_{i}\rangle^{2}]
. The result follows by taking expectation over
P^{1}
and taking infimum over
0\leq\delta\leq\delta_{0}
. In particular, when taken as the minimum between one, we have
\varphi(\delta)\leq\sqrt{\varphi(\delta)}
, thus only
\sqrt{\varphi(\delta)}
appears in the bound.
∎

Lemma 41
.

Suppose
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
and
\{Y\}_{i=1}^{N}
are independent but not identically distributed random variables. Denote
V_{X}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\E_{P_{i}}[X_{i}^{2}]
and

V_{Y}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\E_{P_{i}}[Y_{i}^{2}]

such that
\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[|X_{i}-Y_{i}|^{2}]\leq C\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[X_{i}^{2}]
for all
1\leq i\leq N
. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{X_{i}^{2}}{V_{X}}\min\left\{\frac{|X_{i}|}{\sqrt{V_{X}}},1\right\}\right]-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{Y_{i}^{2}}{V_{Y}}\min\left\{\frac{|Y_{i}|}{\sqrt{V_{Y}}},1\right\}\right]\leq 5C+10C^{1/2}.

Proof of
Lemma

41
.

Suppose
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
and
\{Y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
satisfy
\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[|X_{i}-Y_{i}|^{2}]\leq C\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[X_{i}^{2}]
for all
1\leq i\leq N
. We observe

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\left|\frac{X_{i}^{2}}{V_{X}}-\frac{Y_{i}^{2}}{V_{Y}}\right|\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\left|\frac{X_{i}^{2}V_{Y}-Y_{i}^{2}V_{X}}{V_{X}V_{Y}}\right|\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\left|\frac{X_{i}^{2}V_{Y}-Y_{i}^{2}V_{X}-Y_{i}^{2}V_{Y}+Y_{i}^{2}V_{Y}}{V_{X}V_{Y}}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\left|\frac{(X_{i}^{2}-Y_{i}^{2})V_{Y}}{V_{X}V_{Y}}\right|\right]+\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\left|\frac{Y_{i}^{2}(V_{Y}-V_{X})}{V_{X}V_{Y}}\right|\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\left|\frac{(X_{i}^{2}-Y_{i}^{2})}{V_{X}}\right|\right]+\left|\frac{\sum_{i=1}\E_{P_{i}}(Y_{i}^{2}-X_{i}^{2})}{V_{X}}\right|\frac{\E_{P_{i}}[Y_{i}^{2}]}{V_{Y}}.

Summing over
i
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\left|\frac{X_{i}^{2}}{V_{X}}-\frac{Y_{i}^{2}}{V_{Y}}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\left|\frac{(X_{i}^{2}-Y_{i}^{2})}{V_{X}}\right|\right]+\left|\frac{\sum_{i=1}\E_{P_{i}}(Y_{i}^{2}-X_{i}^{2})}{V_{X}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\frac{|X_{i}^{2}-Y_{i}^{2}|}{V_{X}}\right]

by Jensen’s inequality. Since
Y^{2}=(Y-X)^{2}+2X(Y-X)+X^{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[|Y_{i}^{2}-X_{i}^{2}|]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[|Y_{i}-X_{i}|^{2}]+2\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[X_{i}(Y_{i}-X_{i})]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[|Y_{i}-X_{i}|^{2}]+2\left(\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[X_{i}^{2}]\right)^{1/2}\left(\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[|Y_{i}-X_{i}|^{2}]\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[X_{i}^{2}]+2C^{1/2}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[X_{i}^{2}]

by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Hence we conclude

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}\left[\left|\frac{X_{i}^{2}}{V_{X}}-\frac{Y_{i}^{2}}{V_{Y}}\right|\right]\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\frac{(C+2C^{1/2})\mathbb{E}_{P_{i}}[X_{i}^{2}]}{V_{X}}\right]\leq 2(C+2C^{1/2}).

Define a function
g:\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\displaystyle g(x)=x\min\{\sqrt{x},1\}.

We claim that this function is Lipschitz. When
x,y\geq 1
,

\displaystyle|g(x)-g(y)|=|x-y|.

Next, when
x,y<1
,

\displaystyle|g(x)-g(y)|=|x^{3/2}-y^{3/2}|=\frac{3}{2}\xi^{1/2}|x-y|,

by the mean value theorem and
\min(x,y)\leq\xi\leq\max(x,y)
. Since
x,y\leq 1
, we conclude
|g(x)-g(y)|=\frac{3}{2}|x-y|
. Finally, for
x\geq 1,y<1
,

\displaystyle|g(x)-g(y)|=|x-y^{3/2}|\leq|x-1|+|1-y^{3/2}|\leq|x-y|+\frac{3}{2}|x-y|=\frac{5}{2}|x-y|.

The case with
y\geq 1,x<1
is analogous. Hence
g
is
5/2
-Lipschitz. This implies that

\displaystyle\frac{X_{i}^{2}}{V_{X}}\min\left\{\frac{|X_{i}|}{\sqrt{V_{X}}},1\right\}-\frac{Y_{i}^{2}}{V_{Y}}\min\left\{\frac{|Y_{i}|}{\sqrt{V_{Y}}},1\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left|g\left(\frac{X_{i}^{2}}{V_{X}}\right)-g\left(\frac{Y_{i}^{2}}{V_{Y}}\right)\right|\leq\frac{5}{2}\left|\frac{X_{i}^{2}}{V_{X}}-\frac{Y_{i}^{2}}{V_{Y}}\right|.

We conclude the result by taking the expectation both sides, summing over
1\leq i\leq N
and applying the first result.
∎

S.7.6.2

Results for width analysis

Proof of Lemma
33
.

Define a sequence of independent Rademacher random variables. Then, we have

\displaystyle\E^{*}_{P^{2}}\left[\sup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}}\,|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P^{2})m^{2}|\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2n_{2}^{-1}\E^{*}_{P^{2}\times\varepsilon}\left[\sup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}}\,\left|\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\epsilon_{i}m^{2}(Z_{i})\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2n_{2}^{-1}\E^{*}_{P^{2}\times\varepsilon}\left[\sup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}}\,\left|\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\epsilon_{i}m^{2}(Z_{i})\mathbf{1}\{M_{\delta}>B\}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\quad+2n_{2}^{-1}\E^{*}_{P^{2}\times\varepsilon}\left[\sup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}}\,\left|\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\epsilon_{i}m^{2}(Z_{i})\mathbf{1}\{M_{\delta}\leq B\}\right|\right].

where the second inequality follows from symmetrization (see for instance, Lemma 2.3.1 of
van der Vaart and Wellner (1996)
). We now handle two terms separately. For the unbounded part, we have

\displaystyle\E^{*}_{P^{2}\times\varepsilon}\left[\sup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}}\,\left|\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\epsilon_{i}m^{2}(Z_{i})\mathbf{1}\{M_{\delta}>B\}\right|\right]\leq\E_{P^{2}}\left[\left|\sum_{i\in I_{2}}M_{\delta}^{2}(Z_{i})\mathbf{1}\{M_{\delta}>B\}\right|\right].

We apply the Hoffmann-J
\o
rgensen inequality (See Proposition 6.8 of
Ledoux and Talagrand (2013)
with
p=1
), which states

\displaystyle\E_{P^{2}}\left[\left|\sum_{i\in I_{2}}M_{\delta}^{2}(Z_{i})\mathbf{1}\{M_{\delta}>B\}\right|\right]\leq 8\left(\mathbb{E}_{P^{2}}\left[\max_{i\in I_{2}}M_{\delta}^{2}(Z_{i})\right]+t_{0}^{2}\right)

for any
t_{0}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P^{2}}\left(\sum_{i\in I_{2}}M_{\delta}^{2}(Z_{i})\mathbf{1}\{M_{\delta}>B\}>t_{0}\right)\leq 1/8.

One can take
t_{0}=0
as long as the following satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P^{2}}\left(\sum_{i\in I_{2}}M_{\delta}^{2}(Z_{i})\mathbf{1}\{M_{\delta}>B\}>0\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{P^{2}}\left(\max_{i\in I_{2}}M_{\delta}(Z_{i})>B\right)\leq 1/8.

(E.18)

Under (
E.18
), which to be verified late, the Hoffmann-J
\o
rgensen inequality implies

\displaystyle 2n_{2}^{-1}\E^{*}_{P^{2}\times\varepsilon}\left[\sup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}}\,\left|\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\epsilon_{i}m^{2}(Z_{i})\mathbf{1}\{M_{\delta}>B\}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq 16n_{2}^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{P^{2}}\left[\max_{i\in I_{2}}|M_{\delta}(Z_{i})|^{2}\right].

For the second term, we observe that the entire process is uniformly bounded by
B
. We can thus apply the contraction inequality, such as, Theorem 4.12 of
Ledoux and Talagrand (2013)
or Corollary 3.2.2 of
Giné and Nickl (2021)
. This in tern implies that

\displaystyle 2n_{2}^{-1}\E^{*}_{P^{2}}\left[\sup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}}\,\left|\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\epsilon_{i}m^{2}(Z_{i})\mathbf{1}\{M_{\delta}\leq B\}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\quad\leq 4Bn_{2}^{-1}\E^{*}_{P^{2}}\left[\sup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}}\,\left|\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\epsilon_{i}m(Z_{i})\right|\right]

\displaystyle\quad\leq 8Bn_{2}^{-1}\E^{*}_{P^{2}}\left[\sup_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}}\,\left|\sum_{i\in I_{2}}m(Z_{i})-\E_{P^{2}}[m(Z_{i})]\right|\right]

\displaystyle\quad\leq 8B\phi_{n_{2}}(\delta),

where the first inequality is by the symmetrization and the second by desymmetrization. Now it remains to verify (
E.18
).

Finite
q
th moment

When
\E_{P^{2}}[|M_{\delta}|^{q}]\leq C_{q}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P^{2}}\left(\max_{i\in I_{2}}M_{\delta}(Z_{i})>B\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{P}_{P^{2}}\left(\max_{i\in I_{2}}M^{q}_{\delta}(Z_{i})>B^{q}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{P^{2}}\left(\sum_{i\in I_{2}}M^{q}_{\delta}(Z_{i})>B^{q}\right)\leq\frac{n_{2}\E_{P^{2}}[M_{\delta}^{q}]}{B^{q}}.

In this case, we set
B^{q}=8n_{2}\E_{P^{2}}[M_{\delta}^{q}]
. The final bound is

\displaystyle 16n_{2}^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{P^{2}}\left[\max_{i\in I_{2}}|M_{\delta}(Z_{i})|^{2}\right]+8B\phi_{n_{2}}(\delta)

\displaystyle\quad\leq 16n_{2}^{-1}\left(\mathbb{E}_{P^{2}}\left[\sum_{i\in I_{2}}|M_{\delta}(Z_{i})|^{2\cdot q/2}\right]\right)^{2/q}+8\cdot 8^{1/q}\cdot n_{2}^{1/q}C_{q}^{1/q}\phi_{n_{2}}(\delta)

\displaystyle\quad\leq 16n_{2}^{-1+2/q}C_{q}^{2/q}+8\cdot 8^{1/q}\cdot n_{2}^{1/q}C_{q}^{1/q}\phi_{n_{2}}(\delta).

Finite
\gamma
Sub-Weibull

In this case, we have
\E_{P^{2}}[|M_{\delta}|^{q}]\leq K^{q}q^{q/\gamma}
for all
q\geq 1
. Then the final bound becomes

\displaystyle 16n_{2}^{-1+2/q}K^{2}q^{2/\gamma}+8\cdot 8^{1/q}\cdot n_{2}^{1/q}Kq^{1/\gamma}\phi_{n_{2}}(\delta).

The second contraction term is dominated when
n_{2}^{1/q}q^{1/\gamma}
is minimized. We choose
q_{*}=(\gamma\log n_{2})>2
, which requires
n_{2}>e^{2/\gamma}
. This choice yields

\displaystyle 16n_{2}^{-1+2/q_{*}}K^{2}q_{*}^{2/\gamma}+8\cdot 8^{1/q_{*}}\cdot n_{2}^{1/q_{*}}Kq_{*}^{1/\gamma}\phi_{n_{2}}(\delta)

\displaystyle\quad=16n_{2}^{-1+2/(\gamma\log n_{2})}K^{2}(\gamma\log n_{2})^{2/\gamma}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+8\cdot 8^{1/(\gamma\log n_{2})}\cdot n_{2}^{1/(\gamma\log n_{2})}K(\gamma\log n_{2})^{1/\gamma}\phi_{n_{2}}(\delta)

\displaystyle\quad\leq 16n_{2}^{-1}e^{2/\gamma}K^{2}(\gamma\log n_{2})^{2/\gamma}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+8\cdot 2\cdot e^{1/\gamma}K(\gamma\log n_{2})^{1/\gamma}\phi_{n_{2}}(\delta)

where
8^{1/x}\leq 2
for
x>2
.
∎

S.8

Miscellaneous Derivation from Examples

S.8.1

Derivation for
Example

1

Let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N}
be IID observations following
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
. We split data into two parts so that
|I_{1}|+|I_{2}|=n_{1}+n_{2}=N
. Recall that

\theta(P^{N})=\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}}\,\E_{P^{2}}[(Z_{1}Z_{2}-\theta)^{2}],

and

\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\theta)={n_{2}\choose 2}^{-1}\sum_{n_{1}+1\leq i<j\leq N}(Z_{i}Z_{j}-\theta)^{2}.

First, the curvature follows as

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}_{P^{2}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\E_{P^{2}}[(Z_{1}Z_{2}-\theta)^{2}]-\E_{P^{2}}[(Z_{1}Z_{2}-\mu^{2})^{2}]

\displaystyle=\E_{P^{2}}[(Z_{1}Z_{2}-\mu^{2}+\mu^{2}-\theta)^{2}]-\E_{P^{2}}[(Z_{1}Z_{2}-\mu^{2})^{2}]

\displaystyle=2(\mu^{2}-\theta)^{2}\E_{P^{2}}[Z_{1}Z_{2}]-2\mu^{2}(\mu^{2}-\theta)^{2}+(\theta-\mu^{2})^{2}

\displaystyle=(\theta-\mu^{2})^{2}.

Since
\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
is an unbiased estimator, the MSE is given by variance. It then follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{P^{2}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Var}_{P^{2}}\left[{n_{2}\choose 2}^{-1}\sum_{n_{1}+1\leq i<j\leq N}(Z_{i}Z_{j}-\theta)^{2}-(Z_{i}Z_{j}-\mu^{2})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=4(\mu^{2}-\theta)^{2}\mathrm{Var}_{P^{2}}\left[{n_{2}\choose 2}^{-1}\sum_{n_{1}+1\leq i<j\leq N}Z_{i}Z_{j}\right].

The variance of U-statistics with degree-2 Kernel is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{P^{2}}\left[{n_{2}\choose 2}^{-1}\sum_{n_{1}+1\leq i<j\leq N}Z_{i}Z_{j}\right]=\frac{2}{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}[\zeta_{2}+2(n_{2}-2)\zeta_{1}]

where
\zeta_{1}=\mathrm{Cov}(Z_{1}Z_{2},Z_{1}Z_{3})
and
\zeta_{2}=\mathrm{Var}(Z_{1}Z_{2})
. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}(Z_{1}Z_{2},Z_{1}Z_{3})=\E[Z_{1}^{2}]\E[Z_{2}]\E[Z_{3}]-\mu^{4}=\mu^{2}(\sigma^{2}+\mu^{2})-\mu^{4}=\mu^{2}\sigma^{2},

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(Z_{1}Z_{2})=\E[Z_{1}^{2}]\E[Z_{2}^{2}]-\mu^{4}=(\sigma^{2}+\mu^{2})^{2}-\mu^{4}=\sigma^{4}+2\sigma^{2}\mu^{2}.

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{P^{2}}(\theta)=(\mu^{2}-\theta)^{2}\left(\frac{8\sigma^{4}}{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}+\frac{16\mu^{2}\sigma^{2}}{n_{2}}\right).

We conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}_{P^{2}}^{2}(\theta)/\mathbb{V}_{P^{2}}(\theta)=(\mu^{2}-\theta)^{2}\left(\frac{8\sigma^{4}}{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}+\frac{16\mu^{2}\sigma^{2}}{n_{2}}\right)^{-1}.

The result for the constant estimator can be obtained by plugging in
\theta=c
to the display above. Next, we consider the standard U-statistics estimator based on
D_{1}
, that is,

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta}_{1}={n_{1}\choose 2}^{-1}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n_{1}}Z_{i}Z_{j}=\frac{1}{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}Z_{i}\right)^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}Z_{i}^{2}\right].

(E.19)

Denote sample mean and variance as

\displaystyle\overline{Z}_{n_{1}}=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}Z_{i}\quad\mbox{and}\quad S_{n_{1}}^{2}=\frac{1}{n_{1}-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\left(Z_{i}-\overline{Z}_{n_{1}}\right)^{2}.

Then we can write

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}Z_{i}\right)^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}Z_{i}^{2}=n_{1}(n_{1}-1)\overline{Z}_{n_{1}}^{2}-(n_{1}-1)S_{n_{1}}^{2}

and hence

\displaystyle\mu^{2}-\widehat{\theta}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mu^{2}-\overline{Z}_{n_{1}}^{2}+\frac{S_{n_{1}}^{2}}{n_{1}}

\displaystyle=(\mu-\overline{Z}_{n_{1}})(\mu+\overline{Z}_{n_{1}})+\frac{S_{n_{1}}^{2}}{n_{1}}

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}-\frac{2Z\sigma}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}-\frac{Z^{2}\sigma^{2}}{n_{1}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}V}{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)}=\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\left(-2Z-\frac{Z^{2}\sigma}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}+\frac{\sigma V}{\sqrt{n_{1}}(n_{1}-1)}\right)

where
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and
V\sim\chi^{2}_{n-1}
, with
Z
and
V
independent. Putting together, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}_{P^{2}}^{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1})/\mathbb{V}_{P^{2}}(\widehat{\theta}_{1})\overset{d}{=}\left(-2Z-\frac{Z^{2}\sigma}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}+\frac{\sigma V}{\sqrt{n_{1}}(n_{1}-1)}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{8n_{1}\sigma^{2}}{n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}+\frac{16n_{1}\mu^{2}}{n_{2}}\right)^{-1}.

S.8.2

Numerical study for
Example

1

Below, we provide a numerical result to demonstrate how the behavior of the estimator affects the miscoverage probability of the proposed confidence set. We generate
200
observations
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{200}
from independent
\mathcal{N}(\mu,1)
, where
\mu
varies over the grid
\{0.01,0.002,\ldots,0.5\}
. The first
100
observations are used to construct three estimators: (1) the constant estimator at zero, denoting
\widehat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}
; (2) the U-statistics estimator (
E.19
), denoting
\widehat{\theta}_{1}^{(2)}
. For each estimator and value of
\mu
, we estimate, based on
1000
replications, the probability

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\widehat{\M}_{2}(\mu)-\widehat{\M}_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}^{(k)})>0\right)\quad\text{for $k\in\{1,2\}$},

where

\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\theta)={100\choose 2}^{-1}\sum_{101\leq i<j\leq 2}(Z_{i}Z_{j}-\theta)^{2}.

For each estimator and value of
\mu
, we can compute the distribution of exactly. We then compute the upper bound of miscoverage,
\E[1/(1+\ratio^{2})]
, by numerical integration. The results are displayed in
Figure

1(a)
. The
X
-axis shows the values of true mean
\mu
and the observed miscoverage based on
1000
replication is shown in red. The theoretical upper bound of
Theorem

3
is provided in dashed line. We observe that
Theorem

3
is valid but a conservative upper bound of the miscoverage. In particular,
Theorem

3
confirms that constant estimator at zero yields asymptotically conservative confidence set with miscoverage of zero as the value of
\mu
increases. This is reflected in diverging . For the estimator based on U-statistics,
Theorem

3
provides a conservative bound such that the miscoverage should be less than
\approx 80\%
but in practice the miscoverage can be less than
50\%
.

S.8.3

Derivation for
Example

2

Again, let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N}
be IID observations following
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
. We split data evenly such that
|D_{1}|=n_{1}
and
|D_{2}|=n_{2}
. Recall

\theta(P^{N})=\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}}\,\E_{P^{2}}[(Z_{1}-\theta)^{2}],

and

\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}(\theta)=\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{n_{1}+1\leq i\leq N}(Z_{i}-\theta)^{2}.

It is straightforward to show that

\ratio{}^{2}=\frac{n_{2}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\mu)^{2}}{4\sigma^{2}}=\frac{n_{1}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\mu)^{2}}{4\sigma^{2}}\cdot\frac{n_{2}}{n_{1}}.

From this, the behavior of the constant estimator is immediate. For sample mean,

\ratio{}^{2}=\frac{n_{1}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\mu)^{2}}{4\sigma^{2}}\cdot\frac{n_{2}}{n_{1}}\overset{d}{=}\frac{Z^{2}}{4}\cdot\frac{n_{2}}{n_{1}}\quad\textrm{where}\quad Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1).

Now consider Hodge’s estimator based on
D_{1}
such that
\widehat{\theta}_{1}:=\bar{X}_{n_{1}}\mathbf{1}\{|\bar{X}_{n_{1}}|\geq{n_{1}}^{-1/4}\}
where
\bar{X}_{n_{1}}={n_{1}}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}X_{i}
. Then

\displaystyle{n_{1}}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\mu)^{2}={n_{1}}(\bar{X}_{n_{1}}-\mu)^{2}\mathbf{1}\{|\bar{X}_{n_{1}}|\geq{n_{1}}^{-1/4}\}+{n_{1}}\mu^{2}\mathbf{1}\{|\bar{X}_{n_{1}}|<{n_{1}}^{-1/4}\}.

When
\mu=0
, we have

\displaystyle{n_{1}}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\mu)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{{n_{1}}}\bar{X}_{n_{1}}}{\sigma}\right)^{2}\mathbf{1}\left\{\left|\frac{\sqrt{{n_{1}}}\bar{X}_{n_{1}}}{\sigma}\right|\geq\sigma^{-1}{n_{1}}^{1/4}\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}\sigma^{2}Z^{2}\mathbf{1}\left\{|Z|\geq\sigma^{-1}{n_{1}}^{1/4}\right\}\overset{p}{\to}0,

where
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
On the other hands, take
\mu={n_{1}}^{-1/4}/2
. Then

\displaystyle{n_{1}}(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\mu)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sigma^{2}\left(\sqrt{{n_{1}}}\frac{(\bar{X}_{n_{1}}-\mu)}{\sigma}\right)^{2}\mathbf{1}\left\{\sqrt{{n_{1}}}\frac{(\bar{X}_{n_{1}}-\mu)}{\sigma}\geq\frac{{n_{1}}^{1/4}}{2\sigma}\,\cup\,\sqrt{{n_{1}}}\frac{(\bar{X}_{n_{1}}-\mu)}{\sigma}\leq\frac{-3{n_{1}}^{1/4}}{2\sigma}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{\sqrt{{n_{1}}}}{4}\mathbf{1}\left\{\sqrt{{n_{1}}}\frac{(\bar{X}_{n_{1}}-\mu)}{\sigma}<\frac{{n_{1}}^{1/4}}{2\sigma}\,\cap\,\sqrt{{n_{1}}}\frac{(\bar{X}_{n_{1}}-\mu)}{\sigma}>\frac{-3{n_{1}}^{1/4}}{2\sigma}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{d}{=}\sigma^{2}Z^{2}\mathbf{1}\left\{Z\geq\frac{{n_{1}}^{1/4}}{2\sigma}\,\cup\,Z\leq\frac{-3{n_{1}}^{1/4}}{2\sigma}\right\}+\frac{\sqrt{{n_{1}}}}{4}\mathbf{1}\left\{Z<\frac{{n_{1}}^{1/4}}{2\sigma}\,\cap\,Z>\frac{-3{n_{1}}^{1/4}}{2\sigma}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{p}{\to}\infty,

where
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. In general for any
\mu
, Hodges’ estimator yields

2

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}\frac{n_{2}}{4n_{1}}\bigg(Z^{2}\mathbf{1}\left\{Z\geq\frac{n_{1}^{1/4}-n_{1}^{1/2}\mu}{\sigma}\,\cup\,Z\leq\frac{-n_{1}^{1/4}-n_{1}^{1/2}\mu}{\sigma}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{n_{1}\mu^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\mathbf{1}\left\{\frac{-n_{1}^{1/4}-n_{1}^{1/2}\mu}{\sigma}<Z<\frac{n_{1}^{1/4}-n_{1}^{1/2}\mu}{\sigma}\right\}\bigg)

S.8.4

Derivation for
Example

3

Consider the same setting as
Example

2
. Then

\begin{split}\widehat{\xi}_{i}&=(Z_{i}-\widehat{\theta}_{1})^{2}-(Z_{i}-\mu)^{2}-(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\mu)^{2}=2(Z_{i}-\mu)(\mu-\widehat{\theta}_{1}),\\
\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}&=4n_{2}(\mu-\widehat{\theta}_{1})^{2}\sigma^{2},\quad\textrm{and}\quad|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|/\widehat{\mathbb{V}}^{1/2}_{2}=\frac{|Z_{i}-\mu|}{\sqrt{n_{2}}\sigma}.\end{split}

The remainder term of
Theorem

3
can be bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P^{N}}\left[\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\frac{|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|^{2}}{n_{2}^{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}(1+\widehat{\mathbb{C}}_{2}/\widehat{\mathbb{V}}^{1/2}_{2})^{2}}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|}{n_{2}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{2}^{1/2}(1+\widehat{\mathbb{C}}_{2}/\widehat{\mathbb{V}}^{1/2}_{2})}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\mathbb{E}_{P^{N}}\left[\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\frac{|\widehat{\xi}_{i}|^{3}}{n_{2}^{3}\widehat{\mathbb{V}}^{3/2}_{2}(1+\widehat{\mathbb{C}}_{2}/\widehat{\mathbb{V}}^{1/2}_{2})^{3}}\right]=\frac{\E[|Z_{1}-\mu|^{3}]}{n_{2}^{1/2}\sigma^{3/2}(1+\ratio)^{3}}=2\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{\sigma^{3/2}}{n_{2}^{1/2}(1+\ratio)^{3}}.

S.8.5

Numerical study for
Example

3

Below, we provide a numerical result to demonstrate how the behavior of the estimator affects the miscoverage probability of the proposed confidence set. We consider the following setting. First, we generate
200
observations
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{200}
from independent
\mathcal{N}(\mu,1)
, where
\mu
varies over the grid
\{0.01,0.002,\ldots,0.5\}
. The first
100
observations are used to construct three estimators: (1) the constant estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}=0
; (2) the sample mean estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{1}^{(2)}=\overline{Z}=(100)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{100}Z_{i}
; (3) Hodges’ estimator
\widehat{\theta}^{(3)}_{1}=\overline{Z}\mathbf{1}\{|\overline{Z}|\geq 100^{-1/4}\}
.

For each estimator, we estimates the probability

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{100}\sum_{i=101}^{200}(Z_{i}-\mu)^{2}-(Z_{i}-\widehat{\theta}_{1}^{(k)})^{2}>0\right)\quad\text{for $k\in\{1,2,3\}$}

using
1000
replications. This probability corresponds to the miscoverage probability of the confidence set (
8
) when the respective estimator is used. The results are shown in
Figure

1(b)
.

The simulation displays how closely the upper bound
Theorem

3
tracks the observed miscoverage. First, the miscoverage does not exceed
1/2
(indicated by the dashed line) for any estimator across all values of
\mu
, hence the confidence set is valid at level
1/2
, which is evident from the fact that
\ratio\geq 0
almost surely. The upper bound
\E[1-\Phi(\ratio)]
gives sharper control on the miscoverage. When is close to zero, the coverage is close to
1/2
, or the exact nominal level. This is observed for a constant estimator or Hodges’ estimator near
\mu=0
where
\ratio\approx 0
. For these estimators, there are regions where the corresponding confidence set is more conservative than the set based on the sample mean estimator, for instance, the constant estimator for
\mu>0
or Hodges’ estimator near
\mu=100^{-1/4}\approx 0.31
. Finally, the sample mean estimator yields miscoverage that is approximately constant in
\mu
as the behavior does not depend on
\mu
.

S.9

Additional Numerical
Results

S.9.1

High-dimensional Mean Inference

The setup follows from
Section

8.1
. For
N=300
and
2\leq d\leq 200
, observations
X_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
1\leq i\leq N
are generated independently as

X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)\quad\textrm{where}\quad\Sigma_{i,j}=0.1^{|i-j|}.

(E.20)

Five methods are compared. The first method,
Wald
, is based on the asymptotic distribution of the sample mean, with confidence set

\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{Wald},\mathtt{Mean}}_{N,\alpha}:=\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:(\theta-\bar{X}_{N})^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{N}^{-1}(\theta-\bar{X}_{N})\leq N^{-1}\chi^{2}_{d,\alpha}\right\},

(E.21)

where
\bar{X}_{N}
is the sample mean and
\widehat{\Sigma}_{N}

is the sample covariance matrix. Two proposed methods use an even split of the data:
CLT
, based on (
27
), and
CLT+UCB
, which combines (
27
) with the upper confidence bound derived in
Example

5
as in (
44
). These are compared against nonparametric bootstrap (
Bootstrap
) and multiplier bootstrap (
Multiplier
); see Section
S.9.5
for implementation details.

Validity

Figure

A.1
displays the empirical coverage of
90\%
and
50\%
confidence sets. The
X
-axis displays the dimension
d
and the
Y
-axis displays the estimated coverage over
500
replications, with
N=300
fixed. The
50\%
nominal level corresponds to (
8
) and (
43
), for which no variance estimation is required. For both levels, the coverage of the
Wald
method deteriorates rapidly as
d
increases. The proposed CLT-based methods maintain validity across all dimensions examined. The improvement from incorporating the upper confidence bound is substantial:
CLT+UCB
achieves near-nominal coverage across all dimensions, whereas the coverage of
CLT
approaches one as
d
increases. This conservativeness of
CLT
is captured by
Theorem

3
, which identifies large as the mechanism driving the coverage toward one in high dimensions. Both bootstrap methods achieve near-nominal coverage with slight conservativeness at
\alpha=0.1
when
d
is large.

:
Estimated coverage of five confidence set methods for high-dimensional mean, targeted at the

and
50\%
nominal levels. The

-axis displays the dimension
d
and the
Y
-axis displays the estimated coverage over
500
replications, with

fixed. The methods compared are the Wald interval (

), the CLT-based proposed method (

), the CLT-based method with upper confidence bound (

), nonparametric bootstrap (

), and multiplier bootstrap (
Multiplier
). The coverage of
Wald
deteriorates rapidly as
d
increases. Both
CLT
and
CLT+UCB
maintain validity across all dimensions. The
CLT+UCB
method in particular achieves near-nominal coverage throughout, while the coverage of
CLT
approaches one as
d
increases. Both bootstrap methods achieve near-nominal coverage with slight conservativeness at
\alpha=0.1
and large
d
.

:
Average ratio of radii of each confidence set relative to the
Wald
interval for high-dimensional mean, computed over
500
replications with
N=300
fixed. The
X
-axis displays the dimension

and the
Y
-axis displays the average radius ratio. For
\alpha=1
, the proposed methods use the three-way data split of
Section

7
. The
CLT
method produces the largest sets, with radii more than twice those of
Wald
. Incorporating the upper confidence bound substantially reduces the size:
CLT+UCB
achieves radii approximately
1.5
times those of
Wald
at
\alpha=0.1
and is considerably closer in size at
\alpha=0.5
. At
\alpha=0.5
, the methods
CLT+UCB
,
Bootstrap
, and
Multiplier
are comparable in size.

Width Analysis

We next compare the size of the proposed confidence sets against the
Wald
interval. As established in
Theorem

16
, all proposed confidence sets are Euclidean balls and can be computed in closed form. Since all methods yield

d
-dimensional balls, size comparison reduces to comparing radii, which corresponds to the
d
th root of the volume ratio:

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(B_{d}(c_{1};r_{1}))}{\mathrm{Vol}(B_{d}(c_{2};r_{2}))}\right)^{1/d}=r_{1}/r_{2}.

Figure

A.2
the average ratio of radii relative to
Wald
, computed over
500
replications across different dimensions. The
X
-axis displays the dimension
d
and the
Y
-axis displays the average radius ratio. For
\alpha=0.1
, we use the three-way data split procedure of
Section

7
, which provides the closed-form expression for the corresponding confidence sets.

The
CLT
method yields the largest sets, with radii on average more than twice those of
Wald
. Incorporating the upper confidence bound substantially reduces the size:
CLT+UCB
achieves radii approximately
1.5
times those of
Wald
at
\alpha=0.1
, and is considerably closer in size at
\alpha=1/2
. The bootstrap methods (
Bootstrap
and
Multiplier
) produce smaller confidence sets, and at
\alpha=1/2
all three methods
CLT+UCB
,
Bootstrap
, and
Multiplier
are comparable in size. For high-dimensional mean inference, the bootstrap achieves both valid coverage and competitive size. A practical advantage of
CLT+UCB
over the bootstrap procedures is that the confidence set has an exact closed-form expression given by
Theorem

16
, requiring only a single pass through the data rather than repeated resampling.

Finally, we provide a qualitative illustration of different confidence sets by visualizing the confidence regions in a bivariate setting. We generate
X_{1},\ldots,X_{100}
from a bivariate normal distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
where

\displaystyle\Sigma^{2}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0.3\\
0.3&1\end{bmatrix}.

Figure

A.3
displays the confidence regions three methods defined in Section
9
for confidence levels of
95\%
,
85\%
, and
75\%
. This visualization highlights key qualitative differences between the methods. As shown, the proposed confidence sets (middle and right panels) are non-convex, whereas the Wald interval (left panel) forms an ellipse. Additionally, the proposed methods yield slightly enlarged confidence regions. A close inspection of the confidence set presented in the right panel reveals that it coincides with the adaptive confidence sets by
Robins and van der Vaart (2006)
and dimension-agnostic confidence sets based on cross U-statistics proposed by
Kim and Ramdas (2024)
in their Appendix D (See the left panel of their Figure 3). For mean estimation, these methods become identical.

Figure A.3
:
An illustration of confidence sets for the bivariate mean
\theta=(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})^{\top}
, where the true parameter corresponds to
\theta(P)=(0,0)^{\top}
. Three confidence sets are shown at confidence levels of
95\%
,
85\%
, and
75\%
. The confidence set based on the asymptotic distribution (left) yields an elliptical region while the proposed confidence sets (middle and right) are non-convex.

S.9.2

High-dimensional linear regression

The setup follows from
Section

8.2
. For given sample size
N
and
d
, observations
(X_{i},Y_{i})
are generated independently as,

X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)\quad\textrm{where}\quad\Sigma_{i,j}=0.1^{|i-j|},

(E.22)

with
\beta_{0}=(1/\sqrt{d},\ldots,1/\sqrt{d})^{\top}
and heteroskedastic errors

Y_{i}=\beta_{0}^{\top}X_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\quad\textrm{where}\quad\varepsilon_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,|\beta_{0}^{\top}x|+0.5).

(E.23)

Seven methods are compared. The first method,
Wald
, is based on the asymptotic distributions of the ordinary least square with sandwich variance estimator:

\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{Wald},\mathtt{LR}}_{N,\alpha}:=\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:(\theta-\theta_{\mathtt{OLS}})^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{N}^{-1}(\theta-\theta_{\mathtt{OLS}})\leq N^{-1}\chi^{2}_{d,\alpha}\right\},

(E.24)

where
\mathtt{OLS}
is ordinary least square and
\widehat{\Sigma}_{N}
is the sandwich variance estimator. Two proposed methods use an even split of the data:
CLT
, based on (
27
), and
CLT+UCB
, which combines (
27
) with the upper confidence bound derived in
Example

6
as in (
44
). These are compared against nonparametric bootstrap (
Bootstrap
), multiplier bootstrap (
Multiplier
), wild bootstrap (
Wild
) and residual bootstrap (
Resid
); see Section
S.9.5
for implementation details. Confidence sets are constructed at levels
\alpha\in\{0.1,0.5\}
.

Validity

Figure

A.4
displays the empirical coverage of
90\%
and
50\%
confidence sets over
500
replications. The
X
-axis displays the dimension
d\in\{10,\ldots,120\}
. The
Y
-axis displays the estimated coverage over
500
replications, with
N=300
fixed. As in the mean inference setting,
Wald
suffers from severe undercoverage as
d
increases. Both proposed methods maintain validity across all dimensions examined. The coverage of
CLT
approaches one as
d
increases, reflecting the resulf of
Theorem

3
. The
CLT+UCB
method stays closer to the nominal level but becomes moderately conservative at extreme dimensions, coming from overestimation of the variance term as discussed by
Takatsu (2025)
. At
\alpha=1/2
, where no variance estimation is required, the coverage of
CLT+UCB
remains at the nominal level across all dimensions. In contrast to the mean inference setting, aslmost all bootstrap variants undercover as
d
increases, though less severely than
Wald
. The exception is
Bootstrap
, which remains above the nominal level; for both
\alpha=1/2

and
\alpha=0.1
, it is more conservative than
CLT+UCB
but less conservative than
CLT
.

Width Analysis

We next compare the size of the proposed confidence sets against the
Wald
interval. As establish in
Theorem

17
, all proposed confidence sets are
d
-dimensional ellipsoids with respect to the
\widehat{\Gamma}
-norm. For two ellipsoids with semi-axes,
\bar{r}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{d})
and
\bar{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{d})
, size comparison reduces to the geometric mean of the axes ratio, which equals the
d
th root of the volume ratio:

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(\mathcal{E}_{d}(c_{1};\bar{r}))}{\mathrm{Vol}(\mathcal{E}_{d}(c_{2};\bar{s}))}\right)^{1/d}=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{d}r_{i}\right)^{1/d}/\left(\prod_{i=1}^{d}s_{i}\right)^{1/d}.

Since bootstrap procedures yield
d
-dimensional balls rather than ellipsoids, their radius is compared against the geometric mean of the semi-axes of the
Wald
ellipsoid. For
\alpha=0.1
, the three-way data split of
Section

7
is used. For
CLT
and
CLT+UCB
, volume ratios are not reported for
d>100
, though the method itself remains valid since the initial estimator can incorporate ridge regularization.

Results are displayed in
Figure

A.5
. The
CLT
method yields the largest sets across all dimensions and both levels. The bootstrap variants
Wild
,
Multiplier
, and
Resid
produce smaller confidence sets, but, they fail to achieve nominal coverage and so the size comparison is not meaningful. The comparison between
Bootstrap
and
CLT+UCB
is more informative, as both achieve valid coverage. At
\alpha=0.1
, where the three-way split is used,
CLT+UCB
is larger than
Bootstrap
; at
\alpha=1/2
, where no variance estimation is required,
CLT+UCB
yields a smaller set than
Bootstrap
. As with the mean inference setting, a practical advantage of
CLT+UCB
over all bootstrap procedures is that Theorem
17
provides an exact closed-form expression for the confidence set, requiring no resampling.

Figure A.4
:
Estimated coverage of seven confidence set methods for high-dimensional linear regression under heteroskedastic errors, targeted at the

and

-axis displays the dimension
d\in\{10,\ldots,120\}
and the

-axis displays the estimated coverage over

replications, with

fixed. The methods compared are the Wald interval with sandwich variance estimator (

), the CLT-based proposed method (

), the CLT-based method with upper confidence bound (

), nonparametric bootstrap (

), multiplier bootstrap (

), wild bootstrap (

), and residual bootstrap (
Resid
). The
Wald
method undercovers severely as
d
increases. Both proposed methods maintain validity across all dimensions;
CLT
becomes increasingly conservative as
d
grows, while
CLT+UCB
remains close to the nominal level, with moderate conservativeness at extreme dimensions due to variance overestimation. At
\alpha=0.5
, where no variance estimation is required,
CLT+UCB
achieves coverage at the nominal level across all dimensions. All bootstrap variants undercover as
d
increases, with the exception of
Bootstrap
, which remains above the nominal level and is more conservative than
CLT+UCB
but less conservative than
CLT
.

for high-dimensional linear regression computed over

and the

d
-th root of the volume ratio.For
\alpha=0.1
, the proposed methods use the three-way data split of
Section

7
. The
CLT
method produces the largest sets. Among valid methods,
CLT+UCB
is larger than
Bootstrap
at
\alpha=0.1
but smaller at
\alpha=0.5
. The proposed methods
CLT
and
