admit exact closed-form expressions by
Theorem

17
and require no resampling.

S.9.3

Manski’s Discrete Choice Model

Diameter estimation for high-dimension

We provide the approximation algorithm employed in the numerical study to estimate the diameter of the confidence set for Manski’s problem. The algorithm is agnostic to the choice of confidence set among (
8
), (
6
) or their improvements considered in
Section

6
. We denote a generic confidence set by
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}
. The key properties we use are (1) for Manski’s problem,
\theta(P^{N})\in\mathbb{S}^{d}
and (2) for all confidence sets in this manuscript, the initial estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
is always contained in the set.

The general idea is as follows. First, draw a random vector
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and project onto the tangent space at
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
:

v=\frac{u-(u^{\top}\widehat{\theta}_{1})\widehat{\theta}_{1}}{\|u-(u^{\top}\widehat{\theta}_{1})\widehat{\theta}_{1}\|}.

(E.25)

Then, define a geodesic through
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
in the direction
v
,

\gamma(\phi)=\cos(\phi)\widehat{\theta}_{1}+\sin(\phi)v\quad\textrm{where}\quad\phi\in[-2\pi,2\pi].

(E.26)

A univariate bisection search finds the largest angle
\phi^{*}
such that
\gamma(\phi^{*})\in\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}
. We repeat this procedure via Gram-Schmidt, which generates an orthonormal basis of the tangent space at
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
with
v
as the first component. We keep monitoring the maximum
\phi^{*}
as we search across all directions. this is repeated until
\phi^{*}
stabilizes. The diameter is estimated as then
2\phi^{*}
.

We do not perform a formal analysis of this algorithm. We expect that when
X
is close to isotropic,
d-1
evaluations suffice, as the confidence set is approximately a spherical cap and the boundary distance is nearly uniform across directions. With a more ill-conditioned
X
, additional repetitions may be needed. This procedure is well-suited to the confidence sets in this manuscript as it is fast to compute membership
\gamma(\phi)\in\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}
.

S.9.4

Quantile without Positive Densities

We report numerical results for median inference under non-standard rates of convergence. For a given sample size
N
, IID observations are generated as

X_{i}=\mathrm{sgn}(Z_{i})\cdot|Z_{i}|^{1/(\gamma+1)}\quad\textrm{where}\quad Z_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1).

(E.27)

The distribution of
X_{i}
has a flat density near zero when
\gamma>0
so the sample median converges at rate
N^{-1/(2\gamma+2)}
. We consider
\gamma\in\{0,1/2,1\}
, corresponding to rates
N^{-1/2},N^{-1/3}
, and
N^{-1/4}
, respectively.

Validity

Varying sample size over
N\in\{100,200,\ldots,1000\}
is analyzed during the numerical study. Three methods are compared: the proposed CLT-based confidence set with
m_{\theta}(X)=|X-\theta|
based on an even data split; nonparametric bootstrap; and subsampling with rate of convergence estimated via
Bertail et al. (1999)
. Coverage is estimated over
500
replications. The nominal level is
90\%
.

Results are displayed in
Figure

A.6
, with sample size
N
on the
X
-axis and estimated coverage on the
Y
-axis. Across all values of
\gamma
, the proposed method achieves coverage above
90\%
. A certain level of conservativeness is expected in view of
Theorem

3
. The performance of the resampling methods vary. Subsampling with estimated rate achieves nominal coverage only for
\gamma=0
and large
N
, and fails for
\gamma=1/2
and
\gamma=1
. Nonparametric bootstrap fails for all values of
\gamma
. As its coverage falls below
60\%
, it is not visible in
Figure

A.6
at the reported scale.

Width Analysis

During the same replications, the diameter of each confidence set is recorded and averaged across replications. Results are displayed in
Figure

A.7
, with sample size on a log scale on the
X
-axis and estimated average diameter on a log scale on the
Y
-axis. The slope of each line corresponds to the exponent in the rate of convergence, with theoretical values
-1/2,-1/3
and
-1/4
for
\gamma=0,1/2,1
respectively. The slope for the proposed method is also estimated via linear regression and reported in the figure. Although the proposed confidence sets are wider than those of the resampling methods, this comparison is not meaningful since the resampling methods fail to achieve the nominal coverage. the diameter of the proposed confidence set converges at a rate closely matching the theoretical values in each case, demonstrating that the method adapts to the unknown smoothness parameter
\gamma
without requiring knowledge of it.

Figure A.6
:
Estimated coverage of the proposed confidence set and two sampling methods, targeted at the
90\%
nominal level. The
X
-axis displays the total sample size
N
and the
Y
-axis displays the estimated coverage over
500
replications. From left to right, the panels correspond to
\gamma=0,1/2
, and
1
, resulting in convergence rates
N^{-1/2},N^{-1/3}
and
N^{-1/4}
. The base estimator for the proposed method is the sample median, with an even split of observations. The proposed method achieves coverage above
90\%
across all settings with a certain conservativeness agreeing with the theoretical result in
Theorem

3
. Subsampling with estimated rate achieves nominal coverage only for
\gamma=0
and large
N
. Nonparametric bootstrap fails for all
\gamma
, with coverage below
60\%
and outside of the visible range.

Figure A.7
:
Average diameter of the proposed confidence set on a log–log scale. The
X
-axis displays sample size on a log scale the
Y
-axis displays the average diameter of the confidence sets on a log scale over
500
replications. From left to right, the panels correspond to
\gamma=0,1/2
and
1
, with theoretical rates correspond to
N^{-1/2},N^{-1/3}
and
N^{-1/4}
. The slope for the proposed method estimated by linear regression is reported in the figure. The observed slopes closely match the theoretical rates, demonstrating that the proposed confidence set adapts to the unknown smoothness parameter
\gamma
without requiring prior knowledge of the convergence rate.

S.9.5

Implementation details on baseline methods

This section describes the inferential methods implemented during the numerical studies for comparison. All methods take a parameter
B
, a bootstrap sample. For all simulations, we set
B=200
for computational reasons, which might be considered small for some problems.

1.

Bootstrap
(
Efron, 1979
)
.
Given observations
D=\{Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
and an estimator
\widehat{\theta}=\widehat{\theta}(D)
, draw
B
bootstrap samples
D_{b}=\{Z_{i}^{(b)}\}_{i=1}^{N}
for
1\leq b\leq B
by sampling with replacement from
D
, and recompute
\widehat{\theta}^{(b)}
on each
D_{b}
. For a univariate parameter, bootstrap confidence interval is constructed as the empirical
(\alpha/2,1-\alpha/2)
quantile of
\{\widehat{\theta}^{(b)}-\widehat{\theta}\}_{b=1}^{B}
. For a multivariate parameter, bootstrap confidence set is constructed by inverting the
(1-\alpha)
quantile of
\{\|\widehat{\theta}^{(b)}-\widehat{\theta}\|_{2}\}_{b=1}^{B}
.

2.

Subsampling.
Given observations
D=\{Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
and an estimator
\widehat{\theta}=\widehat{\theta}(D)
, draw
B
subsamples
D_{s}
of size
m=\lceil N^{2/3}\rceil
without replacement for
1\leq s\leq B
, and recompute
\widehat{\theta}^{(s)}
on each
D_{s}
. The subsample size
m
is an additional tuning parameter, but we fix
m=\lceil N^{2/3}\rceil
for computational convenience. The convergence rate
\tau_{N}
of the estimator is unknown in the settings we considered. Thus, we employ
Bertail et al. (1999)
to estimate
\widehat{\tau}_{m}
. For a univariate parameter, confidence interval is constructed as the empirical
(\alpha/2,1-\alpha/2)
quantile of
\{\widehat{\tau}_{m}(\widehat{\theta}^{(s)}-\widehat{\theta})\}_{s=1}^{B}
. For a multivariate parameter, the confidence set is constructed by inverting the
(1-\alpha)
quantile of
\{\widehat{\tau}_{m}\|\widehat{\theta}^{(s)}-\widehat{\theta}\|_{2}\}_{s=1}^{B}
.

3.

Multiplier Bootstrap.
For mean estimation and linear regression, we consider the following procedure. Let
\widehat{\theta}
be an estimator; sample mean for mean estimation and OLS for linear regression. Define the estimated score
\widehat{\psi}_{i}
as

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{i}=\begin{cases}Z_{i}-\widehat{\theta}&\textrm{mean estimation}\\
(Y_{i}-X_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\theta})X_{i}&\textrm{linear regression}\end{cases},\qquad\widehat{\Gamma}=\begin{cases}I_{d}&\textrm{mean estimation}\\
N^{-1}X^{\top}X&\textrm{linear regression.}\end{cases}

For each
1\leq b\leq B
, draw
W_{1}^{(b)},\ldots,W_{N}^{(b)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
independently, and compute

\displaystyle t^{(b)}=\widehat{\Gamma}^{-1}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}W_{i}^{(b)}\widehat{\psi}_{i}.

The bootstrap quantile is taken as the empirical
(1-\alpha)
quantile of
\{N^{-1/2}\|t^{(b)}\|_{2}\}_{b=1}^{B}
.

4.

Residual Bootstrap.
For linear regression, first obtain the OLS estimator
\widehat{\theta}
using the full data and compute the empirical residuals
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{i}=Y_{i}-X_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\theta}
. For each
1\leq b\leq B
, draw a bootstrap sample
\{\widehat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{(b)}\}_{i=1}^{N}
with replacement from
\{\widehat{\varepsilon}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
, and construct bootstrap data
D_{b}=\{(X_{i},X_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\theta}+\widehat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{(b)}\}_{i=1}^{N}
. A new OLS estimator
\widehat{\theta}^{(b)}

is obtained from
D_{b}
, and the bootstrap quantile is taken as the empirical
(1-\alpha)
quantile of
\{\|\widehat{\theta}^{(b)}-\widehat{\theta}\|_{2}\}_{b=1}^{B}
.

5.

Wild Bootstrap
(
Wu, 1986
)
.
As in the residual bootstrap, compute the empirical residuals
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{i}=Y_{i}=X_{i}\widehat{\theta}
from the full-data OLS estimator
\widehat{\theta}
. For each
1\leq b\leq B
, draw
\epsilon^{(b)}_{1},\ldots,\epsilon^{(b)}_{N}
from independent Rademacher and construct bootstrap data by
\{(X_{i},X_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\theta}+\widehat{\varepsilon}_{i}\cdot\epsilon_{i}^{(b)}\}_{i=1}^{N}
. A new OLS estimator
\widehat{\theta}^{(b)}
is obtained from
D_{b}
, and the bootstrap quantile is taken as the empirical
(1-\alpha)
quantile of
\{\|\widehat{\theta}^{(b)}-\widehat{\theta}\|_{2}\}_{b=1}^{B}
.

S.10

Results based on Concentration Inequality

For some M-estimation problems, the loss difference
m_{\theta}-m_{\theta^{\prime}}
is uniformly bounded by definition, making concentration inequalities for bounded random variables directly applicable. For instance, when the observation is bounded uniformly, all applications, then
m_{\theta}-m_{\theta^{\prime}}
is also uniformly bounded for all applications considered in this manuscript. We emphasize that boundedness is required of the loss difference, not of the observations.

Example 7

(Manski’s Maximum Score Estimator)
.

We consider independent observations
(X_{1}^{\top},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{N}^{\top},Y_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\}
with loss function:

m_{\theta}(X,Y)=-Y\mathrm{sgn}(\theta^{\top}X)\quad\text{where}\quad\mathrm{sgn}(t)=2\mathbf{1}\{t\geq 0\}-1,

(E.28)

corresponding to Manski’s maximum score estimator
(
Manski, 1975
)
. The loss difference
m_{\theta}-m_{\theta^{\prime}}
takes values in
\{-2,0,2\}
and is therefore bounded by construction with
B=2
.

As a concrete illustration, we apply the one-sided empirical Bernstein inequality of
Maurer and Pontil (2009, Theorem 11)
, which holds under independent but not necessarily identically distributed observations. Given
\{Z_{i}\,:\,i\in I_{2}\}
with
n_{2}=|I_{2}|
, suppose
\|m_{\theta}-m_{\theta^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}\leq B
. This yields the empirical Bernstein confidence set

\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{EB}}_{N,\alpha}:=\left\{\theta\in\Theta:\,\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}(m_{\theta}-m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}})(Z_{i})\leq\sqrt{\frac{2\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1}}\log(2/\alpha)}{n_{2}}}+\frac{7B\log(2/\alpha)}{3(n_{2}-1)}\right\},

(E.29)

where
\mathtt{EB}
stands for empirical Bernstein. The following is an immediate consequence of Theorem
6
.

Theorem 42
.

Let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N}
be independent observations satisfying
\|m_{\theta}-m_{\theta^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}\leq B
for all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
. Then for any
n_{2}\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P^{N}}\left(\theta(P^{N})\not\in\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{EB}}_{N,\alpha}\right)\leq\alpha.

Proof of
Theorem

42
.

This result is a direct consequence of
Theorem

6
with
\beta(r)=0
and Theorem 11 of
Maurer and Pontil (2009)
.
∎

Theorem 43
.

Assume
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N}
is independent and
B=\|m_{\theta}-m_{\theta^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}
for
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
. Assume
\theta(P^{N})
is the unique solution of (
1
) that satisfies
(A1)
,
(A2)
,
(A4)
and
(A5)
. Define
r_{n_{2}}
and
u_{n_{2}}
as any values that satisfy

\displaystyle r_{n_{2}}^{-2}\phi_{n_{2}}(c_{0}^{-1/(1+\gamma)}r_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)})\leq 1,\quad\textrm{and}\quad u_{n_{2}}^{-2}\omega_{\mathtt{pop}}(c_{0}^{-1/(1+\gamma)}u_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)})\leq n_{2}^{1/2}.

(E.30)

Then, for any
n_{1}\geq 1
,
n_{2}\geq 2
and
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{*}_{P^{N}}\left(\mathrm{Diam}_{\|\cdot\|}\big(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{EB}}_{N,\alpha}\big)\leq C\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{\max\{1,B\}\log(2/\alpha)}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{1/(1+\gamma-q)}\mathrm{R}_{N}^{\mathtt{EB}}\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon-\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\mathtt{init}},

where

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}_{N}^{\mathtt{EB}}=c_{0}^{-1/(1+\gamma)}(r_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)}+u_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)}+\widetilde{s}_{n_{1},n_{2}}^{1/(1+\gamma)}+(B/n_{2})^{1/(1+\gamma)}),

and
C
is a constant depending only on
\gamma,q
, and
\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{init}}
.

We remark that the term
u_{n_{2}}
is only determined through
\omega_{n_{2},\mathtt{pop}}
while
Theorem

13
involves both
\omega_{n_{2},\mathtt{pop}}
and
\omega_{n_{2},\mathtt{emp}}
. When
\|m_{\theta}-m_{\theta^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}
is bounded, it follows that
\omega_{n_{2},\mathtt{emp}}=\phi_{n_{2}}
via the contraction inequality
(
Ledoux and Talagrand, 2013
, Theorem 4.12)
. Consequently, the proof of
Theorem

43
can be seen as a special case of
Theorem

13
. Both
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{EB}}
and
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}
can be used for bounded cases, but only
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}
extends to unbounded cases.

While the diameter bound based on
Theorem

13
is harder to establish due to
\omega_{n_{2},\mathtt{emp}}
, it always results in a smaller confidence set. This is evident in the dependence on
\alpha
, made explicit in
Theorem

13
and
Theorem

43
.

Although the convergence rates are comparable (up to the dependence on
\alpha
), the validity requirements differ: as discussed in
Section

4
and summarized in
Table

2
, the set
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{N,\alpha}^{\mathtt{CLT}}
can fail for some bounded random variables. For instance, it is not valid for Bernoulli random variables with success probability
p
such that
np\to\lambda
.

Finally, we will not provide an analogous result to
Theorem

15
where the relevant assumptions are imposed only on the neighborhood of
\theta_{0}
. This can be established without significant modification to the proof since the boundedness of
\|m_{\theta}-m_{\theta^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}
allows the peeling step in the proof to be over finite partitions, meaning that a remainder term is summable under weaker assumptions. While we do not write out the corresponding result, the almost identical proof as follows goes through.

Proof of
Theorem

43
.

The proof is similar to that of
Theorem

13
. First, observe that

\displaystyle\widehat{t}_{\alpha}(\theta,\widehat{\theta}_{1})

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{2\log(2/\alpha)}n_{2}^{-1/2}\sqrt{\left|\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\{(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})(Z_{i})\}^{2}-\E_{P_{i}}[\{(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})(Z)\}^{2}]\right|}

\displaystyle\quad+2\sqrt{2\log(2/\alpha)}n_{2}^{-1/2}\sqrt{\left|\E_{P_{i}}[\{(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})(Z)\}^{2}]\right|}

\displaystyle\quad+2\sqrt{2\log(2/\alpha)}n_{2}^{-1/2}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\{(m_{\theta(P^{N})}-m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}})(Z_{i})\}^{2}}+\frac{7/3B\log(2/\alpha)}{n_{2}-1}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}_{1}+\mathfrak{R}_{2}+\mathfrak{R}_{3}.

The proof follows analogously up to the definition of
\mathbf{I}
–
\mathbf{V}
. No modification is necessary for
\mathbf{I}
and
\mathbf{II}
, and we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{II}\lesssim\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{init}}2^{-M(1+\gamma)}+C_{q,\gamma}2^{-M(1+\gamma-q)}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\mathtt{init}}.

For
\mathbf{III}
, we note that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\{(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})(Z_{i})\}^{2}-\E_{P_{i}}[\{(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})(Z)\}^{2}]\right|

\displaystyle\quad=4B^{2}\left|\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\left\{\frac{(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})(Z_{i})}{2B}\right\}^{2}-\E_{P_{i}}\left[\left\{\frac{(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})(Z)}{2B}\right\}^{2}\right]\right|

where
(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})(Z)/2B\in[-1,1]
be the boundedness and
t\mapsto t^{2}
is
2
-Lipschitz on
t\in[-1,1]
.
Now let
\epsilon
be independent Rademacher random variables. By symmetrization (see for instance, Lemma 2.3.1 of
van der Vaart and Wellner (1996)
), the contraction inequality (for instance, Theorem 4.12 of
Ledoux and Talagrand (2013)
or Corollary 3.2.2 of
Giné and Nickl (2021)
), and desymmetrization (Lemma 2.3.6 of
van der Vaart and Wellner (1996)
for mean-zero processes), we arrive for
S\subseteq\Theta

\displaystyle\E_{P^{2}}\left[\sup_{\theta\in S}\left|\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\left\{\frac{(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})(Z_{i})}{2B}\right\}^{2}-\E_{P_{i}}\left[\left\{\frac{(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})(Z)}{2B}\right\}^{2}\right]\right|\right]

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2\E_{P^{2}\times\epsilon}\left[\sup_{\theta\in S}\left|\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\epsilon_{i}\left\{\frac{(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})(Z_{i})}{2B}\right\}^{2}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{16}{B}\E_{P^{2}\times\epsilon}\left[\sup_{\theta\in S}\left|\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\epsilon_{i}(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})(Z_{i})\right|\right]

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{32}{B}\E_{P^{2}}\left[\sup_{\theta\in S}\left|(m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})})(Z_{i})-\E_{P_{i}}[m_{\theta}-m_{\theta(P^{N})}]\right|\right].

Hence, we have
\omega_{n_{2},\mathtt{emp}}^{2}(\delta)\leq 128B\cdot\phi_{n_{2}}(\delta)
. Take

\displaystyle\bar{u}_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)}=u^{2/(1+\gamma)}_{n_{2}}+\left(\frac{B}{n_{2}}\right)^{1/{(1+\gamma)}}.

By AM-GM inequality, we have

\displaystyle u^{2/(1+\gamma)}_{n_{2}}+\left(\frac{B}{n_{2}}\right)^{1/{(1+\gamma)}}\geq 2\sqrt{u^{2/(1+\gamma)}_{n_{2}}\left(\frac{B}{n_{2}}\right)^{1/{(1+\gamma)}}}

\displaystyle\quad\implies u^{4}_{n_{2}}\geq 2^{2(1+\gamma)}u^{2}_{n_{2}}\left(\frac{B}{n_{2}}\right).

Using these results, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{III}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}^{*}_{P^{2}|\widetilde{P}^{1}}\left(c_{0}\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|^{1+\gamma}\leq 5\mathfrak{R}_{1}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\bigg.\mbox{ for }\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|\geq 2^{M}c_{0}^{-1/(1+\gamma)}\bar{u}_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}^{*}_{P^{2}|\widetilde{P}^{1}}\left(c_{0}^{2}\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|^{2+2\gamma}\leq 25\mathfrak{R}_{1}^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\bigg.\mbox{ for }\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|\geq 2^{M}c_{0}^{-1/(1+\gamma)}\bar{u}_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq 25\cdot 4(2\log(2/\alpha))n_{2}^{-1}\sum_{j=M}^{\infty}2^{-2j(1+\gamma)}\bar{u}_{n_{2}}^{-4}\omega^{2}_{n_{2}}(2^{j+1}c_{0}^{-1/(1+\gamma)}\bar{u}_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)})

\displaystyle\leq 25\cdot 4(2\log(2/\alpha))n_{2}^{-1}\sum_{j=M}^{\infty}2^{-2j(1+\gamma)}2^{2q(j+1)}\bar{u}_{n_{2}}^{-4}128B\cdot\phi_{n_{2}}(c_{0}^{-1/(1+\gamma)}u_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)})

\displaystyle\leq 25\cdot 8(2B\log(2/\alpha))\sum_{j=M}^{\infty}2^{-2j(1+\gamma)}2^{2q(j+1)}\leq 200(2\log(2/\alpha))C_{q,\gamma}^{2}2^{-2M(1+\gamma-q)}.

Meanwhile a similar derivation for
\mathbf{IV}
gives

\displaystyle\mathbf{IV}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}^{*}_{P^{2}|\widetilde{P}^{1}}\left(c_{0}\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|^{1+\gamma}\leq 5\mathfrak{R}_{2}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\bigg.\mbox{ for }\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|\geq 2^{M}c_{0}^{-1/(1+\gamma)}u_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)}\bigg)\lesssim\log(2/\alpha)\cdot C_{q,\gamma}^{2}2^{-2M(1+\gamma-q)}.

Finally, for
\mathbf{V}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}^{*}_{P^{2}|\widetilde{P}^{1}}\left(c_{0}\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|^{1+\gamma}\leq 5\mathfrak{R}_{3}\cap B^{c}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}^{*}_{P^{2}|\widetilde{P}^{1}}\left(c_{0}\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|^{1+\gamma}\leq\frac{20\sqrt{2\log(2/\alpha)}}{n_{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\{(m_{\theta(P^{N})}-m_{\widehat{\theta}_{1}})(Z_{i})\}^{2}}\cap B^{c}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{P}^{*}_{P^{2}|\widetilde{P}^{1}}\left(c_{0}\|\theta-\theta(P^{N})\|^{1+\gamma}\leq\frac{7/3B\log(2/\alpha)}{n_{2}-1}\cap B^{c}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{init}}\log(2/\alpha)2^{-2M(1+\gamma)}+(B\log(2/\alpha))2^{-M(1+\gamma)}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\mathtt{init}}.

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{*}_{P^{N}}\left(\mathrm{Diam}_{\|\cdot\|}\big(\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}^{\mathtt{EB}}_{N,\alpha}\big)>2^{M}c_{0}^{-1/(1+\gamma)}\left\{r_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)}+u_{n_{2}}^{2/(1+\gamma)}+{s^{\prime}}_{n_{1},n_{2}}^{1/(1+\gamma)}+\left(\frac{B}{n_{2}}\right)^{1/(1+\gamma)}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{init}}2^{-M(1+\gamma)}+C_{q,\gamma}2^{-M(1+\gamma-q)}+BC_{q,\gamma}^{2}\log(2/\alpha)2^{-2M(1+\gamma-q)}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{init}}\log(2/\alpha)2^{-2M(1+\gamma)}+(B\log(2/\alpha))2^{-M(1+\gamma)}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\mathtt{init}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\mathfrak{C}(1+\max\{1,B\}\log(2/\alpha)2^{-M(1+\gamma-q)})2^{-M(1+\gamma-q)}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\mathtt{init}},

where
\mathfrak{C}
only depends on
\gamma,q,\widetilde{C}_{\mathtt{init}}
. We thus conclude the claim by choosing
M
to be

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\frac{\log((1+\sqrt{\max\{1,B\}\log(2/\alpha)})\mathfrak{C}/\varepsilon)}{(1+\gamma-q)\cdot\log 2}\quad\textrm{and}\quad

\displaystyle 2^{M}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\mathfrak{C}(1+\sqrt{\max\{1,B\}\log(2/\alpha)})}{\varepsilon}\right)^{1/(1+\gamma-q)}.

∎

We provide two remarks regarding statistical applications.

Remark 11

(Manski’s Discrete Choice Model)
.

For the Manski’s problem introduced in
Section

8.3
, the loss function is uniformly bounded with
B=2
. Hence, the result in this section applies.
Theorem

23
requires
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
to converge not too rapidly. The validity for the set developed in this section can be established without any assumptions.

Remark 12

(Discrete Argmin Inference)
.

For the discrete inference problem introduced in
Section

8.5
, the result in this section applies if it can be assumed that
e_{j}^{\top}X_{i}
is almost surely uniformly bounded for all
1\leq j\leq d
and
1\leq i\leq n
for some constant. The validity follows without the control on the remainder term (
56
).
